### Scope: Philosophy (values + principles)

Scope is an orchestration organism for building with agents. Its job is not to “write code.” Its job is to **turn intent into verified change**, continuously, under explicit constraints.

#### 1) Verification is sovereign
Nothing counts until it survives a verifier.
- Prefer executable verifiers (tests, typecheck, fuzz, property tests).
- Use natural-language verifiers only where execution can’t reach (spec/UX/logic gaps).
- Verification is an independent actor: decoupled context, adversarial stance.

#### 2) Loops > plans
Plans are hypotheses. Loops are reality contact.
- Every unit of work is a **doer↔verifier loop** with a stop condition.
- Long work is composition: a DAG of loops, not a monolithic sprint.

#### 3) Boundedness is kindness (and reliability)
Unbounded agents thrash. Thrash looks like progress until it isn’t.
- Hard budgets: time, tokens, retries, parallelism.
- Backoff, quarantine, escalation.
- “Admit failure” is a first-class outcome.

#### 4) Separation of concerns, everywhere
Scope is modular by design:
- UI is a skin (tmux/tauri/web) over a stable event/command API.
- Agents are adapters (Claude Code/Codex/etc), not the architecture.
- File/runtime isolation is a substrate (worktrees/sandboxes), not a side quest.

#### 5) State is the product
The user doesn’t want chats; they want **artifacts**.
- Every loop produces: patches, diffs, logs, proofs, receipts.
- Everything is replayable: inputs, decisions, verifiers, outputs.

#### 6) Prefer invariants to instructions
Natural language is leaky. Invariants are sharp.
- Translate “make it better” into measurable constraints.
- When porting/rewriting: equivalence harnesses + property tests.

#### 7) Default to safe autonomy
Autonomy is earned by demonstrated verifier performance.
- Start conservative (small loops, frequent gates).
- Expand autonomy only when verifiers show stability.

#### 8) Humans own the gradient
Agents do execution and exploration.
Humans do:
- goal selection,
- architectural decisions,
- risk acceptance,
- quality bar.
Scope should maximize “human time spent on high-leverage judgment.”

#### 9) Transparency over magic
If Scope can’t explain what it did, it didn’t do it.
- Show why a step was taken, what it changed, and how it was verified.
- No hidden prompts as the source of truth; the DAG + receipts are.

#### 10) Culture: anti-vampire
Scope is designed to prevent pace inflation.
- Replace dopamine loops with verifier-gated loops.
- Encourage sustainable throughput: steady, audited, compounding.

### Verifier Pyramid (per loop)
1) **Static checks**
- lint, typecheck, dead code, complexity, code smells

2) **Deterministic tests**
- unit + integration + regression suite

3) **Generative tests**
- property-based (invariants)
- fuzz (parser/IO/state transitions)
- metamorphic tests (input transform → predictable output relation)

4) **Behavioral/runtime checks**
- perf budgets (latency/memory)
- flaky detector / stability runs
- security checks (taint/sast/dependency)

5) **Semantic/product checks**
- contract/rubric assertions (API behavior, UX constraints, business rules)

Then loop scoring:
- each layer gives pass/fail + confidence + cost
- merge gate = weighted threshold + mandatory hard gates (e.g. typecheck + critical security + non-flaky core tests)

Humans can tweak the constraints on the fly (to be applied to the next iteration)

Show more

Automated layers (1-4): fast, deterministic, property-based
Semantic layer (5): rubric/contract assertions (may involve LLM judgment)
Scoring system: pass/fail + confidence + cost per layer
Merge gate: weighted threshold + mandatory hard gates
Human tuning: adjust thresholds/gates mid-loop

Git worktree, will it be too heavy?

Feb 14
Git worktrees are lightweight. They share .git objects but give separate working directories. Disk overhead is minimal (hardlinks + index), and checkout is fast.

Pros:

Real filesystem isolation (no virtualization overhead)
Native git operations (diff, merge, bisect)
Easy cleanup (delete directory)
Parallelizable (each loop in own worktree)
Cons:

Shared .git/index contention if parallel writes (solvable: separate indexes)
Doesn't isolate runtime (network, filesystem side effects)
Follow-up:

Do you need runtime isolation too (e.g., preventing a doer from accidentally hitting prod APIs, writing to /tmp, network calls), or is filesystem isolation enough?

Let's start with filesystem isolation. The runtime isolation can be provided within each coding agent (claude code, codex)

Feb 14
Smart. Delegate runtime isolation to the agent layer (Claude Code's containers, etc.). Scope just orchestrates worktrees.

Core programming interfaces:
Based on the architecture, I see these key interfaces:

1. Task → Loop decomposition

Input: task description, repo context
Output: DAG of atomic loops (nodes = loops, edges = dependencies)
2. Loop executor

Input: atomic task, rubric, context

3. Doer adapter

Input: task, context, history
Output: artifact (code/patch)
4. Verifier

Input: artifact, intent, rubric

Input: loop ID
Output: isolated workspace, merge/cleanup operations
6. Event bus

Pub/sub for state changes, connects orchestrator → UIs
7. Rubric generator

Input: task, repo analysis
Output: verification pyramid config
8. Prompt tuner

Surface architecture for Tauri:

Doer output (code diff, live)

Event stream (all loops, chronological)

DAG rendering (reactflow / cytoscape / custom canvas)
Real-time event stream (WebSocket → React state)

Protocol:
Command API (frontend → backend): JSON-RPC or similar
Event stream (backend → frontend): JSON over WebSocket
Terminal passthrough: PTY bridge

Event schema design:
Events flow: Rust orchestrator → WebSocket → UI state

Event types:
typescript
type ScopeEvent =
  // Ticket lifecycle
  | { type: "ticket.created", ticket_id, title, description, created_at }
  | { type: "ticket.status_changed", ticket_id, status: "open"|"in_progress"|"blocked"|"done" }

  // DAG composition
  | { type: "dag.planned", ticket_id, nodes: Loop[], edges: Dependency[] }
  | { type: "dag.updated", ticket_id, changes } // if replanning happens

  // Loop lifecycle
  | { type: "loop.started", loop_id, ticket_id, task, rubric, context_summary }
  | { type: "loop.attempt", loop_id, attempt_num, doer_output_preview }
  | { type: "loop.verifying", loop_id, attempt_num }
  | { type: "loop.verified", loop_id, attempt_num, scores: PyramidScores, pass: bool, feedback }
  | { type: "loop.passed", loop_id, commit_sha, elapsed_ms, total_attempts }
  | { type: "loop.failed", loop_id, reason: "max_turns"|"budget", final_scores }
  | { type: "loop.escalated", loop_id, reason, blocking_downstream: loop_id[] }
  | { type: "loop.resolved", loop_id, resolution: "human_fix"|"skip"|"redefine" }

  // Manual interactions
  | { type: "loop.rubric_updated", loop_id, rubric, updated_by: user_id }
  | { type: "loop.terminal_attached", loop_id, user_id }
  | { type: "loop.terminal_detached", loop_id, user_id }

  // Resource tracking
  | { type: "loop.tokens_used", loop_id, attempt_num, tokens, cost_usd }
  | { type: "loop.worktree_created", loop_id, path }
  | { type: "loop.worktree_cleaned", loop_id }

  // Multiplayer presence
  | { type: "user.joined", user_id, ticket_id?, loop_id? }
  | { type: "user.left", user_id }
Core data structures:
typescript
type Loop = {
  id: string,
  ticket_id: string,
  task: string,
  status: "pending"|"running"|"passed"|"failed"|"escalated",
  rubric: VerifierPyramid,
  attempts: Attempt[],
  dependencies: string[], // loop_ids that must complete first
  worktree_path?: string,
  commit_sha?: string,
}

type VerifierPyramid = {
  static_checks: Layer,
  deterministic_tests: Layer,
  generative_tests: Layer,
  runtime_checks: Layer,
  semantic_checks: Layer,
  merge_gate: MergeGate,
}

type Layer = {
  enabled: bool,
  checks: Check[], // e.g. ["eslint", "tsc", "jest"]
  weight: float, // for scoring
}

type MergeGate = {
  threshold: float, // weighted score must exceed
  mandatory: string[], // layer names that MUST pass
}

type Attempt = {
  num: number,
  started_at: timestamp,
  doer_output: Artifact,
  verifier_scores: PyramidScores,
  verifier_feedback: string,
  passed: bool,
  tokens_used: number,
}

type PyramidScores = {
  static_checks: LayerScore,
  deterministic_tests: LayerScore,
  // ... etc
  overall: float, // weighted
  pass: bool,
}

type LayerScore = {
  pass: bool,
  confidence: float,
  cost_ms: number,
  details: CheckResult[],
}
Does this event schema cover your mental model?

Is it overengineered if models keep improving?

Feb 14
Sharp question. Let's stress-test the design:

If models get dramatically better:
What breaks?

Nothing. Better models just mean fewer doer→verifier iterations.
The loop primitive still holds: even perfect models need verification to prove correctness.
Verifier pyramid becomes more important as models tackle harder tasks (complex state, distributed systems, security).
What becomes unnecessary?

Maybe fewer retries (max_turns could drop from 5 → 2)
Maybe simpler rubrics (less hand-holding)
But you still need the rubric infrastructure — better models don't eliminate the need for executable verification.
What becomes more valuable?

Verification as the bottleneck shifts. When models are cheap/fast, verification quality becomes the constraint.
Composability (DAGs). Better models unlock bigger tasks → more need for decomposition + orchestration.
Audit trails (.scope/ logs). As agents do more autonomously, humans need more transparency, not less.
The real risk:
Overengineering isn't the pyramid or loops — those are durable primitives.

The risk is:

Too many knobs. (Do you really need per-layer weights + confidence scores + cost tracking in v1?)
Premature multiplayer. (Do you need CRDT rubric editing before you have 10 users?)
UI polish before core works. (Figma-like DAG canvas is sexy, but can you prototype with ASCII art first?)
Brutally honest MVP:
Could you ship with:

Single ticket, single loop (no DAG yet)
CLI only (no Tauri)
Hardcoded rubric (no editor)
Just prove: doer↔verifier loop + escalation + git worktree + .scope/ logs
Then add:

DAG (when you have a task that needs it)
Tauri UI (when CLI is too limiting)
Rubric editing (when defaults fail often)
Multiplayer (when you have collaborators)
Counter-question:

What's the earliest moment where Scope is more useful than just using Claude Code directly?

If you can define that razor's edge, you know your true MVP.

I'm building this with coding agents, you can be more ambitious. I just need to nail the architecture. I can swap the crdt with a lock etc. but it'll be tougher to go from a single player to multiplayer system

Feb 14
You're right. Architecture choices are load-bearing; implementation details aren't.

If you design for single-player and bolt on multiplayer later, you get:

Brittle state synchronization
Race conditions everywhere
UI that wasn't built for concurrent edits
Event model that assumes one actor
Better: Design multiplayer-first, even if v1 has one user. The primitives are the same:

Architectural decisions that matter:
1. Event-sourced state
All state changes = events (append-only log)
UI state is a projection of events
Makes replay, multiplayer, audit trails trivial
Single-player is just "one event producer"
2. Command/query separation
Commands: mutate (async, return ack/error)
Queries: read-only (can be cached, replicated)
No hidden state changes in queries
3. Optimistic UI + conflict resolution
UI applies commands immediately (optimistic)
Backend either confirms or rejects
For conflicts (two users edit same rubric): last-write-wins is fine for v1, swap for CRDT later
4. Presence as first-class
Even single-player: treat "user X is viewing loop Y" as an event
Makes adding avatars/cursors later a UI change, not an architecture change
5. Worktree-per-loop is already concurrent
Each loop has isolated git worktree
Parallelism is baked in (doesn't matter if it's one agent or ten users kicking off loops)
What you DON'T need to decide now:
CRDT vs lock (both work with event-sourced design)
WebSocket vs polling (transport layer is swappable)
Tauri vs web (both consume same event stream)
Rust vs Go vs TypeScript backend (as long as it emits events)
The real architectural question:
Where does state live?

Option A: Centralized server

Single source of truth
All clients connect to it
Easy consistency, harder to run locally
Option B: Local-first (e.g., SQLite + sync)

Each user has local state
Sync layer reconciles
Works offline, harder consistency
Option C: Hybrid

Local orchestrator (per machine)
Optional central server for multiplayer/dashboards
Which model fits your vision?

Is Scope:

A local tool (runs on your machine, talks to your git repos)?
A team service (hosted, everyone connects to it)?
Both (works solo, can sync if you want)?
The actual loops run on the client side only. The  views need to be synced, can we do it p2p?

Feb 14
P2P for view sync is viable. Here's the architecture:

Hybrid model: Local execution + P2P sync
Loop execution:

Runs entirely on client (Tauri backend talks to local git, spawns agents, manages worktrees)
Each user's machine is authoritative for loops they started
No central server needed for core functionality
View sync (P2P):

Clients broadcast events to peers (WebRTC data channels or libp2p)
Each client maintains own event log + projections
CRDTs for collaborative state (rubric edits, ticket metadata)
P2P Stack options:
Option 1: WebRTC (simpler, web-friendly)
Tauri can use webrtc-rs or embed a browser for signaling
Signaling server (tiny, just for peer discovery): use public STUN/TURN or self-host
Data channels for events
Pros: Works in browser too (if you add web UI later)
Cons: NAT traversal can be flaky
Option 2: libp2p (more robust)
Rust-native, battle-tested (IPFS, Polkadot use it)
Built-in peer discovery (mDNS for LAN, DHT for internet)
Gossipsub for event broadcasting
Pros: No signaling server, robust NAT hole-punching
Cons: More complex, adds binary size
Option 3: Hybrid (local-first, optional P2P)
Default: single-player, no network
Opt-in: "share this project" → generates P2P room code
Peers join room, sync events
Falls back gracefully if P2P fails
Event sync design:
Each client maintains:

Local event log (append-only, SQLite or similar)
Causal ordering (vector clocks or hybrid logical clocks)
CRDT state (for ticket metadata, rubric edits)
When a loop completes on Alice's machine:

Alice: loop.passed event → local log → broadcast to peers
Bob: receives event → appends to local log → updates UI
Conflict resolution:

Loop execution: no conflicts (each client owns its loops)
Rubric edits: CRDT (Automerge or yjs-rs)
Ticket status: last-write-wins with HLC timestamp
What gets synced vs. what stays local:
Synced (P2P events):

Ticket created/updated
DAG planned
Loop started/passed/failed/escalated
Rubric edits
User presence (who's viewing what)
Local only:

Doer/verifier transcripts (too large, query on-demand)
Worktree paths (machine-specific)
Terminal PTY state (ephemeral)
On-demand fetch:

"Show me loop X's transcript" → request from owner peer
Uses request/response pattern over P2P channel
Bootstrapping:
How do peers find each other?

Option A: Room codes (Figma-style)

User clicks "Share project"
Generates short code (e.g., scope-abc-123)
Peers enter code → signaling server connects them
Simple UX, requires tiny signaling service
Option B: Git-native discovery

Scope embeds peer info in .scope/peers.json (committed to repo)
Cloning repo = auto-discover collaborators
No external service, but requires git coordination
Option C: LAN-only (mDNS)

Auto-discover on local network
Zero config, but only works for colocated teams
My recommendation:
Start with Option A (room codes + WebRTC)

Why:

Simplest to implement (webrtc-rs + tiny signaling server)
Familiar UX (users understand "join code")
Can layer in libp2p later if you need better reliability
Signaling server can be a public Cloudflare Worker (free tier)
v1 flow:

Alice: "Share this ticket" → generates code "scope-x7k2"
Bob: "Join session" → enters code → peers connect
Both: see live DAG updates, can edit rubrics, presence indicators
Does P2P fit your mental model, or do you want a simpler sync mechanism first?

How does Zed editor do it?

Feb 14
Zed uses a hybrid model: client-server for collab, not pure P2P.

Zed's architecture (as of their open-source release):
Collab server (central)
Rust backend (collyb crate)
Handles:
Authentication (GitHub OAuth)
Room/channel management (who's in what workspace)
Presence (cursors, selections, view state)
Message routing (broadcasts edits between peers)
Conflict-free state (CRDTs for buffer edits, using Automerge-like primitives)
Client (Zed app)
Connects to collab server via WebSocket
Sends:
Buffer edits (as CRDT operations)
Cursor positions
File system changes
Receives:
Peers' edits (merged via CRDT)
Presence updates
Why not P2P?
NAT traversal hell: WebRTC works ~80% of time; corporate networks break it
Discoverability: Need a rendezvous point anyway (signaling server)
Presence + rooms: Easier to manage "who's online" with central state
Authentication: Can't do OAuth handshake P2P
Key insight:
Server is dumb relay + room manager
Clients do all the work (CRDT merging, rendering, file watching)
Server doesn't understand code, just routes messages
How Zed's model maps to Scope:
What Zed syncs:

Text buffer edits (character-level CRDTs)
Cursor positions (ephemeral presence)
File tree changes (CRDT-based)
What Scope would sync:

Ticket metadata (title, status)
Rubric edits (CRDT or last-write-wins)
Loop events (who started what, results)
Presence (who's viewing which loop)
Differences:

Zed: many small edits/sec (typing), needs low-latency CRDT
Scope: fewer, larger events (loop.passed, rubric.updated), can tolerate ~100ms latency
Zed-inspired architecture for Scope:
Collab server (Rust, tiny)
rust
// Handles WebSocket connections
// Routes events between clients in same "project room"
// Stores ephemeral presence, no persistent state

struct Room {
    project_id: String,
    clients: HashMap<UserId, WebSocket>,
    presence: HashMap<UserId, PresenceState>,
}

// On event from client:
fn broadcast_event(room: &Room, event: ScopeEvent, sender: UserId) {
    for (user_id, ws) in &room.clients {
        if user_id != sender {
            ws.send(event.clone());
        }
    }
}
Client (Tauri app)
rust
// Connects to collab server when user "shares" a ticket
// Sends local events (loop.started, rubric.updated)
// Receives peers' events, merges into local state

struct CollabClient {
    ws: WebSocket,
    local_event_log: EventLog, // SQLite
    crdt_state: TicketCRDT,    // For rubric/metadata edits
}

// Local loop completes:
fn on_loop_passed(loop_id: String) {
    let event = Event::LoopPassed { loop_id, commit_sha, ... };
    local_event_log.append(event);
    ws.send(event); // Broadcast to peers
}

// Receive peer's event:
fn on_remote_event(event: Event) {
    local_event_log.append(event);
    ui.update(event); // Re-render DAG
}
Zed's tech stack (relevant parts):
CRDT: Custom implementation (similar to Automerge, optimized for text)
Transport: WebSocket (wss://)
Server: Axum (Rust web framework)
Auth: GitHub OAuth → JWT tokens
Deployment: Fly.io (their hosted collab server)
Recommended path for Scope:
Phase 1: Single-player (no sync)
Local Tauri app
SQLite event log
Proves core loop execution
Phase 2: Zed-style collab
Tiny Rust collab server (Axum + WebSocket)
Client connects when user clicks "Share ticket"
Server just routes events (stateless relay)
Use automerge-rs for CRDT (rubric edits)
Deploy server to Fly.io free tier
Phase 3: Optional self-hosting
User can run their own collab server (Docker image)
Client can connect to custom server URL
Why Zed's approach > pure P2P for Scope:
✅ Simpler: No NAT traversal, no peer discovery
✅ Reliable: Works on corporate networks
✅ Scalable: Server can handle 100s of concurrent rooms
✅ Auth-ready: Can add GitHub login later
✅ Familiar UX: "Join project link" instead of "enter room code"

❌ Requires server: But it's tiny, cheap, and can be self-hosted

Concrete implementation:
Collab server (50 lines of Rust):

rust
use axum::{Router, extract::ws::{WebSocket, WebSocketUpgrade}};
use std::collections::HashMap;
use tokio::sync::broadcast;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() {
    let app = Router::new()
        .route("/room/:project_id", get(ws_handler));

    axum::Server::bind(&"0.0.0.0:3000".parse().unwrap())
        .serve(app.into_make_service())
        .await
        .unwrap();
}

async fn ws_handler(
    ws: WebSocketUpgrade,
    Path(project_id): Path<String>,
) -> impl IntoResponse {
    ws.on_upgrade(|socket| handle_socket(socket, project_id))
}

async fn handle_socket(socket: WebSocket, project_id: String) {
    let (tx, rx) = broadcast::channel(100);

    // Join room (get existing broadcast channel for this project)
    let room_tx = ROOMS.entry(project_id).or_insert(tx).clone();

    // Relay messages between WebSocket and room broadcast
    tokio::spawn(async move {
        // Receive from WS → broadcast to room
        // Receive from room → send to WS
    });
}

# Cost: Free tier (3 VMs, enough for 100s of users)

Updated architecture:
Client (Tauri app)
Runs loop execution locally (git worktrees, agent orchestration)
Connects to collab server via WebSocket when user shares a ticket
Sends events (loop state changes, rubric edits, presence)
Receives peers' events, updates UI
Collab server (Rust/Axum)
Manages rooms (one per project/ticket)
Routes events between clients in same room
Ephemeral presence tracking
No persistent storage (clients own their event logs)
Optional: Redis for scaling beyond single instance
Programming interfaces (updated):
Let's define the contracts now that we know the topology.

1. Coding Agent Adapter
Purpose: Abstract over Claude Code, Codex, Aider, etc.

rust
#[async_trait]
trait AgentAdapter {
    /// Execute a task with context, return artifact
    async fn execute(
        &self,
        task: Task,
        context: Context,
        history: Vec<Attempt>,
    ) -> Result<Artifact>;

    /// Stream progress updates (optional)
    fn subscribe_progress(&self) -> Receiver<ProgressEvent>;

    /// Cancel in-flight execution
    async fn cancel(&self) -> Result<()>;

    /// Query agent capabilities
    fn capabilities(&self) -> AgentCapabilities;
}

struct Task {
    description: String,
    acceptance_criteria: Vec<String>,
    constraints: Vec<String>, // e.g., "use TypeScript", "max 100 LOC"
}

struct Context {
    worktree_path: PathBuf,
    repo_context: String,      // README, key files, etc.
    dependencies: Vec<String>, // npm/cargo/pip dependencies
}

struct Artifact {
    changes: GitDiff,          // What changed
    summary: String,           // Agent's explanation
    reasoning: Option<String>, // Why it made these choices (optional)
}

struct AgentCapabilities {
    supported_languages: Vec<String>,
    has_terminal_access: bool,
    has_web_search: bool,
    max_context_tokens: usize,
}

2. Verifier
Purpose: Run verification pyramid, return scores + feedback.

rust
#[async_trait]
trait Verifier {
    /// Run all pyramid layers, return results
    async fn verify(
        &self,
        artifact: &Artifact,
        intent: &Task,
        rubric: &VerifierPyramid,
    ) -> VerificationResult;
}

struct VerificationResult {
    scores: PyramidScores,
    passed: bool,
    feedback: String,        // For doer to improve
    execution_time_ms: u64,
}

struct PyramidScores {
    layers: HashMap<LayerName, LayerScore>,
    overall_score: f64,      // Weighted
    meets_merge_gate: bool,
}

struct LayerScore {
    passed: bool,
    confidence: f64,         // 0.0-1.0
    cost_ms: u64,
    details: Vec<CheckResult>,
}

struct CheckResult {
    check_name: String,      // e.g., "eslint", "jest", "typescript"
    status: CheckStatus,
    message: Option<String>,
    output: Option<String>,  // Full output for debugging
}

enum CheckStatus {
    Pass,
    Fail,
    Skip,                    // Layer disabled or not applicable
    Error,                   // Check crashed
}
Implementation:

rust
struct DefaultVerifier {
    executor: CommandExecutor, // Runs linters, tests, etc.
}
3. Loop Executor
Purpose: Orchestrate doer↔verifier loop with retries.

rust
struct LoopExecutor {
    agent: Box<dyn AgentAdapter>,
    verifier: Box<dyn Verifier>,
    event_bus: EventBus,
}

impl LoopExecutor {
    async fn execute(
        &self,
        loop_def: LoopDefinition,
    ) -> LoopResult {
        let mut attempts = vec![];

        for attempt_num in 1..=loop_def.max_turns {
            // Emit event
            self.event_bus.emit(Event::LoopAttempt {
                loop_id: loop_def.id,
                attempt_num
            });

            // Doer produces artifact
            let artifact = self.agent.execute(
                loop_def.task.clone(),
                loop_def.context.clone(),
                attempts.clone(), // History for learning
            ).await?;

            // Verifier checks
            self.event_bus.emit(Event::LoopVerifying {
                loop_id: loop_def.id,
                attempt_num
            });

            let result = self.verifier.verify(
                &artifact,
                &loop_def.task,
                &loop_def.rubric,
            ).await?;

            attempts.push(Attempt {
                num: attempt_num,
                artifact,
                verification: result.clone(),
            });

            // Check pass condition
            if result.passed {
                return LoopResult::Success {
                    attempts,
                    final_artifact: attempts.last().unwrap().artifact.clone()
                };
            }

            // Emit verification result
            self.event_bus.emit(Event::LoopVerified {
                loop_id: loop_def.id,
                attempt_num,
                scores: result.scores,
                passed: false,
                feedback: result.feedback.clone(),
            });
        }

        // Max turns exceeded
        LoopResult::Failure {
            attempts,
            reason: FailureReason::MaxTurns
        }
    }
}

struct LoopDefinition {
    id: LoopId,
    task: Task,
    rubric: VerifierPyramid,
    context: Context,
    max_turns: usize,
}

enum LoopResult {
    Success { attempts: Vec<Attempt>, final_artifact: Artifact },
    Failure { attempts: Vec<Attempt>, reason: FailureReason },
}
4. Event Bus
Purpose: Decouple orchestrator from UIs, enable multiplayer sync.

rust
struct EventBus {
    local_log: EventLog,           // SQLite
    remote_tx: Option<WebSocketSender>, // To collab server
}

impl EventBus {
    /// Emit event locally and to remote peers
    fn emit(&self, event: Event) {
        // Append to local log
        self.local_log.append(&event);

        // Broadcast to collab server if connected
        if let Some(tx) = &self.remote_tx {
            tx.send(event.clone());
        }
    }

    /// Subscribe to events (for UI updates)
    fn subscribe(&self) -> Receiver<Event> {
        // Returns channel that receives all events
    }

    /// Connect to collab server
    async fn connect(&mut self, server_url: &str, project_id: &str) {
        // Establish WebSocket, set remote_tx
    }
}

struct EventLog {
    db: SqliteConnection,
}

impl EventLog {
    fn append(&self, event: &Event) {
        // INSERT INTO events (timestamp, type, payload) ...
    }

    fn query(&self, filter: EventFilter) -> Vec<Event> {
        // SELECT * FROM events WHERE ...
    }
}
5. Collab Server Protocol
Purpose: Define wire format for client↔server communication.

rust
// Messages from client → server
enum ClientMessage {
    JoinRoom { project_id: String, user_id: String },
    LeaveRoom,
    Event(Event),              // Broadcast this event to room
    PresenceUpdate(Presence),
}

// Messages from server → client
enum ServerMessage {
    RoomJoined { users: Vec<UserId> },
    UserJoined { user_id: UserId },
    UserLeft { user_id: UserId },
    Event(Event),              // From another peer
    PresenceUpdate { user_id: UserId, presence: Presence },
}

struct Presence {
    viewing_ticket: Option<TicketId>,
    viewing_loop: Option<LoopId>,
    cursor_position: Option<CursorPosition>, // For rubric editor
}
```

**WebSocket flow:**
```
Client A                    Server                    Client B
   |                          |                          |
   |--JoinRoom--------------->|                          |
   |<-----RoomJoined----------|                          |
   |                          |<----JoinRoom-------------|
   |<-----UserJoined----------|---RoomJoined------------>|
   |                          |                          |
   |--Event(loop.started)---->|                          |
   |                          |--Event(loop.started)---->|
   |                          |                          |
   |                          |<--Event(rubric.updated)--|
   |<--Event(rubric.updated)--|                          |

7. Worktree Manager
Purpose: Isolate each loop in git worktree.

rust
struct WorktreeManager {
    repo_path: PathBuf,
}

impl WorktreeManager {
    /// Create worktree for loop
    fn create(&self, loop_id: &LoopId) -> Result<PathBuf> {
        let worktree_path = self.repo_path
            .join(".scope/worktrees")
            .join(loop_id.to_string());

        // git worktree add <path> -b scope-loop-<id>
        Command::new("git")
            .args(&["worktree", "add", worktree_path.to_str().unwrap()])
            .output()?;

        Ok(worktree_path)
    }

    /// Merge worktree changes, squash commit
    fn merge(&self, loop_id: &LoopId) -> Result<CommitSha> {
        // git merge --squash scope-loop-<id>
        // git commit -m "Loop <id>: <task>"
    }

    /// Clean up worktree
    fn cleanup(&self, loop_id: &LoopId) -> Result<()> {
        // git worktree remove <path>
    }
}
Summary of interfaces:
Interface	Purpose	Key methods
AgentAdapter	Talk to coding agents	execute(), cancel(), capabilities()
Verifier	Run verification pyramid	verify()
LoopExecutor	Orchestrate doer↔verifier	execute()
EventBus	Emit/subscribe events	emit(), subscribe(), connect()
EventLog	Persist events locally	append(), query()
WorktreeManager	Git isolation	create(), merge(), cleanup()
CollabServer	Route events between clients	WebSocket handlers
TauriCommands	UI → backend API	create_ticket(), start_loop(), etc.

Based on what I can see from your README, here's what I understand about hooks in your current Scope:

My answer: Do we still need hooks?

No, not in the same way. Here's why:

The new architecture changes the hook model:
Old Scope (current):
Problem: Need to track what Claude Code is doing
Solution: Install hooks (tmux monitoring, file watchers, etc.)
Hook purpose: Observe black box behavior from outside
New Scope (redesigned):
Different problem: Orchestrate doer↔verifier loops
Different agents: Multiple coding agents (Claude Code, Codex, Aider), not just one
Agent adapter abstraction: Each agent reports its own state through the interface
What replaces hooks:
1. Agent Adapter Interface (built-in state reporting)
rust
trait AgentAdapter {
    // Agent tells us what it's doing
    fn subscribe_progress(&self) -> Receiver<ProgressEvent>;
}

enum ProgressEvent {
    ReadingFile(PathBuf),
    EditingFile(PathBuf),
    RunningCommand(String),
    Thinking(String),
}
No external monitoring needed — agents self-report through the interface.

2. Worktree filesystem watching
For detecting changes agents make:

rust
// Use notify-rs or similar
let watcher = notify::recommended_watcher(|res| {
    match res {
        Ok(Event { kind: Modify(_), paths, .. }) => {
            event_bus.emit(Event::FileChanged { path });
        }
    }
})?;

watcher.watch(worktree_path, RecursiveMode::Recursive)?;
Standard filesystem watching — no custom hooks needed.

3. Git operations (native)
rust
// git2-rs gives us everything
let repo = Repository::open(worktree_path)?;
let diff = repo.diff_tree_to_workdir(None, None)?;
Native git library — no hooks needed.

The only "hook" you might want:
Optional: Agent capability detection

If an agent doesn't support subscribe_progress(), you could fall back to heuristics:

rust
impl ClaudeCodeAdapter {
    fn infer_activity(&self) -> Option<String> {
        // Parse tmux pane content
        // Check recent file modifications
        // Scrape terminal output
    }
}
But this is adapter-specific fallback logic, not a system-wide hook mechanism.

Bottom line:
Hooks in old Scope: Necessary because you're observing an opaque process (Claude Code in tmux).

Hooks in new Scope: Unnecessary because:

Agents report state through the adapter interface
Filesystem changes are tracked by standard watchers
Git operations use native libraries
Event bus centralizes all state changes
The "transparency" comes from architecture (agent adapters, event bus, isolated worktrees), not from external monitoring (hooks).

Does this make sense, or did I misunderstand how your hooks work?

Yes, one feature I used hooks for was to report what the latest tool call was. We don't have that feature anymore. If user wants to see what's happening they click on the loop

Feb 14
Perfect. So hooks are gone entirely.

Old model (hooks):
Hooks monitor tmux/files → infer "agent is editing X"
Push updates to dashboard automatically
User sees activity feed in real-time without asking
New model (pull, not push):
Agent runs in worktree (opaque by default)
User clicks loop → opens inspector
Inspector shows:
Terminal (live PTY or replay)
Current diff
Verifier scores
Doer/verifier transcript
Activity tracking is on-demand, not ambient.

What this means for the event model:
Events are coarse-grained (loop lifecycle), not fine-grained (tool calls):

rust
// What we emit:
Event::LoopStarted { loop_id, task }
Event::LoopAttempt { loop_id, attempt_num }
Event::LoopVerifying { loop_id }
Event::LoopVerified { loop_id, scores, passed }
Event::LoopPassed { loop_id, commit_sha }
Event::LoopFailed { loop_id, reason }

// What we DON'T emit (user pulls this if they care):
Event::AgentReadFile { path }        // ❌ removed
Event::AgentEditedFile { path }      // ❌ removed
Event::AgentRanCommand { command }   // ❌ removed
When user clicks a loop:
Backend sends:

rust
#[tauri::command]
async fn get_loop_details(loop_id: LoopId) -> LoopDetails {
    LoopDetails {
        status: "running",
        current_attempt: 2,
        terminal_output: read_pty_buffer(loop_id),
        current_diff: get_worktree_diff(loop_id),
        attempts: vec![
            Attempt { /* full history */ },
        ],
        verifier_scores: latest_scores,
    }
}
Frontend renders:

Live terminal embed (xterm.js showing PTY)
Diff viewer (monaco-diff)
Verifier pyramid heatmap
Attempt history timeline
Updated event schema (hooks removed):
typescript
type ScopeEvent =
  // Ticket
  | { type: "ticket.created", ticket_id, title }
  | { type: "ticket.status_changed", ticket_id, status }

  // DAG
  | { type: "dag.planned", ticket_id, nodes, edges }

  // Loop lifecycle (coarse-grained only)
  | { type: "loop.started", loop_id, task }
  | { type: "loop.attempt", loop_id, attempt_num }
  | { type: "loop.verifying", loop_id }
  | { type: "loop.verified", loop_id, scores, passed, feedback }
  | { type: "loop.passed", loop_id, commit_sha }
  | { type: "loop.failed", loop_id, reason }
  | { type: "loop.escalated", loop_id }

  // Human interaction
  | { type: "loop.rubric_updated", loop_id, rubric }
  | { type: "loop.terminal_attached", loop_id, user_id }

  // Presence
  | { type: "user.joined", user_id, viewing_loop? }
This simplifies everything:
No hooks to install. No tmux scraping. No file watching for activity feed.

Just:

Loop executor emits lifecycle events
Dashboard shows DAG with status colors
User clicks → inspector pulls current state

Feb 14
Scope: Product Requirements Document
Document Version
Version: 1.0
Date: February 14, 2026
Status: Draft

Executive Summary
Scope is an orchestration system for building software with AI agents. It transforms agentic coding from an opaque, thrashing process into a transparent, verifiable, bounded workflow. Scope's core innovation is the doer↔verifier loop primitive: every unit of work is a cycle where an agent produces an artifact, a verifier checks it against explicit criteria, and the loop terminates on success or escalates on failure.

Target users: Developers and teams building software with AI coding agents (Claude Code, Codex, Aider, etc.)

Core value proposition: Turn intent into verified change, continuously, under explicit constraints—without context rot, thrashing, or black-box uncertainty.

Product Principles
1. Verification is sovereign
Nothing counts until it survives a verifier.

Prefer executable verifiers (tests, typecheck, fuzz, property tests)
Use natural-language verifiers only where execution can't reach (spec/UX/logic gaps)
Verification is an independent actor: decoupled context, adversarial stance
2. Loops > plans
Plans are hypotheses. Loops are reality contact.

Every unit of work is a doer↔verifier loop with a stop condition
Long work is composition: a DAG of loops, not a monolithic sprint
3. Boundedness is kindness (and reliability)
Unbounded agents thrash. Thrash looks like progress until it isn't.

Hard budgets: time, tokens, retries, parallelism
Backoff, quarantine, escalation
"Admit failure" is a first-class outcome
4. Separation of concerns, everywhere
Scope is modular by design:

UI is a skin (Tauri/CLI/web) over a stable event/command API
Agents are adapters (Claude Code/Codex/etc), not the architecture
File/runtime isolation is a substrate (worktrees/sandboxes), not a side quest
5. State is the product
The user doesn't want chats; they want artifacts.

Every loop produces: patches, diffs, logs, proofs, receipts
Everything is replayable: inputs, decisions, verifiers, outputs
6. Prefer invariants to instructions
Natural language is leaky. Invariants are sharp.

Translate "make it better" into measurable constraints
When porting/rewriting: equivalence harnesses + property tests
7. Default to safe autonomy
Autonomy is earned by demonstrated verifier performance.

Start conservative (small loops, frequent gates)
Expand autonomy only when verifiers show stability
8. Humans own the gradient
Agents do execution and exploration. Humans do:

Goal selection
Architectural decisions
Risk acceptance
Quality bar
Scope maximizes "human time spent on high-leverage judgment."

9. Transparency over magic
If Scope can't explain what it did, it didn't do it.

Show why a step was taken, what it changed, and how it was verified
No hidden prompts as the source of truth; the DAG + receipts are
10. Culture: anti-vampire
Scope is designed to prevent pace inflation.

Replace dopamine loops with verifier-gated loops
Encourage sustainable throughput: steady, audited, compounding
Product Pitch
The Problem
Context rot kills productivity. Every task you give an AI coding agent accumulates context: file contents, failed attempts, exploratory tangents. When the agent's context window fills up, critical details vanish. Your session becomes a diluted mess of half-remembered explorations.

Black-box async agents don't help. Tools that run agents in the background give you no visibility, no control, and no way to intervene when things go wrong. You submit a task and hope.

Unbounded autonomy leads to thrashing. Without explicit stop conditions and verification criteria, agents burn tokens exploring dead ends that look like progress until they aren't.

The Solution
Scope orchestrates AI agents through verifier-gated loops.

Every task becomes a DAG of atomic loops. Each loop:

Runs in isolated git worktree (fresh context, no contamination)
Has a doer (AI agent) and verifier (test pyramid)
Terminates on verifier pass or max turns
Escalates to human on failure
You get:

Transparency: Real-time DAG view, click any loop to inspect terminal/diff/scores
Control: Pause, resume, abort, attach to terminal, edit verification criteria mid-flight
Reliability: Bounded retries, explicit failure modes, no silent thrashing
Collaboration: Multiplayer-first architecture, real-time sync, presence indicators
Auditability: Full event logs, replay any session, tune prompts from traces
Scope feels like Figma meets Linear meets Terminal meets GitHub CI.

Feature Set
MVP Features (v1.0)
1. Core Loop Execution
Doer↔verifier loop with configurable max turns (default: 5)
Agent adapter interface supporting multiple coding agents:
Claude Code (primary)
Codex
Aider
Git worktree isolation per loop (automatic creation, merge, cleanup)
Verification pyramid with 5 layers:
Static checks (lint, typecheck, dead code, complexity)
Deterministic tests (unit, integration, regression)
Generative tests (property-based, fuzz, metamorphic)
Runtime checks (perf budgets, security, stability)
Semantic checks (contract/rubric assertions)
Merge gate with weighted scoring + mandatory hard gates
Escalation on failure (max turns hit → human intervention required)
2. Task Decomposition
HTN-style decomposition into DAG of atomic loops
Automatic decomposition criteria:
Can task fit in context?
Does task have verification constraint?
Dependency-aware execution (topological sort)
Parallel execution where dependencies allow
Blocking on failure (downstream loops pause, await resolution)
3. Tauri Desktop Application
Project View (Linear-style):

Table/board of tickets
Status indicators: open, in_progress, blocked, done
Assignee (human/agent/collaborative)
DAG preview per ticket
Ticket Detail View (GitHub CI-style):

DAG visualization (nodes = loops, edges = dependencies)
Color-coded status (pending/running/passed/failed/escalated)
Real-time updates via WebSocket
Parallel branches visible
Loop Inspector (click any loop node):

Terminal embed (xterm.js, live PTY or replay)
Diff viewer (monaco-diff showing worktree changes)
Quality rubric editor (pyramid layers, live-editable thresholds)
Verifier scores (current attempt + history chart)
Attempt history (expandable timeline)
Doer/verifier transcripts (for debugging)
4. Multiplayer Collaboration
Central collab server (Rust/Axum WebSocket relay)
Real-time event sync (all clients in same project room)
Presence system:
Who's viewing which ticket/loop
Live cursor positions in rubric editor
Avatars/indicators
Conflict resolution:
Rubric edits: last-write-wins (v1), CRDT swap later
Loop execution: client-owned (no conflicts)
Room-based access via project ID
5. Configuration & Persistence
Per-repo configuration (.scope/):

rubric-template.yaml (default verification pyramid)
prompts/ (tuned doer/verifier prompts)
logs/ (structured event logs + raw transcripts)
SQLite event log (queryable, replayable)
Rubric generation:

Infer from existing tooling (detect test frameworks, linters)
Merge with repo-wide defaults
Live-editable in UI
6. Human Intervention
Manual gates after loop completion (optional checkpoints)
Mid-loop intervention:
Attach to terminal (take over manually)
Edit rubric (affects next attempt)
Pause/resume loop
Cancel loop (and descendants)
Escalation resolution:
Fix: human resolves issue, loop retries
Skip: mark loop as done, unblock downstream
Redefine: change task, reset loop
7. Event System
Event bus architecture:

Local SQLite log (append-only)
WebSocket broadcast to collab server
UI subscribes to event stream
Event types: (see detailed schema in interview notes)

Ticket lifecycle
DAG planning
Loop lifecycle (started, attempt, verifying, verified, passed, failed, escalated)
Human interactions (rubric updates, terminal attach)
Presence updates
8. Prompt Evolution (EOD Tuning)
Batch job analyzes day's event logs
Optimization targets:
Fewer turns to verifier pass
Lower token cost
Fewer escalations
Higher verifier confidence
Tuned prompts saved to .scope/prompts/
Versioned with repo
9. Command/Query API (Tauri ↔ Frontend)
Commands (user actions):

create_ticket(title, description)
start_loop(loop_id)
update_rubric(loop_id, rubric)
resolve_escalation(loop_id, resolution)
attach_terminal(loop_id)
pause_loop(loop_id)
cancel_loop(loop_id)
Queries (UI data):

subscribe_events() → event stream
get_loop_details(loop_id) → terminal, diff, scores
list_tickets() → all tickets with status
Post-MVP Features (v1.1+)
10. Advanced Verification
Custom verifier plugins (user-defined checks)
Verifier composition (chain multiple verifiers)
Adaptive thresholds (ML-tuned merge gates based on history)
11. Enhanced Collaboration
Shared cursors in code diffs (Figma-style)
Voice/video integration (call into loop discussion)
Async comments on loops (GitHub PR-style)
@mentions to request human review
12. Integrations
GitHub/GitLab webhooks (trigger Scope on PR)
Slack/Discord notifications (escalation alerts)
Linear/Jira sync (bidirectional ticket updates)
Datadog/Sentry monitoring (loop performance metrics)
13. Advanced UI
Timeline view (Gantt-style, see all loops over time)
Resource dashboard (token usage, cost tracking, agent utilization)
Heatmaps (which files/modules get most loop activity)
Search/filter (find loops by status, agent, file touched)
14. Self-Hosting & Enterprise
Docker image for collab server
Custom server URL support
SSO integration (SAML, OIDC)
Audit logs (compliance-ready event exports)
Role-based access (admin/reviewer/executor)
User Stories
Story 1: Solo Developer
As a solo developer building a side project,
I want to decompose a feature into verified atomic tasks,
So that I get high-quality code without context rot or thrashing.

Acceptance:

Create ticket "Add user authentication"
Scope decomposes into: schema → API → tests → integration
Each loop runs in isolation, passes verification pyramid
I get 4 clean commits, full audit trail
Story 2: Team Lead
As a team lead managing 3 junior devs + 2 AI agents,
I want to see real-time progress on all work,
So that I can intervene when loops get stuck.

Acceptance:

Open Scope dashboard, see DAG of all active loops
Click escalated loop, attach to terminal, fix issue
Resume loop, see it pass verification
Downstream loops auto-unblock
Story 3: Quality Engineer
As a QA engineer defining acceptance criteria,
I want to edit verification rubrics mid-flight,
So that I can tighten quality gates without restarting work.

Acceptance:

Open loop inspector while loop is running
Edit rubric: add "code coverage > 80%" check
Next verifier attempt includes new check
Loop fails, agent retries with coverage in mind
Story 4: Remote Team
As a remote team across timezones,
I want to collaborate on the same ticket asynchronously,
So that work continues 24/7 without blocking.

Acceptance:

Alice (SF) starts ticket, spawns 3 loops, goes to sleep
Bob (London) wakes up, sees loop escalated, resolves
Charlie (Tokyo) reviews rubric, tightens threshold
All changes sync in real-time, full audit trail
Story 5: Security Auditor
As a security auditor,
I want to replay any Scope session to verify compliance,
So that I can prove code changes were verified.

Acceptance:

Query event log: "show all loops for ticket X"
Replay: see every attempt, verifier score, human intervention
Export: PDF report with commit SHAs, timestamps, verifier results
Success Metrics
Adoption Metrics
Weekly active users (WAU)
Loops executed per user (engagement)
Tickets created per week (usage depth)
Quality Metrics
Verifier pass rate (% loops passing without escalation)
Average turns to pass (efficiency trend)
Escalation rate (% loops requiring human intervention)
Efficiency Metrics
Time to resolution (ticket created → all loops passed)
Token cost per loop (optimization target)
Parallelism utilization (% time with >1 loop running)
Reliability Metrics
Loop failure rate (% hitting max turns)
Verifier stability (confidence scores over time)
Session replay success (% logs fully replayable)
Technical Requirements
Performance
Loop start latency: < 2s (worktree creation + agent spawn)
Event latency: < 100ms (emit → UI update)
Dashboard responsiveness: 60fps DAG rendering, smooth pan/zoom
Multiplayer sync: < 500ms (event broadcast to all clients)
Scalability
Concurrent loops per user: 20+ (limited by machine resources)
Clients per project room: 50+ (collab server capacity)
Event log size: Handle 100k+ events per repo
Worktree disk usage: Auto-cleanup to prevent bloat
Reliability
Event delivery: At-least-once (idempotent handlers)
State consistency: Eventual (CRDT or last-write-wins)
Crash recovery: Loop resume from last checkpoint
Data durability: SQLite WAL mode, fsync on critical events
Security
Collab server auth: Optional (v1 is trust-based, auth in v1.1)
Event encryption: Optional TLS for WebSocket
Secrets handling: Never log API keys, tokens, credentials
Sandbox escape: Runtime isolation delegated to agent adapters
Non-Goals (v1.0)
No agent training: Scope orchestrates existing agents, doesn't train them
No code generation: Scope verifies, doesn't generate
No hosted service: Self-hosted only (v1), cloud offering later
No mobile app: Desktop + web only (v1)
No Windows native: Cross-platform Tauri works, but optimize for macOS/Linux first
Open Questions
Rubric inference accuracy: How good can we make default rubric generation without manual tuning?
CRDT necessity: Can we ship v1 with last-write-wins, or do conflicts break the UX?
Agent adapter standardization: Should we publish an adapter spec for community agents?
Collab server hosting: Do we provide a public relay, or is self-hosted the default?
Prompt tuning triggers: EOD batch job, or real-time adaptive tuning?
Appendix: Glossary
Loop: Atomic unit of work (doer↔verifier cycle with stop condition)
DAG: Directed Acyclic Graph of loops (represents task decomposition)
Doer: AI coding agent (Claude Code, Codex, etc.)
Verifier: Test pyramid + scoring system
Rubric: Verification criteria (pyramid layers + merge gate)
Escalation: Loop failure requiring human intervention
Worktree: Isolated git workspace per loop
Event bus: Pub/sub system for state changes
Collab server: WebSocket relay for multiplayer sync
Presence: Real-time "who's viewing what" system
End of PRD

Scope is the orchestrator that manages agents that generates code and agents that verify the diff.

Scope: Product Requirements Document
Document Version
Version: 1.0
Date: February 14, 2026
Status: Draft

Executive Summary
Scope is an orchestration system that manages AI agents to generate and verify code. It transforms agentic coding from an opaque, thrashing process into a transparent, verifiable, bounded workflow. Scope's core innovation is the maker↔checker loop primitive: a maker agent generates code, a checker agent verifies it against explicit criteria, and the loop terminates on approval or escalates on failure.

Target users: Developers and teams building software with AI coding agents

Core value proposition: Turn intent into verified change, continuously, under explicit constraints—without context rot, thrashing, or black-box uncertainty.

Product Principles
1. Verification is sovereign
Nothing counts until it survives a checker.

Checker agents run verification pyramids (tests, typecheck, lint, security, semantics)
Checker is independent: decoupled context, adversarial stance
Human-defined rubrics set the quality bar
2. Loops > plans
Plans are hypotheses. Loops are reality contact.

Every unit of work is a maker↔checker loop with a stop condition
Long work is composition: a DAG of loops, not a monolithic sprint
3. Boundedness is kindness (and reliability)
Unbounded agents thrash. Thrash looks like progress until it isn't.

Hard budgets: time, tokens, retries, parallelism
Backoff, quarantine, escalation
"Admit failure" is a first-class outcome
4. Separation of concerns, everywhere
Scope is modular by design:

UI is a skin (Tauri/CLI/web) over a stable event/command API
Agents are adapters (maker agents + checker agents), not the architecture
File/runtime isolation is a substrate (worktrees/sandboxes), not a side quest
5. State is the product
The user doesn't want chats; they want artifacts.

Every loop produces: patches, diffs, logs, proofs, receipts
Everything is replayable: inputs, decisions, verifications, outputs
6. Prefer invariants to instructions
Natural language is leaky. Invariants are sharp.

Translate "make it better" into measurable constraints
When porting/rewriting: equivalence harnesses + property tests
7. Default to safe autonomy
Autonomy is earned by demonstrated checker performance.

Start conservative (small loops, frequent gates)
Expand autonomy only when checkers show stability
8. Humans own the gradient
Agents do execution and exploration. Humans do:

Goal selection
Architectural decisions
Risk acceptance
Quality bar
Scope maximizes "human time spent on high-leverage judgment."

9. Transparency over magic
If Scope can't explain what it did, it didn't do it.

Show why a step was taken, what it changed, and how it was verified
No hidden prompts as the source of truth; the DAG + receipts are
10. Culture: anti-vampire
Scope is designed to prevent pace inflation.

Replace dopamine loops with checker-gated loops
Encourage sustainable throughput: steady, audited, compounding
Product Pitch
The Problem
Context rot kills productivity. Every task you give an AI coding agent accumulates context: file contents, failed attempts, exploratory tangents. When the agent's context window fills up, critical details vanish. Your session becomes a diluted mess of half-remembered explorations.

Black-box async agents don't help. Tools that run agents in the background give you no visibility, no control, and no way to intervene when things go wrong. You submit a task and hope.

Unbounded autonomy leads to thrashing. Without explicit stop conditions and verification criteria, agents burn tokens exploring dead ends that look like progress until they aren't.

Self-verification doesn't work. Maker agents are optimistic—they think their code works. You need an independent adversary.

The Solution
Scope orchestrates maker and checker agents through verification loops.

Every task becomes a DAG of atomic loops. Each loop:

Runs in isolated git worktree (fresh context, no contamination)
Has a maker agent (generates code) and checker agent (verifies diff)
Terminates on checker approval or max turns
Escalates to human on failure
The checker agent:

Sees only: the diff, the original intent, and the quality rubric
Has no access to maker's reasoning (forces independent judgment)
Runs verification pyramid: static checks → tests → generative tests → runtime checks → semantic validation
Returns: scores per layer, pass/fail, concrete feedback for maker
You get:

Transparency: Real-time DAG view, click any loop to inspect terminal/diff/scores
Control: Pause, resume, abort, attach to terminal, edit verification criteria mid-flight
Reliability: Bounded retries, explicit failure modes, no silent thrashing
Collaboration: Multiplayer-first architecture, real-time sync, presence indicators
Auditability: Full event logs, replay any session, tune prompts from traces
Scope feels like Figma meets Linear meets Terminal meets GitHub CI.

Feature Set
MVP Features (v1.0)
1. Core Maker↔Checker Loop
Maker agent generates code/patches
Supports multiple coding agents via adapter interface:
Claude Code (primary)
Codex
Aider
Receives: task, context, history of prior attempts
Produces: code diff
Checker agent verifies diff
Independent agent (separate context from maker)
Receives: diff, original intent, quality rubric
Runs verification pyramid with 5 layers:
Static checks (lint, typecheck, dead code, complexity)
Deterministic tests (unit, integration, regression)
Generative tests (property-based, fuzz, metamorphic)
Runtime checks (perf budgets, security, stability)
Semantic checks (contract/rubric assertions)
Returns: scores per layer, pass/fail, feedback
Loop orchestration:
Max turns configurable (default: 5)
Maker sees checker feedback, retries
History compressed (old attempts) + full detail (current attempt)
Terminates on checker approval or max turns
Escalates to human on failure
Git worktree isolation per loop:
Automatic creation, squash/rebase on success, cleanup
Each loop gets fresh workspace
Merge gate with weighted scoring + mandatory hard gates:
Example: "Overall score > 0.8 AND typecheck MUST pass AND security MUST pass"
2. Task Decomposition
HTN-style decomposition into DAG of atomic loops
Decomposer agent analyzes task:
Can task fit in context?
Does task have verification constraint?
If no → decompose into subtasks
If yes → create atomic loop
Dependency-aware execution:
Topological sort
Parallel execution where dependencies allow
Blocking on failure (downstream loops pause, await resolution)
3. Tauri Desktop Application
Project View (Linear-style):

Table/board of tickets

DAG visualization (nodes = loops, edges = dependencies)
Color-coded status:
Gray: pending
Blue: running (maker working)
Yellow: verifying (checker running)
Green: passed
Red: failed
Orange: escalated
Real-time updates via WebSocket
Parallel branches visible

Terminal embed (xterm.js):
Live PTY if loop is running
Replay if loop is done
Shows maker agent's work

Heatmap showing layer scores

Live-editable pyramid configuration

Full conversation for debugging
Maker's reasoning (if available)

Auth/permissions in v1.1
5. Configuration & Persistence
Per-repo configuration (.scope/):

.scope/
├── rubric-template.yaml       # Default verification pyramid
├── prompts/
│   ├── maker-prompt.md        # Tuned maker agent prompt
│   └── checker-prompt.md      # Tuned checker agent prompt
├── logs/
│   ├── events.db              # SQLite event log
│   └── transcripts/
│       └── loop-{id}/
│           ├── attempt-1.json
│           └── attempt-2.json
└── worktrees/                 # Git worktrees (gitignored)
Rubric generation:

Scan repo for existing tooling (package.json, pyproject.toml, etc.)
Detect: test framework, linter, type checker
Generate pyramid config automatically
Merge with .scope/rubric-template.yaml if exists
User can edit in UI, changes saved to template
Example rubric:

yaml
static_checks:
  enabled: true
  checks: [eslint, tsc, prettier]
  weight: 0.2

deterministic_tests:
  enabled: true
  checks: [jest]
  weight: 0.3

merge_gate:
  threshold: 0.8
  mandatory: [tsc, jest]
6. Human Intervention
Manual gates:

Optional checkpoints after loop completion
User reviews diff before proceeding to dependent loops
Mid-loop intervention:

Attach to terminal: Take over maker agent manually
Edit rubric: Changes apply to next checker run
Pause/resume loop: Freeze state, resume later
Cancel loop: Abort loop + all descendants
Escalation resolution: When loop hits max turns:

Fix: Human makes changes directly, loop retries with checker
Skip: Mark loop as done, unblock downstream (with warning)
Redefine: Change task description, reset loop from attempt 1
7. Event System
Event bus architecture:

Local SQLite log (append-only, queryable)
WebSocket broadcast to collab server
UI subscribes to event stream
All state changes = events
Event types:

typescript
type ScopeEvent =
  // Ticket lifecycle
  | { type: "ticket.created", ticket_id, title, description }
  | { type: "ticket.status_changed", ticket_id, status }

  // DAG composition
  | { type: "dag.planned", ticket_id, nodes: Loop[], edges: Dependency[] }

  // Loop lifecycle
  | { type: "loop.started", loop_id, ticket_id, task, rubric }
  | { type: "loop.maker_attempt", loop_id, attempt_num, diff_preview }
  | { type: "loop.checker_running", loop_id, attempt_num }
  | { type: "loop.checker_result", loop_id, attempt_num, scores, passed, feedback }
  | { type: "loop.passed", loop_id, commit_sha, total_attempts }
  | { type: "loop.failed", loop_id, reason: "max_turns" | "budget" }
  | { type: "loop.escalated", loop_id, blocking_downstream: loop_id[] }
  | { type: "loop.resolved", loop_id, resolution }

  // Human interaction
  | { type: "loop.rubric_updated", loop_id, rubric, user_id }
  | { type: "loop.terminal_attached", loop_id, user_id }

  // Resources
  | { type: "loop.tokens_used", loop_id, attempt_num, maker_tokens, checker_tokens }
  | { type: "loop.worktree_created", loop_id, path }

  // Presence
  | { type: "user.joined", user_id, viewing_ticket?, viewing_loop? }
  | { type: "user.left", user_id }
8. Agent Adapters
Maker Agent Interface:

rust
trait MakerAgent {
    async fn generate(
        &self,
        task: Task,
        context: Context,
        history: Vec<Attempt>,
    ) -> Result<Diff>;

    fn subscribe_progress(&self) -> Receiver<ProgressEvent>;
    async fn cancel(&self) -> Result<()>;
    fn capabilities(&self) -> AgentCapabilities;
}
Checker Agent Interface:

rust
trait CheckerAgent {
    async fn verify(
        &self,
        diff: &Diff,
        intent: &Task,
        rubric: &VerificationPyramid,
    ) -> VerificationResult;
}

struct VerificationResult {
    scores: PyramidScores,      // Per-layer scores
    passed: bool,               // Meets merge gate
    feedback: String,           // Concrete feedback for maker
    execution_time_ms: u64,
}
Supported agents (v1):

Makers: Claude Code, Codex, Aider
Checkers: Claude (Sonnet 4), GPT-4o (via API)
9. Prompt Evolution (EOD Tuning)
Automated prompt optimization:

Batch job runs nightly (or on-demand)
Analyzes event logs for patterns:
Which rubrics cause most maker failures?
Which checker feedback is most effective?
Where do tokens get wasted?
Optimization targets:

Fewer turns to checker approval
Lower token cost
Fewer escalations
Higher checker confidence scores
Output:

Updated prompts saved to .scope/prompts/
Versioned with git (prompts are code)
User can review/approve changes before applying
10. Command/Query API (Tauri ↔ Frontend)
Commands (mutations):

typescript

Queries (reads):

typescript

Post-MVP Features (v1.1+)
11. Advanced Checker Capabilities
Multi-checker consensus: Run 2+ checker agents, require agreement
Custom checker plugins: User-defined verification logic
Adaptive rubrics: ML-tuned thresholds based on historical performance
Checker explanations: Detailed reasoning for each layer score
12. Maker Agent Improvements
Maker self-critique: Before submitting to checker, maker reviews own diff
Incremental diffs: Maker can submit partial work for early checker feedback
Maker specialization: Route tasks to specialist makers (frontend, backend, tests)
13. Enhanced Collaboration
Shared cursors in diff viewer (Figma-style)
Live code reviews: Multiple humans can comment on diffs in real-time
@mentions to request checker re-run or human review
Async comments: GitHub PR-style discussion threads per loop
14. Integrations
CI/CD pipelines: Trigger Scope on GitHub PR, report results as checks
Slack/Discord: Escalation notifications, status updates
Linear/Jira: Bidirectional ticket sync
Monitoring: Datadog/Sentry integration for loop performance metrics
15. Advanced UI
Timeline view: Gantt-style visualization of all loops over time
Resource dashboard: Token usage, cost tracking, agent utilization
Heatmaps: Which files/modules get most loop activity
Search/filter: Find loops by status, agent, file touched, error type
16. Enterprise Features
Self-hosted collab server: Docker image, custom deployment
SSO integration: SAML, OIDC
Audit logs: Compliance-ready event exports
Role-based access: Admin/reviewer/executor permissions
Budget controls: Token/cost limits per user/team
User Stories
Story 1: Solo Developer
As a solo developer building a side project,
I want to decompose a feature into verified atomic tasks,
So that I get high-quality code without context rot or thrashing.

Acceptance:

Create ticket "Add user authentication"
Scope decomposes into: schema → API → tests → integration
Each loop: maker generates code → checker verifies → passes
I get 4 clean commits, full audit trail
Story 2: Team Lead
As a team lead managing 3 devs + AI agents,
I want to see real-time progress on all work,
So that I can intervene when loops get stuck.

Acceptance:

Open Scope dashboard, see DAG of all active loops
Click escalated loop, see checker feedback: "Test coverage only 45%"
Attach to terminal, add tests manually
Resume loop, checker approves, downstream loops unblock
Story 3: Quality Engineer
As a QA engineer defining acceptance criteria,
I want to edit verification rubrics mid-flight,
So that I can tighten quality gates without restarting work.

Acceptance:

Open loop inspector while maker is working
Edit rubric: "code coverage > 80%", "security scan MUST pass"
Next checker run includes new requirements
Maker retries with updated constraints
Story 4: Code Reviewer
As a senior engineer reviewing AI-generated code,
I want to see exactly what the checker verified,
So that I can trust the output without re-reviewing everything.

Acceptance:

Open passed loop, see checker scores: all layers green
Expand "deterministic tests": 47/47 passing
Expand "security": no vulnerabilities found
View diff, see maker's changes are minimal and focused
Approve merge with confidence
Story 5: Security Auditor
As a security auditor,
I want to replay any Scope session to verify compliance,
So that I can prove code changes were verified.

Acceptance:

Query event log: "show all loops touching auth code in Q4"
Replay: see every maker attempt, checker result, human intervention
Export: PDF report with commit SHAs, checker scores, timestamps
Verify: all security checks passed on production code
Success Metrics
Adoption Metrics
Weekly active users (WAU)
Loops executed per user (engagement)
Tickets created per week (usage depth)
Quality Metrics
Checker approval rate (% loops passing without escalation)
Average attempts to approval (efficiency trend)
Escalation rate (% loops requiring human intervention)
First-attempt pass rate (maker improving over time)
Efficiency Metrics
Time to resolution (ticket created → all loops passed)
Token cost per loop (maker + checker tokens)
Parallelism utilization (% time with >1 loop running)
Checker latency (time to run verification pyramid)
Reliability Metrics
Loop failure rate (% hitting max turns)
Checker stability (confidence scores over time)
False positives (checker approves bad code)
False negatives (checker rejects good code)
Technical Requirements
Performance
Loop start latency: < 2s (worktree + agent spawn)
Checker latency: < 30s (full pyramid run)
Event latency: < 100ms (emit → UI update)
Dashboard: 60fps DAG rendering
Multiplayer sync: < 500ms (event broadcast)
Scalability
Concurrent loops per user: 20+
Clients per project room: 50+
Event log: Handle 100k+ events per repo
Worktree disk: Auto-cleanup to prevent bloat
Reliability
Event delivery: At-least-once (idempotent)
State consistency: Eventual
Crash recovery: Resume loops from last checkpoint
Data durability: SQLite WAL mode, fsync
Security
Secrets: Never log API keys, tokens, credentials
Isolation: Each loop in separate worktree
Checker independence: No shared state with maker
Sandbox escape: Delegated to agent runtimes
Non-Goals (v1.0)
No agent training: Scope orchestrates, doesn't train
No hosted service: Self-hosted only (cloud later)
No mobile app: Desktop + web only
No Windows optimization: macOS/Linux first
No checker-less mode: Every loop requires checker
Open Questions
Checker agent cost: Will running a second LLM per attempt be too expensive?
Rubric inference: How accurate can auto-generated rubrics be?
Checker hallucination: What if checker gives false approval?
Maker-checker communication: Should maker see checker's full reasoning, or just feedback?
Multi-checker consensus: When is 2+ checkers worth the cost?
Appendix: Glossary
Loop: Atomic unit of work (maker↔checker cycle)
Maker: AI agent that generates code
Checker: AI agent that verifies diffs
DAG: Directed Acyclic Graph of loops
Rubric: Verification criteria (pyramid + merge gate)
Pyramid: Layered verification (static → tests → generative → runtime → semantic)
Escalation: Loop failure requiring human intervention
Worktree: Isolated git workspace per loop
Event bus: Pub/sub system for state changes
Collab server: WebSocket relay for multiplayer
End of PRD

What can we apply here from Lean / Kanban?

Feb 14
Excellent question. Lean/Kanban principles map beautifully to Scope's architecture. Here's what we should apply:

Lean/Kanban Principles for Scope
1. Visualize the Workflow ✅ Already doing this
Kanban board = DAG visualization

Current design:

Ticket Detail View shows full DAG
Color-coded loop states (pending/running/passed/failed)
Real-time updates
Add from Kanban:

Swimlanes for different loop types:
Backend loops
Frontend loops
Test loops
Infrastructure loops
Columns represent states:
Backlog (planned but not started)
In Progress (maker working)
Verifying (checker running)
Blocked (escalated)
Done (passed)
UI enhancement:

Ticket: Add Auth
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Backlog │ In Progress │ Verifying │ Blocked │ Done │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│         │ API ●       │           │ Schema ⚠│      │
│         │             │ Tests ○   │         │ DB ✓ │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
2. Limit Work in Progress (WIP) ⚠️ Missing - add this
Problem without WIP limits:

User spawns 20 loops in parallel
All hit token rate limits
Thrashing, no throughput
Solution: Configurable WIP limits

yaml
# .scope/limits.yaml
wip_limits:
  per_ticket: 5           # Max concurrent loops per ticket
  per_agent: 3            # Max concurrent loops per agent instance
  global: 10              # Total concurrent loops across all tickets

  by_type:
    maker_loops: 5        # Max loops in "maker working" state
    checker_loops: 3      # Max loops in "checker verifying" state
```

**UI indication:**
```
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│ In Progress (3/5)               │  ← Shows WIP limit
├─────────────────────────────────┤
│ API ●                           │
│ Tests ●                         │
│ Frontend ●                      │
└─────────────────────────────────┘

⚠ WIP limit reached. Complete a loop to start next.
```

**Queue behavior:**
- When WIP limit hit, new loops enter "Ready" queue
- As loops complete, queued loops auto-start (FIFO by default)
- User can reorder queue (drag-and-drop priority)

---

### 3. **Manage Flow** ⚠️ Partially doing - enhance this

**Kanban metric: Cycle Time**
- Time from loop start → loop passed
- Track per loop type, per ticket, per agent

**Add to Scope:**

**Flow metrics dashboard:**
```
┌─ Flow Metrics ────────────────────────────────┐
│                                               │
│ Cycle Time (avg):           4.2 min          │
│ ├─ Maker time:              2.1 min          │
│ └─ Checker time:            2.1 min          │
│                                               │
│ Lead Time (avg):            8.5 min          │
│ (planning → done)                             │
│                                               │
│ Throughput:                 12 loops/hour    │
│                                               │
│ Blocked Time:               15% (⚠ high)     │
│ └─ Top blocker: Escalations (80%)            │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Cumulative Flow Diagram (CFD):**
- X-axis: time
- Y-axis: number of loops
- Stacked areas: Backlog, In Progress, Verifying, Blocked, Done
- Spot bottlenecks visually (growing Blocked area = problem)
```
Loops
  ▲
30│         ╱──── Done
  │       ╱╱
20│     ╱╱  ──── Verifying
  │   ╱╱ ╱╱
10│ ╱╱ ╱╱  ──── In Progress
  │╱╱╱╱
 0└─────────────────────────► Time
   Mon  Tue  Wed  Thu  Fri
Identify bottlenecks:

If "Verifying" area grows → checker is slow (optimize rubric)
If "Blocked" area grows → escalations need faster human response
If "In Progress" area grows → maker is thrashing (check prompts)
4. Make Process Policies Explicit ✅ Rubrics do this
Current rubrics = process policies

Example:

yaml
# This IS a Kanban policy: "Definition of Done"
merge_gate:
  threshold: 0.8
  mandatory: [typecheck, security, tests]
Add more explicit policies:

yaml
# .scope/policies.yaml

# When to escalate
escalation_policy:
  max_turns: 5
  auto_escalate_if:
    - checker_confidence < 0.3  # Checker is uncertain
    - same_error_3_times         # Maker is stuck
    - tokens_exceeded: 10000     # Cost runaway

# When to auto-approve (skip checker for low-risk changes)
auto_approve_policy:
  if_all:
    - files_changed < 3
    - lines_added < 50
    - no_files_match: ["src/auth/*", "src/payments/*"]
  then: skip_checker_layers: [generative_tests, runtime_checks]

# When to require human gate
manual_gate_policy:
  require_human_approval_if:
    - touches: ["database/migrations/*"]
    - security_score < 0.9
    - breaks_api_contract: true
```

**UI: Show active policies**
```
Loop: API endpoint
├─ Policy: Standard (5 turns, full pyramid)
├─ Policy: Manual gate required (touches auth)
└─ Policy: WIP limit 3/5
```

6. Improve Collaboratively, Evolve Experimentally ⚠️ Missing - add A/B testing
Kaizen for Scope: Experiment with policies

A/B test rubrics:

yaml
# Run 50% of loops with strict rubric, 50% with lenient
experiment:
  name: "Coverage threshold test"
  variants:
    - name: strict
      weight: 0.5
      rubric_override:
        deterministic_tests:
          coverage_threshold: 0.8

    - name: lenient
      weight: 0.5
      rubric_override:
        deterministic_tests:
          coverage_threshold: 0.6

  success_metric: attempts_to_pass
  duration: 1_week
```

**Show results:**
```
Experiment: Coverage threshold
├─ Strict (80%):   avg 3.2 attempts, 2 escalations
└─ Lenient (60%):  avg 2.1 attempts, 0 escalations ✓

💡 Recommendation: Use lenient (60%) threshold
   Reduces attempts by 34% with no quality loss
```

**Gradual rollout (Canary):**
- New prompt/rubric → applies to 10% of loops
- If metrics improve → expand to 50% → 100%
- If metrics degrade → rollback automatically

### 7. **Theory of Constraints** 🎯 Apply this heavily

**Identify the bottleneck in your workflow, optimize it, repeat.**

┌─ Bottleneck Analysis ─────────────────────────┐
│                                               │
│ Current bottleneck: Checker (Verifying)      │
│                                               │
│ Evidence:                                     │
│ • Avg checker time: 45s (maker: 20s)         │
│ • Verifying queue: 5 loops waiting           │
│ • Checker utilization: 95% (maker: 60%)      │
│                                               │
│ 💡 Recommendations:                           │
│ 1. Reduce rubric scope (disable slow layers) │
│ 2. Parallelize checker layers                │
│ 3. Add second checker instance               │
│                                               │
│ Projected impact:                             │
│ • -30s avg cycle time                        │
│ • +40% throughput                            │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Auto-optimization:**
- Scope notices "Verifying" is the bottleneck
- Suggests: "Disable generative tests for low-risk files"
- User approves → policy updates → flow improves
- Repeat for next bottleneck

### 8. **Pull System** ✅ Loops already work this way

**Pull = work is pulled when capacity is available (vs. pushed)**

**Scope already does this:**
- Loops don't start until dependencies complete (pull)
- WIP limits ensure no overload (pull)
- Queue auto-starts next loop when slot opens (pull)

**Make it more explicit:**
```
┌─ Work Queue ──────────────────────────────────┐
│                                               │
│ Ready to start (3):                           │
│ 1. API endpoint    [Start Now]               │
│ 2. Tests           [Start Now]               │
│ 3. Docs            [Start Now]               │
│                                               │
│ Waiting on dependencies (2):                  │
│ 4. Integration     (needs API)               │
│ 5. E2E tests       (needs Integration)       │
│                                               │
│ Blocked (1):                                  │
│ 6. Schema          (escalated)               │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
User can manually pull work:

Click "Start Now" → loop begins immediately (if under WIP limit)
Or let Scope auto-pull (default)
Concrete Feature Additions
Feature: WIP Limits (High Priority)
User story: As a user, I want to limit concurrent loops so that I don't overload agents and hit rate limits.

Implementation:

rust
struct WIPLimits {
    per_ticket: usize,
    per_agent: usize,
    global: usize,
}

impl LoopOrchestrator {
    fn can_start_loop(&self, loop_id: LoopId) -> bool {
        let current_wip = self.count_loops_in_state(LoopState::InProgress);
        current_wip < self.limits.global
    }

    fn start_next_from_queue(&mut self) {
        if self.can_start_loop(next_loop) {
            self.start_loop(next_loop);
        }
    }
}
UI:

Settings panel: configure WIP limits
DAG view: show "3/5" on column headers
Warning when user tries to exceed limit

    throughput: f64,           // loops/hour
    blocked_time_pct: f64,     // % time in escalated state
}

impl MetricsCollector {
    fn compute_metrics(&self, window: Duration) -> FlowMetrics {
        let events = self.event_log.query(window);
        // Calculate from events
    }
}

type ScopeEvent =
  // ... existing events ...

  // WIP tracking
  | { type: "wip.limit_reached", limit_type: "global" | "per_ticket", current: number, max: number }
  | { type: "wip.queue_added", loop_id, position: number }
  | { type: "wip.queue_started", loop_id, wait_time_ms: number }

  // Policy enforcement
  | { type: "policy.auto_approved", loop_id, policy_name: string }
  | { type: "policy.manual_gate_required", loop_id, reason: string }
  | { type: "policy.escalation_triggered", loop_id, trigger: string }

  // Retrospective
  | { type: "retrospective.generated", ticket_id, insights: Retrospective }
