What was the original motivation for WIP limits? How does it apply to Scope?

Feb 14
WIP Limits: Original Motivation vs. Scope
Original Motivation (Manufacturing/Kanban)
The Problem: Hidden Inventory Costs
Taiichi Ohno (Toyota Production System) observed:

Inventory hides problems
When you have 100 partially-built cars on the factory floor, you can't see which station is the bottleneck
Problems get buried in work-in-progress
Quality issues discovered late (after investment in downstream work)
Context switching waste
Workers jumping between 10 different cars → setup time, mistakes, lost focus
Each context switch = lost productivity
Long feedback cycles
Start 100 cars → discover design flaw on car #50 → already built 50 defective units
With WIP=5, you discover the flaw on unit #3, fix it before building more
Capital tied up
100 half-built cars = cash sitting idle
Can't sell incomplete inventory
Storage costs, handling costs
The insight: Limiting WIP forces you to fix bottlenecks instead of hiding them.

Does This Apply to Scope?
Let's examine each motivation:

1. "Inventory hides problems" → ⚠️ Partially applies
In manufacturing:

Can't see bottleneck when floor is full of WIP
In Scope:

Does NOT apply: DAG visualization makes bottlenecks visible regardless of WIP
If checker is slow, you SEE 10 loops stuck in "Verifying" state
Visibility isn't the issue
DOES apply: WIP hides quality problems
Spawn 20 loops with bad rubric → all fail at attempt 5
If you'd run 3 loops first, you'd catch the bad rubric after 3 failures and fix it
With WIP=20, you waste 17× the tokens before realizing the rubric is wrong
Verdict: WIP limits help you fail fast on systemic issues (bad prompts, bad rubrics, bad task decomposition)

2. "Context switching waste" → ❌ Does NOT apply (agents have no context switching cost)
In manufacturing:

Worker switches from assembling car A to car B → needs to remember where they left off, change tools, etc.
In Scope:

Maker/checker agents have no context switching cost
Each loop runs in isolated worktree
Agent doesn't "remember" previous loops
Running 3 loops vs 20 loops doesn't change agent performance per loop
Verdict: No context switching waste in Scope. This motivation doesn't apply.

3. "Long feedback cycles" → ✅ Strongly applies
In manufacturing:

Build 100 cars → discover flaw on #50 → 50 defective units
In Scope:

Spawn 20 loops with overly strict rubric
All 20 hit max turns and escalate
You don't discover "rubric is too strict" until all 20 fail
Wasted: 20 × 5 attempts × tokens
Better with WIP=3:

Spawn 3 loops
All 3 escalate with same error: "Coverage threshold unreachable"
You fix rubric after 3 failures (15 attempts)
Remaining 17 loops run with corrected rubric, pass on attempt 1-2
Concrete example:

Without WIP limits:
├─ Loop 1-20: all start immediately
├─ Attempts 1-100 (20 loops × 5 attempts each)
└─ Cost: 100 × $0.05 = $5.00

After 100 attempts, human realizes: "Oh, rubric is impossible"

With WIP=3:
├─ Loop 1-3: start
├─ Attempts 1-15 (3 loops × 5 attempts)
├─ Cost so far: $0.75
├─ Human sees pattern: "All 3 hitting same error"
├─ Fix rubric
├─ Loop 4-20: run with fixed rubric, pass in 1-2 attempts
├─ Attempts 16-50 (17 loops × 2 attempts avg)
└─ Total cost: $0.75 + $1.70 = $2.45

Savings: $2.55 (51%)
Verdict: WIP limits enable fast detection of systemic failures

4. "Capital tied up" → ⚠️ Partially applies (computational resources, not capital)
In manufacturing:

100 half-built cars = $$ sitting idle
In Scope:

No capital tied up (loops don't cost $ while waiting)
But computational resources get tied up:
20 concurrent loops → 20 agent instances
20 git worktrees on disk
20 terminals/PTYs
Machine thrashes (CPU, memory, I/O contention)
Rate limits:

Claude API: 50 requests/min
Spawn 20 loops → all 20 hit rate limit simultaneously → thrashing, retries, slowdown
With WIP=5 → requests spread over time → no rate limit hits
Verdict: WIP limits prevent resource exhaustion and rate limit thrashing

So... Does Scope Need WIP Limits?
✅ Yes, but for different reasons than manufacturing:
Manufacturing Reason	Applies to Scope?	Scope-Specific Reason
Hide problems	Partially	Hides quality/prompt issues, not bottlenecks
Context switching	❌ No	Agents have no context switching cost
Feedback cycles	✅ Yes	Fail fast on systemic issues (rubrics, prompts)
Capital tied up	Partially	Computational resources + rate limits
New Scope-Specific Motivations:
1. Token budget blowout

20 loops × 5 attempts × 10k tokens = 1M tokens = $50
User doesn't realize it until bill arrives
WIP=5 → only 250k tokens in-flight at once, easier to abort
2. Escalation flooding

20 loops escalate simultaneously → human overwhelmed
Can't fix all 20 at once
WIP=5 → human fixes 5, learns pattern, prevents next 15 from failing
3. Prompt/rubric iteration

Bad prompt → 20 loops fail → fix prompt → can't retry (already failed)
WIP=5 → 5 fail → fix prompt → remaining 15 use fixed prompt
4. Machine resource limits

Can't actually run 20 Claude Code instances simultaneously on laptop
WIP enforces realistic concurrency
Recommendation: Adaptive WIP Limits
Don't use fixed WIP. Use adaptive WIP based on failure rate:

rust
struct AdaptiveWIP {
    current_limit: usize,
    baseline: usize,        // User-configured (default: 5)

    // Auto-adjust based on metrics
    escalation_rate: f64,   // % loops escalating
    same_error_rate: f64,   // % loops failing with same error
}

impl AdaptiveWIP {
    fn adjust(&mut self) {
        // If >50% of recent loops escalate with same error → reduce WIP
        if self.same_error_rate > 0.5 {
            self.current_limit = max(1, self.current_limit / 2);
            // Force human to fix systemic issue before continuing
        }

        // If <10% escalation rate → increase WIP (things are working)
        if self.escalation_rate < 0.1 {
            self.current_limit = min(self.baseline * 2, self.current_limit + 1);
        }
    }
}
Behavior:

Start with WIP=5 (baseline)
3 loops escalate with "Coverage unreachable" → WIP drops to 2
User fixes rubric
Next 2 loops pass → WIP increases to 3, then 4, then 5
System learns "when to slow down"
Counterargument: Maybe Scope Doesn't Need WIP Limits?
Devil's advocate: What if we just rely on:

Resource limits (max 5 Claude Code instances)
Rate limit backoff (agent adapter handles retries)
User attention (they'll notice if 20 loops fail)
Why this might work:

Scope isn't manufacturing (no inventory waste)
Agents are stateless (no context switching)
User can abort anytime (not locked in)
Why this fails:

❌ Token blowout before user notices
❌ 20 escalations = overwhelming cognitive load
❌ Can't iterate on prompts/rubrics if all loops already running
Final Answer: WIP Limits in Scope
Implement WIP limits, but frame them differently:

Manufacturing WIP = prevent inventory buildup
Scope WIP = enable rapid iteration

Feature: "Batch Size" (not "WIP Limit")

yaml
# .scope/config.yaml
batch_size: 3  # Run loops in batches of 3

behavior:
  - Start first 3 loops
  - Wait until all 3 complete (pass or escalate)
  - If >1 escalation → pause, require human intervention
  - Human fixes issue (rubric, prompt, task)
  - Start next batch of 3
  - Repeat
```

**UI messaging:**
```
✓ Batch 1 complete (3/3 passed)
  Starting batch 2...

⚠ Batch 2 incomplete (2/3 escalated)
  Review failures before continuing.

  Common error: "Type error in auth.ts"
  Suggested fix: Update TypeScript config

  [Fix & Continue] [Adjust Batch Size]
This reframes WIP from "constraint" to "learning mechanism":

Not: "You can only run 3 loops" (feels limiting)
Instead: "Run 3, learn, adjust, run 3 more" (feels iterative)
Revised PRD Addition
Feature: Batch-Based Execution (High Priority)
Motivation:
Enable rapid iteration on prompts, rubrics, and task decomposition by running loops in small batches and surfacing failures early.

User story:
As a user, I want loops to run in batches so that I can fix systemic issues (bad rubric, bad prompt) before wasting tokens on 20 failed loops.

Design:

Default batch size: 3 loops
User configurable: 1-20
Auto-pause between batches if escalation rate >33%
Show batch completion status in UI
Suggest fixes based on common errors across batch
Non-goal:
Not about "limiting concurrency" (that's a resource concern). This is about structured learning cycles.

WIP Limits: The Human Context Switching Problem
The Real Bottleneck is Human Attention

9:00 AM - User spawns 15 loops across 3 tickets

Start fixing → loop 7 notification → context switch
Load loop 7 context (API tests) → 2 min
Start fixing → loop 11 notification → context switch
Total time: 30 min of context loading, 5 min of actual fixing
This is EXACTLY the manufacturing problem: Worker jumping between 10 stations loses productivity.

What Manufacturing Got Right (That Applies to Scope)
1. One-Piece Flow (Toyota's ideal)
Manufacturing:

Don't start car #2 until car #1 is complete
Forces immediate problem-solving
Worker stays in one context
Scope equivalent: WIP=1

yaml

  per_human: 1  # Only 1 escalation at a time
Flow:

Loop 1 starts
Loop 1 escalates
All other loops pause
Human fixes loop 1 (stays in context)
Loop 1 passes
Loop 2 starts
Repeat
Benefits:

Human never context switches
Each fix is deep (not shallow multitasking)
Pattern recognition: "Oh, 3 loops failed the same way" (because you fixed them sequentially, not in parallel)
2. Andon Cord (Stop the line)
Manufacturing:

Worker sees defect → pulls cord → entire assembly line stops
Forces everyone to focus on fixing the problem NOW
Don't accumulate defects
Scope equivalent:

yaml
andon_policy:
  stop_all_loops_if:
    - escalation_count >= 2
    - same_error_in_2_loops

  require_human_ack: true  # Can't resume until human says "fixed"
```

**UI:**
```

2 loops escalated with same error:
  "Type error: Cannot read property 'id' of undefined"

Affected loops:
  - Loop 3: Auth API
  - Loop 8: User profile

Root cause: Missing null check in User type

[I've Fixed This] [False Alarm - Resume]
Why this matters:

Human doesn't get 10 escalations (context switching hell)
Human gets 2 escalations → stops → fixes root cause → resumes
Remaining 8 loops benefit from the fix
3. Batch Size = Cognitive Load
Your original insight is correct:

WIP limits aren't about agent efficiency.
WIP limits are about human cognitive load management.

Cognitive science:

Humans can hold ~4 items in working memory
Context switching between >4 tasks → thrashing
Each context switch costs ~15 minutes (research: Gloria Mark, UC Irvine)
Scope WIP limits = bounded cognitive load

yaml
wip_limits:
  max_concurrent_escalations: 3  # Human can't handle more than 3 contexts
```

**When loop 4 escalates while 3 are already escalated:**
```
⚠ Cognitive load limit reached (3/3 escalations active)

New escalation: Loop 12 (Frontend component)
Action: Queued until you resolve an existing escalation

Currently escalated:
  1. Loop 3: Auth schema     [Resolve] [View]
  2. Loop 7: API tests       [Resolve] [View]
  3. Loop 11: Type error     [Resolve] [View]

💡 Tip: Fix similar issues together to minimize context switching
The "Maker's Schedule" Problem
Paul Graham's insight: Managers operate on 1-hour blocks. Makers (engineers) need 4-hour uninterrupted blocks.

Without WIP limits, Scope puts you on a "manager's schedule":

9:00 - Context switch to loop 3
9:15 - Notification: loop 7 escalated → context switch
9:30 - Notification: loop 11 escalated → context switch
9:45 - Notification: loop 2 escalated → context switch
Result: 4 context switches, 0 deep work
With WIP limits, Scope keeps you on a "maker's schedule":

9:00 - Loop 3 escalates
9:00-10:00 - Deep focus: fix loop 3 thoroughly
10:00 - Loop 3 resolved
10:05 - Loop 7 starts (was queued)
Result: 1 context, 1 hour of deep work
Revised WIP Limit Design (Human-Centric)
WIP Limit Types:
yaml
wip_limits:
  # Limit concurrent loops to prevent resource exhaustion
  max_running_loops: 10

  # Limit concurrent escalations to prevent human context switching
  max_concurrent_escalations: 3  # ← THIS IS THE KEY ONE

  # Limit concurrent loops per ticket (scoped context)
  max_per_ticket: 5
The Critical Insight:
It's not about limiting running loops.
It's about limiting ESCALATED loops (the ones demanding human attention).

Asymmetry:

20 loops running in background? Fine. (Agents don't care)
20 loops escalated, demanding attention? Cognitive overload. (Humans can't handle it)
New rule:

rust
// Loops can run freely
max_running_loops: unlimited

// But escalations are bounded
max_concurrent_escalations: 3

// When 4th escalation occurs:
if escalated_loops.len() >= 3 {
    queue_escalation(loop_id);
    pause_new_loops();  // Don't start more work that might escalate
}

9:00 - Spawn 12 loops

      → User loads context, starts investigating
9:07 - Loop 5 escalates (API)
      → Notification interrupts user
      → User tabs over to loop 5
      → Loses schema context
9:09 - Loop 8 escalates (tests)
      → Another notification
      → User now has 3 tabs open
9:11 - Loop 11 escalates (types)
      → User overwhelmed, doesn't know which to prioritize
9:30 - User has spent 25 minutes context switching
      → Fixed nothing deeply
      → All 4 escalations still unresolved
```

**Cognitive cost:** 4 context switches × 15 min = 60 min lost

---

### With WIP Limits (Flow)

**Timeline:**
```
9:00 - Spawn 12 loops
      ├─ 3 start immediately (WIP limit)
      └─ 9 queued

9:05 - Loop 2 escalates (schema)
      → All new loops pause (andon policy)
      → User sees: "1/3 escalations, all others paused"
      → User focuses on loop 2 only

9:20 - User resolves loop 2
      → Loop 2 resumes, passes
      → Next queued loop (loop 4) starts

9:25 - Loop 5 escalates (API)
      → User sees: "1/3 escalations"
      → User focuses on loop 5

9:40 - User resolves loop 5
      → Loop 5 resumes, passes
      → Next queued loop (loop 6) starts
```

**Cognitive cost:** 0 context switches. Deep focus on one problem at a time.

**Throughput:**
- Without limits: 4 escalations unresolved after 30 min
- With limits: 2 escalations resolved in 35 min

**Net: Slower starts, faster finishes.**

## The Paradox: Constraints Increase Throughput

**Naive thinking:** "More parallelism = faster"
- Spawn 20 loops → faster than spawning 3, right?

**Reality:** "More parallelism = more context switching = slower"
- Spawn 20 loops → 10 escalate → human thrashes → nothing gets fixed
- Spawn 3 loops → 1 escalates → human fixes it → 2 pass, next 3 start

**Little's Law (queueing theory):**
```
Throughput = WIP / Lead Time

But lead time increases with WIP (due to context switching)

So: Lower WIP → Lower lead time → Same or higher throughput
Empirical data from software teams:

Team with WIP=2 per person: 8 stories/week completed
Team with WIP=5 per person: 6 stories/week completed
Lower WIP = higher throughput (because less context switching)
Source: David Anderson, "Kanban: Successful Evolutionary Change"

Final Recommendation
Implement 3 Types of WIP Limits:
yaml
wip_limits:
  # 1. Resource limit (prevent machine overload)
  max_running_loops: 10

  # 2. Cognitive limit (prevent human context switching)
  max_concurrent_escalations: 3  # ← MOST IMPORTANT

  # 3. Scope limit (keep ticket-level context bounded)
  max_per_ticket: 5
Default Behavior:
Optimistic start:

First ticket: spawn all loops (no artificial slowdown)
Adaptive throttle:

If escalation_rate > 30% → enable WIP limits
If escalation_rate < 10% → relax WIP limits
Andon cord:

2+ escalations with same error → pause all loops
Require human acknowledgment to resume

Not a goal:
Limit running loops (agents can handle 20 concurrent loops fine). Only limit escalations (human attention).

```
## Alternative: Not WIP Limits, but "Circuit Breakers"

**Manufacturing WIP limits:**
- Purpose: Smooth flow, prevent inventory buildup
- Mechanism: Hard cap on concurrent work
- Behavior: Work queues when limit hit

**Scope doesn't need WIP limits. Scope needs circuit breakers.**

---

## Circuit Breaker Pattern

**From distributed systems (Michael Nygard, "Release It!"):**
```
Circuit states:
├─ Closed: Normal operation, requests flow through
├─ Open: Too many failures, stop sending requests
└─ Half-open: Try one request, see if it works

Transitions:
├─ Closed → Open: Failure rate > threshold
├─ Open → Half-open: After timeout
└─ Half-open → Closed: Success
Applied to Scope:

rust
struct LoopCircuitBreaker {
    state: CircuitState,
    failure_count: usize,
    threshold: usize,  // Open circuit after N failures
}

enum CircuitState {
    Closed,      // Spawn loops normally
    Open,        // Stop spawning, show user the pattern
    HalfOpen,    // User fixed issue, try 1 loop
}

impl LoopCircuitBreaker {
    fn should_start_loop(&mut self) -> bool {
        match self.state {
            Closed => true,
            Open => false,
            HalfOpen => {
                // Try one loop to test if fix worked
                self.state = Closed;
                true
            }
        }
    }

    fn on_escalation(&mut self) {
        self.failure_count += 1;

        if self.failure_count >= self.threshold {
            self.state = Open;
            // Trigger: "Pattern detected, pausing loops"
        }
    }

    fn on_user_fix(&mut self) {
        self.state = HalfOpen;
        self.failure_count = 0;
    }
}
```

**Behavior:**
```
9:00 - Spawn 20 loops (circuit: Closed)
9:05 - Loop 1 escalates (circuit: failure_count=1)
9:07 - Loop 2 escalates (circuit: failure_count=2)
9:09 - Loop 3 escalates (circuit: failure_count=3, threshold reached)

      🔴 Circuit breaker opened

      Detected pattern: 3 consecutive escalations
      Common error: "Type error in auth.ts"

      Paused: 17 remaining loops

      [I've Fixed This] [Ignore Pattern]

User clicks "I've Fixed This"

      🟡 Circuit breaker: half-open

      Testing fix with 1 loop...

9:15 - Loop 4 runs with fix, passes

      🟢 Circuit breaker: closed

      Fix confirmed. Resuming 16 remaining loops.
```

---

## This Is Different From WIP Limits

**WIP limit:**
- "Only 3 loops at a time" (always enforced)
- Purpose: Smooth flow
- Metric: Count of concurrent work

**Circuit breaker:**
- "Stop spawning if pattern of failures detected"
- Purpose: Fast failure detection
- Metric: Failure rate

**Key difference:**
```
WIP limit:
├─ Running: 3/3 loops
├─ All 3 pass
├─ Still can't start loop 4 (WIP limit)
└─ Must wait for one to finish

Circuit breaker:
├─ Running: 20 loops
├─ All 20 pass
├─ Can spawn loop 21 (circuit closed)
└─ No artificial constraint
Circuit breaker only activates on failures, not successes.

What About Human Context Switching?
You mentioned "passive viewership" — so running loops don't cause context switching.

But escalations still do.

So we need:

1. Circuit Breaker (prevent failure cascades)
Stop spawning loops after N escalations
Give user chance to fix systemic issue
Resume when user confirms fix
2. Escalation Queue (manage human attention)
Limit concurrent escalations visible to user
Not a WIP limit (doesn't affect running loops)
Just a UI filter: "Show me 3 escalations at a time"
Escalation Queue ≠ WIP Limit
WIP limit:

Constrains concurrent work
Affects system throughput
"Can't start loop 4 until loop 1 finishes"
Escalation queue:

Constrains concurrent visibility
Doesn't affect system (loops still run)
"I'll show you 3 escalations, rest are hidden until you resolve one"
Implementation:

rust
struct EscalationQueue {
    active: Vec<LoopId>,      // Visible to user
    queued: Vec<LoopId>,      // Hidden until active resolved
    max_active: usize,        // Default: 3
}

impl EscalationQueue {
    fn on_escalation(&mut self, loop_id: LoopId) {
        if self.active.len() < self.max_active {
            self.active.push(loop_id);
            ui.show_escalation(loop_id);
        } else {
            self.queued.push(loop_id);
            // Don't show to user yet
        }
    }

    fn on_resolved(&mut self, loop_id: LoopId) {
        self.active.remove(loop_id);

        if let Some(next) = self.queued.pop_front() {
            self.active.push(next);
            ui.show_escalation(next);
        }
    }
}
```

**This doesn't stop loops from running.**
**It just manages what the user sees.**
```

Revised Mental Model
Scope Has Three Independent Concerns:
1. Loop Execution Concurrency (physical limit)

rust
max_running_loops: auto_detect()  // Based on CPU/memory
Not a WIP limit, just a resource constraint
Queues loops when machine is overloaded
User doesn't configure this (it's automatic)
2. Circuit Breaker (failure detection)

rust
circuit_breaker:
  open_after: 3  // Consecutive escalations
  behavior: pause_new_loops
Detects patterns of failures
Pauses loop spawning until user fixes
Resumes after user confirms fix
3. Escalation Visibility (human attention)

rust
escalation_queue:
  max_visible: 3  // Show max 3 escalations
  behavior: queue_rest
Doesn't stop loops from running
Just manages what user sees
Prevents "notification bombardment"
None of These Are Traditional WIP Limits
Traditional Kanban WIP:

Purpose: Smooth flow, prevent inventory
Mechanism: Hard cap on concurrent work
Applies to: All work (pass or fail)
Scope's mechanisms:

Circuit breaker: Only triggers on failures
Escalation queue: Only affects visibility
Resource limit: Physical constraint, not policy
The closest analogy is circuit breaker, not WIP limit.

PRD Update (Revised)
Feature: Circuit Breaker for Loop Failures (High Priority)
Motivation:
Detect patterns of failures early and pause loop execution to allow user to fix systemic issues (bad rubrics, bad prompts).

User story:
As a user, I want Scope to stop spawning loops when it detects a pattern of failures, so I can fix the root cause before wasting more attempts.

Design:

Threshold: 3 consecutive escalations → open circuit
Behavior when open:
Pause spawning new loops
Show pattern to user (common error, affected loops)
Suggest potential fixes
Recovery:
User clicks "I've fixed this"
Circuit → half-open
Test with 1 loop
If passes → circuit closed, resume all
If fails → circuit stays open
Success metric:
Fewer wasted attempts when there's a systemic issue.

Feature: Escalation Queue (Medium Priority)
Motivation:
Prevent human context switching by limiting visible escalations.

User story:
As a user, I want to see 3 escalations at a time so I can focus without being overwhelmed.

Design:

Default: Show 3 escalations max
Queued escalations: Hidden but visible count: "4 more queued"
Behavior: When user resolves one, next queued becomes visible
Override: User can "Show All" if they want
Success metric:
User reports less overwhelm, faster resolution per escalation.

Feature: Resource-Based Loop Limiting (Low Priority)
Motivation:
Prevent machine overload.

User story:
As a user, I don't want Scope to crash my laptop by spawning 100 agents.

Design:

Auto-detect: Monitor CPU, memory, disk
Adaptive limit: Start 10 loops max, adjust based on available resources
Queue: When limit hit, queue new loops
Transparent: Show "10/10 running, 5 queued"
Success metric:
No user-reported crashes or slowdowns.

Token limits don't apply (cost isn't a constraint)
Therefore:

❌ No traditional WIP limits (don't constrain running loops)
✅ Circuit breaker (pause on failure patterns)
✅ Escalation queue (limit visible escalations)
✅ Resource limit (auto-detect physical constraints)

✅ Human attention/energy
The Real Problem
Humans have limited attention budget per day.

From research (Daniel Kahneman, "Thinking Fast and Slow"):

~4 hours of deep cognitive work per day max
Each decision depletes "ego depletion" energy
Context switching drains attention faster than focused work
In Scope context:

Scenario: User has 4 hours of attention budget for the day

Without management:
├─ 9:00 - Spawn 20 loops
├─ 9:15 - 5 escalations hit simultaneously
├─ 9:15-10:00 - User frantically context switches between 5 problems
│   ├─ 15 min per escalation (shallow fixes)
│   ├─ Attention budget burned: 2 hours
│   └─ Actual work done: 45 minutes (rest was context switching)
├─ 10:00 - 3 more escalations arrive
├─ 10:00-11:00 - User continues thrashing
│   └─ Attention budget burned: another 1.5 hours
├─ 11:00 - User exhausted, 3.5 hours burned, only resolved 6 escalations
└─ Remaining 2 hours: Low-quality work (attention depleted)

With attention management:
├─ 9:00 - Spawn 20 loops
├─ 9:15 - Escalation 1 arrives
│   └─ UI shows ONLY escalation 1
├─ 9:15-9:30 - User fixes escalation 1 (deep focus, no distractions)
│   └─ Attention budget spent: 15 min
├─ 9:30 - Escalation 2 becomes visible (1 was resolved)
├─ 9:30-9:45 - User fixes escalation 2
│   └─ Attention budget spent: 15 min
├─ Pattern emerges: both had same root cause
├─ 9:45 - Circuit breaker: "2 similar errors, pause loops"
├─ 9:45-10:00 - User fixes root cause (systemic fix)
│   └─ Attention budget spent: 15 min
├─ 10:00 - Resume loops, remaining 17 pass without escalation
├─ 11:00 - All done, attention budget spent: 45 min
└─ Remaining 3+ hours: Available for other work
Attention budget saved: 2.5 hours

Two Mechanisms to Manage Human Attention
1. Escalation Queue (Prevent Bombardment)
Purpose: Serialize attention demands

Mechanism:

rust
struct EscalationQueue {
    visible: Vec<LoopId>,     // Show these to user
    hidden: Vec<LoopId>,      // Don't show yet
    max_visible: usize,       // Default: 1 (!!)
}
```

**Why max_visible=1 by default?**

Because **"one-piece flow" from manufacturing applies directly:**

- Manufacturer: Don't start car #2 until car #1 is done
- Scope: Don't show escalation #2 until escalation #1 is resolved

**Benefits:**
- Zero context switching
- Deep focus on each problem
- Pattern recognition (you fix 3 similar issues sequentially → "oh, same root cause")

**UI:**
```
┌─ Current Escalation ──────────────────────────┐
│                                               │
│ Loop 7: API endpoint tests failing            │
│                                               │
│ Error: Type error in auth.ts:42               │
│ Expected User, got User | undefined           │
│                                               │
│ [View Code] [Attach Terminal] [Skip]         │
│                                               │
│ ──────────────────────────────────────────── │
│                                               │
│ 4 more escalations queued                    │
│ (will show after you resolve this one)       │
│                                               │
│ ⚠ Show all? This will split your attention   │
│   [Keep Focus] [Show All Anyway]             │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
User can override:

Click "Show All Anyway" → see all 5 escalations
Settings: max_visible: 3 (if they prefer small batches)
Settings: max_visible: 999 (if they want traditional "see everything" mode)
But default is 1 = maximum attention preservation.

2. Circuit Breaker (Prevent Repeated Failure)
Purpose: Don't waste attention on repetitive failures

Mechanism:

rust
struct CircuitBreaker {
    state: CircuitState,
    failures: Vec<Escalation>,
    similarity_threshold: f64,  // How similar are errors?
}

impl CircuitBreaker {
    fn analyze_pattern(&self) -> Option<Pattern> {
        // If last 3 escalations have >80% similar error messages
        if self.detect_similar_errors() {
            Some(Pattern {
                common_error: "Type error in auth.ts",
                affected_loops: [3, 7, 11],
                root_cause_guess: "Missing null check in User type",
            })
        }
    }

    fn should_pause(&self) -> bool {
        self.analyze_pattern().is_some()
    }
}
```

**Behavior:**
```
9:00 - Loop 3 escalates: "Type error in auth.ts:42"
       └─ User fixes loop 3 specifically

9:15 - Loop 7 escalates: "Type error in auth.ts:42" (same error!)
       └─ User fixes loop 7 specifically

9:30 - Loop 11 escalates: "Type error in auth.ts:42" (same error again!!)

       🔴 Circuit breaker opens

       ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
       │ Pattern detected: 3 similar failures   │
       ├─────────────────────────────────────────┤
       │ Common error:                           │
       │ "Type error in auth.ts:42"              │
       │                                         │
       │ Affected loops: 3, 7, 11                │
       │                                         │
       │ 💡 Root cause (guess):                  │
       │ Missing null check in User type         │
       │                                         │
       │ You've fixed this 3 times locally.      │
       │ Fix it once at the source?              │
       │                                         │
       │ Paused: 9 remaining loops               │
       │ (They'll likely hit the same error)     │
       │                                         │
       │ [Fix Root Cause] [Resume Anyway]       │
       └─────────────────────────────────────────┘

User clicks "Fix Root Cause"
       └─ Opens auth.ts:42 in editor
       └─ User adds null check to User type
       └─ Clicks "Done"

       🟡 Testing fix...

       Loop 14 runs with fix → passes ✓

       🟢 Circuit closed

       Fix confirmed. Resuming 8 remaining loops.
```

**This prevents:**
- User fixing same issue 12 times (attention waste)
- User not noticing pattern (attention blindness under load)

---

## The Deeper Insight: Interruption Cost

**Research (Gloria Mark, UC Irvine):**
- Average time to resume task after interruption: **23 minutes**
- Not because the task is hard, but because:
  1. Working memory was cleared (15 min to reload context)
  2. You forgot where you were (5 min to re-orient)
  3. You dealt with the interruption (3 min)

**In Scope:**
```
User is fixing escalation 1 (auth schema)
├─ 5 minutes in, deeply focused
├─ Working memory: schema constraints, migration history, API contracts
└─ Notification: "Escalation 2 arrived (API tests)"
   └─ User thinks: "Should I context switch?"
   └─ Even if user ignores it, attention is disrupted
   └─ Cost: 5-10 min to regain deep focus
```

**With escalation queue (max_visible=1):**
```
User is fixing escalation 1
├─ 5 minutes in, deeply focused
├─ Escalation 2 arrives → queued, NOT shown
├─ No notification, no disruption
└─ User continues in deep focus → resolves in 10 min total
Attention saved: 5-10 min per interruption

Kanban Finally Makes Sense for Scope
I was wrong earlier to question WIP limits.
You were right: they apply because humans are in the loop.

But it's not traditional WIP (concurrent work).
It's attention WIP (concurrent attention demands).

Renaming for Clarity:
Don't call it "WIP limit"
Call it "Attention Limit"

yaml
# .scope/config.yaml
attention_management:
  max_visible_escalations: 1  # How many problems to show at once

  circuit_breaker:
    enabled: true
    threshold: 3              # Pause after 3 similar failures

  focus_mode:
    default: true             # Hide everything except current escalation
    allow_override: true      # User can disable if they want chaos
```

## The UI is Now a Lens, Not a Dashboard

**Old mental model (wrong):**
- UI = show everything happening
- User monitors 20 loops like a control tower
- User clicks into problems as they arise

**New mental model (correct):**
- UI = attention filter
- Shows user ONLY what needs attention RIGHT NOW
- Everything else is hidden (but still running)
- User works in "flow state" (zero context switching)

**UI design:**

### Main View (Focus Mode ON):
```
┌─ Scope ───────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                               │
│ Current task:                                 │
│                                               │
│ ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│ │ Loop 7: Fix API endpoint auth             │ │
│ │                                           │ │
│ │ Error: Type error in auth.ts:42           │ │
│ │ Expected User, got User | undefined       │ │
│ │                                           │ │
│ │ [View Code] [Terminal] [Skip]            │ │
│ └───────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
│                                               │
│ 19 other loops running in background         │
│ 4 escalations queued                         │
│                                               │
│ [Show Background Activity]                   │
│                                               │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### Background Activity (Collapsed by Default):
```
┌─ Background Activity ─────────────────────────┐
│                                               │
│ Running (15):                                 │
│ ├─ Loop 1: Schema migration      ●           │
│ ├─ Loop 2: API endpoints          ●          │
│ ├─ ...                                        │
│                                               │
│ Passed (4):                                   │
│ ├─ Loop 12: Tests                 ✓          │
│ ├─ ...                                        │
│                                               │
│ Queued escalations (4):                       │
│ ├─ Loop 14: Integration           ⚠          │
│ ├─ Loop 18: E2E                   ⚠          │
│ └─ ...                                        │
│                                               │
│ (These will appear one at a time after       │
│  you resolve the current escalation)         │
│                                               │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Key: You have to click to see background activity.**
**Default view = only what needs your attention RIGHT NOW.**

**New dashboard element:**
```

┌─ Your Attention Today ────────────────────────┐
│                                               │

│                                               │

│ ├─ Resolving escalations:   1.5 hrs          │
│ ├─ Reviewing loops:          0.5 hrs         │
│ └─ Context switching:        0.2 hrs (⚠)    │

This gamifies attention preservation:

User sees: "I spent 0.2 hrs context switching today"
User enables focus mode
Next day: "I spent 0.05 hrs context switching" ✓
Behavioral nudge toward sustainable work
PRD Update (Final)
Feature: Attention Management System (Highest Priority)
Motivation:
Human attention is the scarce resource. Protect it by serializing attention demands and preventing repetitive failures.

Core principle:
One problem at a time. No notifications. No context switching.

Feature 1: Escalation Queue (Serialize Attention)
Default behavior:

Show 1 escalation at a time (max_visible=1)
Queue all others (visible count: "4 more queued")
No notifications for queued escalations
Next escalation appears only after current is resolved
User story:
As a user, I want to see one problem at a time so I can focus without context switching.

Settings:

yaml
escalation_queue:
  max_visible: 1              # Default: one-piece flow
  allow_override: true        # User can show all if needed
  notification_mode: none     # Don't interrupt with notifications
Success metric:

Time to resolve per escalation decreases (due to focus)
User-reported: "I can think deeply about each problem"
Feature 2: Circuit Breaker (Prevent Repetitive Failures)
Default behavior:

After 3 similar escalations → pause loop spawning
Show pattern to user: "You've fixed this 3 times, fix root cause?"
Test fix with 1 loop before resuming all
User story:
As a user, I don't want to fix the same issue 10 times when I could fix it once at the source.

Settings:

yaml
circuit_breaker:
  enabled: true
  threshold: 3                # Pause after 3 similar failures
  similarity: 0.8             # 80% error message similarity
  behavior: pause_new_loops   # Stop spawning, don't stop running
Success metric:

Fewer escalations with same root cause
User-reported: "Scope helped me notice the pattern"
Feature 3: Focus Mode (Hide Everything Else)
Default behavior:

Current escalation fills screen
All other UI elements collapsed by default
Must click "Show Background Activity" to see running loops
User story:
As a user, I want zero distractions while fixing an escalation.

Settings:

yaml
focus_mode:
  default: true               # On by default
  hide_background: true       # Collapse running loops, passed loops
  show_only_current: true     # Current escalation only
Success metric:

User engagement time per escalation (longer = deeper focus)
User-reported: "I forgot everything else was running"
Feature 4: Attention Budget Tracking (Optional, Gamification)
Show user:

Hours spent today on Scope work
Breakdown: resolving vs. context switching vs. reviewing
Suggestions: "Enable circuit breaker" when repetitive work detected
User story:
As a user, I want to see how my attention is being spent so I can optimize my workflow.

Success metric:

Users enable focus mode after seeing attention waste
Context switching time decreases over time
Summary
The core insight:

Scope isn't optimizing for machine efficiency
Scope is optimizing for human sustainability
The mechanisms:

Escalation queue = Serialize attention demands (one at a time)
Circuit breaker = Prevent repetitive attention waste (fix root cause)
Focus mode = Zero distractions (current task fills screen)
Attention tracking = Make waste visible (gamify optimization)
The outcome:

User can work 4 hours productively instead of burning out in 90 minutes
Sustainable throughput instead of sprint-and-crash
Anti-vampire principle applied to human attention
This is Lean/Kanban's real value: preventing burnout through bounded attention.

Is this the right framing now?

To me this is another view, issue tracker, dag viewer, loop viewer, monitor view (escalations + running loop view)

Feb 14
Ah! You're designing a multi-view UI, not a single "focus mode" interface.

Scope Has 5 Primary Views
Let me map them out based on what you said:

1. Issue Tracker View (Linear-style)
Purpose: Manage tickets/tasks at the project level

┌─ Issues ──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                            │
│ Search: [____________]  Filter: [All ▼]  Sort: [Status ▼] │
│                                                            │
│ ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
│ │ ID   │ Title              │ Status      │ Loops │ Time ││
│ ├──────┼────────────────────┼─────────────┼───────┼──────┤│
│ │ #142 │ Add authentication │ In Progress │ 4/7   │ 18m  ││
│ │ #141 │ Fix payment API    │ Blocked     │ 2/5   │ 45m  ││
│ │ #140 │ Update docs        │ Done        │ 3/3   │ 12m  ││
│ │ #139 │ Refactor schema    │ Open        │ 0/4   │ -    ││
│ └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
│                                                            │
│ [+ New Issue]                                              │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
User actions:

Create new issues
Assign to human/agent
Change priority
Click issue → opens DAG Viewer
Attention management here:

Filter by "Needs Attention" (has escalations)
Sort by "Escalation Count" (most urgent first)
Badge: "⚠ 3" on issues with escalations
2. DAG Viewer (GitHub Actions-style)
Purpose: See the decomposition and dependencies for a single ticket

┌─ Issue #142: Add Authentication ──────────────────────────┐
│                                                            │
│ Status: In Progress (4/7 loops complete)                  │
│                                                            │
│      ┌─────────┐                                          │
│      │ Schema  │ ✓                                        │
│      └────┬────┘                                          │
│           │                                               │
│      ┌────┴────┬────────────┐                            │
│      │         │            │                            │
│  ┌───▼───┐ ┌──▼──┐    ┌───▼────┐                        │
│  │  API  │ │Tests│    │Middleware│ ●                     │
│  └───┬───┘ └──┬──┘    └───┬────┘                        │
│      │         │            │                            │
│      └────┬────┴────────────┘                            │
│           │                                               │
│      ┌────▼─────┐                                        │
│      │Integration│ ⚠ (blocked)                           │
│      └──────────┘                                        │
│                                                            │
│ Legend: ✓ Passed  ● Running  ⚠ Escalated  ○ Pending      │
│                                                            │
│ [View All Loops] [Monitor View]                           │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
User actions:

Click node → opens Loop Viewer
See topology (dependencies)
Understand blocking relationships
Quick status overview
Attention management here:

Highlight escalated nodes in red/orange
Show "⚠ 2 escalations blocking 4 downstream loops"
Click "Monitor View" → jumps to escalations
3. Loop Viewer (Deep dive on single loop)
Purpose: Inspect what a specific loop is doing/did

┌─ Loop 3: API Endpoint Implementation ─────────────────────┐
│                                                            │
│ Status: Running (Attempt 2/5)                             │
│ Started: 2m ago                                           │
│                                                            │
│ ┌─ Terminal ─────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
│ │ $ claude-code                                          ││
│ │ Maker: Implementing POST /auth/login endpoint...       ││
│ │ Maker: Added rate limiting middleware                  ││
│ │ Maker: Writing integration tests...                    ││
│ │ ▊                                                      ││
│ └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
│                                                            │
│ ┌─ Diff ─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
│ │ src/routes/auth.ts                                     ││
│ │ + router.post('/login', rateLimiter, async (req) => { ││
│ │ +   const { email, password } = req.body;             ││
│ │ +   // ... implementation                             ││
│ │                                                        ││
│ │ [Expand full diff]                                    ││
│ └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
│                                                            │
│ ┌─ Checker Status ───────────────────────────────────────┐│
│ │ Waiting for maker to complete...                      ││
│ └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
│                                                            │
│ ┌─ History ──────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
│ │ Attempt 1: Failed (checker rejected - missing tests)  ││
│ │ Attempt 2: In progress...                             ││
│ └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
│                                                            │
│ [Attach Terminal] [Edit Rubric] [Cancel Loop]             │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
User actions:

Watch live progress (passive viewing)
Review diff
Attach to terminal (take over)
See attempt history
Edit rubric mid-flight
Attention management here:

None needed (user intentionally dove deep)
This is where user goes when they WANT to monitor
4. Monitor View (Mission Control)
Purpose: Active monitoring of all running + escalated loops

This is where attention management happens.

┌─ Monitor ──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                            │

This is where attention management features live:

Escalations section:

Default: Show 1 escalation (max_visible=1)
User can click "Show Next" or "Show All"
No auto-notifications (user pulls, not pushed)
Running section:

Collapsed by default (just count: "12 running")
User can expand to see details
Click any → jump to Loop Viewer
Circuit breaker:

Shows at bottom when triggered
Click "Review Pattern" → shows analysis
Key principle:
This view is pull-based, not push-based.
User comes here when they want to check status, not bombarded with notifications.

Attention Management: Where It Lives
Now I understand: Attention management isn't the whole UI.
It's just ONE VIEW (Monitor or Escalations).

Other views:

Issue Tracker: Project management (planning)
DAG Viewer: Understanding topology (strategy)
Loop Viewer: Deep inspection (debugging)
Monitor/Escalations View: Attention management (operations)

The key insight:

User can freely browse Issues → DAG → Loop (passive exploration)
BUT when escalations occur, they go to Monitor/Escalations View (active work)

Settings: Per-View Configuration
yaml
# .scope/ui-config.yaml

views:
  issue_tracker:
    default_filter: needs_attention
    sort_by: escalation_count

  dag_viewer:
    layout: horizontal  # or vertical, radial
    collapse_passed: true
    highlight_escalated: true

  loop_viewer:
    default_tab: terminal  # or diff, rubric, history
    auto_scroll: true

  monitor:
    escalations:
      max_visible: 1
      show_queue: true
      notification_mode: none

    running:
      collapsed_by_default: true
      show_count: true

    circuit_breaker:
      show_when_triggered: true

  escalations:
    max_visible: 1
    focus_mode: true
    pattern_detection: true
```

---

## Revised PRD: Multi-View UI

### Feature: Five Primary Views

**1. Issue Tracker View**
- Purpose: Project-level ticket management
- Inspiration: Linear
- Key feature: Filter by "Needs Attention"

**2. DAG Viewer**
- Purpose: Understand task decomposition and dependencies
- Inspiration: GitHub Actions workflow graph
- Key feature: Click node → Loop Viewer

**3. Loop Viewer**
- Purpose: Deep inspection of single loop
- Tabs: Terminal, Diff, Rubric, History, Attempts
- Key feature: Live PTY, attach terminal

**4. Monitor View**
- Purpose: Mission control for all active work
- Sections:
  - Needs Attention (escalations, max_visible=1)
  - Running (collapsed by default)
  - Recently Completed
  - Circuit Breaker alerts
- Key feature: Pull-based (no push notifications)

**5. Escalations View** (Alternative to Monitor)
- Purpose: Dedicated escalation queue management
- Shows: 1 active, rest queued
- Key feature: Focus mode (show only current)
- Pattern detection inline

---

## Navigation Flow

**Typical user workflow:**

### Morning: Check status
```
User opens Scope
    ↓
Lands on Issue Tracker
    ↓
Sees: "Issue #142 has 2 escalations ⚠"
    ↓
Clicks issue → DAG Viewer
    ↓
Sees: "Loop 7 escalated" (red node)
    ↓
Clicks "Monitor View" button
    ↓
Escalations View shows Loop 7
    ↓
User fixes escalation
    ↓
Loop 7 resolves, next escalation (Loop 11) appears
```

### During work: Passive monitoring
```
User is coding
    ↓
Occasionally switches to Scope
    ↓
Glances at Monitor View
    ↓
Sees: "15 running, 0 escalations" ✓
    ↓
Returns to coding
```

### When escalation occurs:
```
User checks Monitor View
    ↓
Sees: "1 escalation" in Needs Attention
    ↓
Clicks [Fix Now]
    ↓
Loop Viewer opens (or inline quick fix)
    ↓
User resolves, returns to Monitor
Where Does Attention Management Live?
Only in Monitor/Escalations View:

yaml
attention_management:
  views: [monitor, escalations]  # Only these views

  escalation_queue:
    max_visible: 1
    show_next_button: true
    show_all_button: true  # Override available

  circuit_breaker:
    show_alerts: true
    location: monitor_view_bottom

  focus_mode:
    default: true
    hide_sidebar: false  # Can still switch views
    emphasize_current: true
```

**Other views (Issue Tracker, DAG, Loop Viewer):**
- No attention management
- User browses freely
- No queuing, no filtering, no hiding
```

Integration with wedow/ticket

Fetched: GitHub - wedow/ticket
Perfect. wedow/ticket is a git-backed, CLI issue tracker with:

Markdown files in .tickets/
YAML frontmatter
Dependency tracking
Simple commands (tk create, tk dep, tk ready, tk blocked)
How Scope Integrates with tk
Core Principle: tk Owns Tickets, Scope Owns Loops
tk (ticket system)           Scope (loop orchestrator)
├─ Tickets (.tickets/*.md)   ├─ Loops (.scope/loops/*.json)
├─ Dependencies              ├─ DAG execution
└─ Status tracking           └─ Maker/checker agents

Integration point:
  Ticket → spawns loops
  Loops → update ticket status
Data Model
Ticket (managed by tk)
yaml
# .tickets/nw-5c46.md
---
id: nw-5c46
title: Add user authentication
type: feature
status: in_progress
priority: 2
assignee: alice
dependencies: []
external_ref: null
created: 2026-02-14T09:00:00Z
updated: 2026-02-14T10:30:00Z
---

# Description
Implement JWT-based authentication for API endpoints.

# Design
- Use JWT tokens
- Store in httpOnly cookies
- Refresh token rotation

# Acceptance Criteria
- [ ] POST /auth/login works
- [ ] POST /auth/refresh works
- [ ] Middleware validates tokens
- [ ] Tests cover edge cases
Scope Metadata (managed by Scope)
yaml
# .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
ticket_id: nw-5c46
loops:
  - id: loop-1
    task: "Implement schema for users table"
    status: passed
    commit_sha: abc123

  - id: loop-2
    task: "Implement POST /auth/login endpoint"
    status: in_progress
    attempts: 2

  - id: loop-3
    task: "Write integration tests for auth"
    status: pending
    depends_on: [loop-2]

  - id: loop-4
    task: "Add auth middleware"
    status: pending
    depends_on: [loop-2]

dag:
  nodes: [loop-1, loop-2, loop-3, loop-4]
  edges:
    - [loop-1, loop-2]
    - [loop-2, loop-3]
    - [loop-2, loop-4]

created_at: 2026-02-14T09:05:00Z
updated_at: 2026-02-14T10:30:00Z
Integration Points
1. Ticket Creation → Loop Decomposition
User workflow:

bash
# User creates ticket with tk
$ tk create "Add user authentication" -t feature

Created: nw-5c46

# User tells Scope to work on ticket
$ scope start nw-5c46

Analyzing ticket nw-5c46...
Decomposing into loops...

DAG created:
├─ loop-1: Implement schema
├─ loop-2: Implement login endpoint (depends on loop-1)
├─ loop-3: Write tests (depends on loop-2)
└─ loop-4: Add middleware (depends on loop-2)

Starting loops...
What Scope does:

Read .tickets/nw-5c46.md
Decomposer agent analyzes description, design, acceptance criteria
Generate DAG of loops
Save to .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
Start first loop(s)
2. Loop Progress → Ticket Status
Auto-update ticket status:

rust
impl ScopeOrchestrator {
    async fn on_loop_passed(&mut self, loop_id: LoopId) {
        let ticket_id = self.get_ticket_for_loop(loop_id);
        let all_loops = self.get_loops_for_ticket(ticket_id);

        // Check if all loops are done
        if all_loops.iter().all(|l| l.status == LoopStatus::Passed) {
            // Update tk ticket status
            Command::new("tk")
                .args(&["close", &ticket_id])
                .output()?;
        }
    }
}
Scope automatically:

tk start <id> when first loop starts
tk close <id> when all loops pass
(Optional) Update ticket description with progress
3. Ticket Dependencies → Loop Dependencies
User sets ticket dependencies:

bash
$ tk create "Implement API endpoints" -t feature
Created: nw-a1b2

$ tk create "Write E2E tests" -t task
Created: nw-c3d4

$ tk dep nw-c3d4 nw-a1b2
# c3d4 depends on a1b2
```

**Scope respects ticket dependencies:**
```
Ticket nw-a1b2 (API endpoints)
├─ loop-1: Schema
├─ loop-2: Endpoints
└─ loop-3: Tests

Ticket nw-c3d4 (E2E tests)
├─ loop-4: Setup E2E framework
└─ loop-5: Write E2E tests
    └─ BLOCKED until nw-a1b2 closes

# Scope won't start loop-4 or loop-5 until all loops in nw-a1b2 pass
Implementation:

rust
impl ScopeOrchestrator {
    fn can_start_loop(&self, loop_id: LoopId) -> bool {
        let ticket_id = self.get_ticket_for_loop(loop_id);
        let ticket_deps = self.get_ticket_dependencies(ticket_id); // Read from tk

        // Check if all dependency tickets are closed
        ticket_deps.iter().all(|dep_ticket_id| {
            self.get_ticket_status(dep_ticket_id) == TicketStatus::Closed
        })
    }

    fn get_ticket_dependencies(&self, ticket_id: &str) -> Vec<String> {
        // Parse .tickets/{ticket_id}.md YAML frontmatter
        let ticket = self.read_ticket(ticket_id);
        ticket.dependencies
    }
}
4. Ticket Queries → Scope UI
tk provides queries, Scope uses them:

bash
# tk command
$ tk ready
nw-5c46  Add user authentication
nw-7e8f  Fix payment API

# Scope uses this internally
$ tk query '.[] | select(.status != "closed")'
[
  {
    "id": "nw-5c46",
    "title": "Add user authentication",
    "status": "in_progress",
    "dependencies": []
  },
  ...
]
Scope's Issue Tracker View:

rust
// Fetch tickets from tk
fn fetch_tickets(&self) -> Vec<Ticket> {
    let output = Command::new("tk")
        .args(&["query", r#".[] | select(.status != "closed")"#])
        .output()?;

    serde_json::from_slice(&output.stdout)?
}

// Enrich with Scope data
fn enrich_ticket(&self, ticket: Ticket) -> EnrichedTicket {
    let scope_data = self.read_scope_metadata(&ticket.id);

    EnrichedTicket {
        ticket,
        loops: scope_data.loops,
        progress: format!("{}/{}",
            scope_data.loops.iter().filter(|l| l.status == Passed).count(),
            scope_data.loops.len()
        ),
        has_escalations: scope_data.loops.iter().any(|l| l.status == Escalated),
    }
}
5. Acceptance Criteria → Verification Rubrics
Ticket acceptance criteria inform checker agents:

markdown
# .tickets/nw-5c46.md
---
# ...
---

# Acceptance Criteria
- [ ] POST /auth/login returns 200 with valid credentials
- [ ] POST /auth/login returns 401 with invalid credentials
- [ ] Tokens expire after 1 hour
- [ ] Refresh tokens work correctly
- [ ] All endpoints require valid JWT
Scope uses these to generate rubrics:

rust
impl RubricGenerator {
    fn generate_from_ticket(&self, ticket: &Ticket) -> VerificationPyramid {
        let acceptance = self.parse_acceptance_criteria(&ticket);

        VerificationPyramid {
            semantic_checks: SemanticLayer {
                enabled: true,
                checks: acceptance.iter().map(|criterion| {
                    Check {
                        name: criterion.text,
                        weight: 1.0,
                    }
                }).collect(),
            },
            // ... other layers
        }
    }
}
```

**Checker agent sees:**
```
Original intent: Implement POST /auth/login endpoint
Acceptance criteria from ticket:
  - POST /auth/login returns 200 with valid credentials
  - POST /auth/login returns 401 with invalid credentials

Current diff: [shows maker's implementation]

Verify: Does this implementation meet the criteria?
```

## File Structure
```
my-project/
├─ .tickets/                    # tk's domain
│  ├─ nw-5c46.md               # Ticket: Add auth
│  ├─ nw-7e8f.md               # Ticket: Fix payment API
│  └─ nw-9a0b.md               # Ticket: Update docs
│
├─ .scope/                      # Scope's domain
│  ├─ tickets/                  # Links tickets to loops
│  │  ├─ nw-5c46.yaml          # Loop DAG for ticket nw-5c46
│  │  ├─ nw-7e8f.yaml          # Loop DAG for ticket nw-7e8f
│  │  └─ nw-9a0b.yaml          # Loop DAG for ticket nw-9a0b
│  │
│  ├─ loops/                    # Loop execution state
│  │  ├─ loop-1/
│  │  │  ├─ state.json         # Current state
│  │  │  ├─ attempts.jsonl     # All attempts
│  │  │  └─ worktree -> ../../worktrees/loop-1
│  │  └─ loop-2/
│  │      └─ ...
│  │
│  ├─ worktrees/                # Git worktrees (gitignored)
│  │  ├─ loop-1/
│  │  └─ loop-2/
│  │
│  ├─ logs/
│  │  └─ events.db             # SQLite event log
│  │
│  ├─ rubric-template.yaml     # Default verification pyramid
│  └─ prompts/
│     ├─ maker-prompt.md
│     └─ checker-prompt.md
│
└─ src/                         # Your actual code
   └─ ...
Commands Integration
Scope wraps tk commands:
bash
# Create ticket AND start Scope on it
$ scope new "Add user authentication"
  ↳ tk create "Add user authentication" -t feature
  ↳ scope start nw-5c46

# List tickets with Scope enrichment
$ scope ls
  ↳ tk query (get tickets)
  ↳ enrich with .scope/tickets/*.yaml (add loop progress)
  ↳ display enriched view

# Show ticket + DAG
$ scope show nw-5c46
  ↳ tk show nw-5c46 (show ticket details)
  ↳ display DAG from .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml

# Close ticket when all loops pass (auto)
# (Scope calls `tk close nw-5c46` internally)
PRD Updates
Feature: tk Integration (Highest Priority)
Motivation:
Don't reinvent ticket management. Use proven, git-backed CLI tool.

Design:

1. Ticket Creation:

User runs: scope new "Title" or tk create "Title"
Scope reads ticket from .tickets/{id}.md
Decomposer agent creates DAG
Save to .scope/tickets/{id}.yaml
2. Ticket Status Sync:

First loop starts → tk start {id}
All loops pass → tk close {id}
(Optional) Add Scope-specific fields to ticket frontmatter
3. Dependency Handling:

Read ticket dependencies from YAML frontmatter
Block loops if ticket dependencies unresolved
Use tk dep tree to visualize ticket-level dependencies
4. Acceptance Criteria → Rubrics:

Parse markdown acceptance criteria
Generate semantic checks for checker agent
Display in DAG Viewer (checkbox list)
5. Issue Tracker View:

Data source: tk query
Enrich with: loop progress, escalation count
Actions: Open in tk, start Scope on ticket
Non-goals:

Don't modify tk (use it as-is)
Don't duplicate ticket data (single source of truth: .tickets/)
Don't build custom ticket UI (just display tk data)
Example Workflow
bash
# Day 1: Create ticket
$ tk create "Add user authentication" \
    -t feature \
    -d "Implement JWT-based auth" \
    --acceptance "POST /auth/login works" \
    --acceptance "Tokens expire after 1 hour"

Created: nw-5c46

# Start Scope on ticket
$ scope start nw-5c46

Analyzing ticket nw-5c46...
Decomposed into 4 loops:
├─ loop-1: Implement schema
├─ loop-2: Implement login endpoint
├─ loop-3: Write tests
└─ loop-4: Add middleware

Starting loop-1...

# [Scope runs loops in background]

# Day 1, later: Check status
$ scope status nw-5c46

Ticket: nw-5c46 - Add user authentication
Status: in_progress (via tk)

Loops:
✓ loop-1: Schema (passed)
● loop-2: Login endpoint (running, attempt 2/5)
○ loop-3: Tests (pending, blocked by loop-2)
○ loop-4: Middleware (pending, blocked by loop-2)

1 escalation:
⚠ loop-2: Type error in auth.ts:42

# Open Monitor View (Tauri app)
$ scope

# [User sees escalation in Monitor View]
# [User fixes escalation]
# [loop-2 passes]
# [loop-3 and loop-4 start]

# Day 2: All loops complete
# Scope automatically runs:
$ tk close nw-5c46

# Verify
$ tk show nw-5c46

---
id: nw-5c46
status: closed  ← updated by Scope
---

# Check what's ready to work on
$ tk ready
nw-7e8f  Fix payment API
nw-9a0b  Update documentation
Benefits of Using tk
✅ Git-backed: Tickets are versioned, diffable, mergeable
✅ CLI-first: Works without Scope UI (fallback)
✅ Markdown: Human-readable, AI-readable
✅ Dependencies: Already has tk dep, tk dep tree
✅ Queries: tk query gives JSON for Scope to consume
✅ No lock-in: If Scope dies, you still have tk

This integration keeps Scope focused on loop orchestration, not ticket management. Does this match your vision?

Scope: Dependencies & Build Plan (Revised)

Core Components (Rust Backend)
1. Agent Adapter System
rust
// Unified interface for BOTH maker and checker agents
trait Agent {
    async fn execute(
        &self,
        task: Task,
        context: Context,
    ) -> Result<AgentOutput>;

    fn capabilities(&self) -> AgentCapabilities;
}

struct MakerTask {
    description: String,
    context: Context,
    history: Vec<Attempt>,  // Prior attempts for learning
}

struct CheckerTask {
    diff: Diff,
    intent: String,
    rubric: VerificationPyramid,
}

enum AgentOutput {
    Maker(Diff),
    Checker(VerificationResult),
}

// Implementations:
struct ClaudeCodeAdapter { /* ... */ }
struct CodexAdapter { /* ... */ }
struct AiderAdapter { /* ... */ }
struct ClaudeAPIAdapter { /* ... */ }  // For checker-only workflows
Build effort: Medium

Each adapter = 200-500 LOC
4 adapters × 350 LOC avg = ~1400 LOC
Need to handle: process spawning, PTY management, output parsing
2. Loop Executor
rust
struct LoopExecutor {
    maker: Box<dyn Agent>,
    checker: Box<dyn Agent>,
    event_bus: EventBus,
}

impl LoopExecutor {
    async fn execute(&self, loop_def: LoopDefinition) -> LoopResult {
        for attempt in 1..=loop_def.max_turns {
            // Maker generates diff
            let maker_output = self.maker.execute(
                MakerTask {
                    description: loop_def.task,
                    context: loop_def.context,
                    history: attempts.clone(),
                }
            ).await?;

            // Checker verifies diff
            let checker_output = self.checker.execute(
                CheckerTask {
                    diff: maker_output.diff,
                    intent: loop_def.task,
                    rubric: loop_def.rubric,
                }
            ).await?;

            if checker_output.passed {
                return LoopResult::Success { ... };
            }

            attempts.push(Attempt {
                maker_output,
                checker_feedback: checker_output.feedback,
            });
        }

        LoopResult::Failure { reason: MaxTurns }
    }
}
Build effort: Medium

~500 LOC for core loop logic
Needs: state management, attempt history, timeout handling
3. Task Decomposer
rust
struct TaskDecomposer {
    agent: Box<dyn Agent>,  // LLM for decomposition
}

impl TaskDecomposer {
    async fn decompose(&self, ticket: Ticket) -> DAG {
        let prompt = format!(
            "Decompose this ticket into atomic tasks:\n\
             Title: {}\n\
             Description: {}\n\
             Acceptance: {:?}\n\n\
             Return JSON with tasks and dependencies.",
            ticket.title, ticket.description, ticket.acceptance
        );

        let response = self.agent.execute(prompt).await?;
        let dag: DAG = serde_json::from_str(&response)?;

        dag
    }
}
Build effort: Small

~200 LOC
Mostly prompt engineering + JSON parsing
Agent does the heavy lifting
4. Worktree Manager
rust
struct WorktreeManager {
    repo_path: PathBuf,
}

impl WorktreeManager {
    fn create(&self, loop_id: &str) -> Result<PathBuf> {
        let path = self.repo_path
            .join(".scope/worktrees")
            .join(loop_id);

        Command::new("git")
            .args(&["worktree", "add", path.to_str().unwrap(), "-b",
                   &format!("scope-loop-{}", loop_id)])
            .output()?;

        Ok(path)
    }

    fn merge(&self, loop_id: &str) -> Result<CommitSha> {
        // git merge --squash scope-loop-{id}
        // git commit -m "Loop {id}: {task}"
    }

    fn cleanup(&self, loop_id: &str) -> Result<()> {
        // git worktree remove
    }
}
Build effort: Small

~150 LOC
Wraps git commands or uses git2-rs

impl RubricGenerator {
    fn generate(&self, ticket: &Ticket, repo_context: &RepoContext)
        -> VerificationPyramid
    {
        // Detect existing tooling
        let has_typescript = repo_context.has_file("tsconfig.json");
        let has_jest = repo_context.has_file("jest.config.js");
        let has_eslint = repo_context.has_file(".eslintrc");

        // Load template
        let template = self.load_template(".scope/rubric-template.yaml");

        // Merge with detected tooling
        VerificationPyramid {
            static_checks: Layer {
                enabled: has_eslint || has_typescript,
                checks: vec!["eslint", "tsc"],
            },
            deterministic_tests: Layer {
                enabled: has_jest,
                checks: vec!["jest"],
            },
            // ... parse acceptance criteria into semantic checks
        }
    }

6. Event Bus
rust
struct EventBus {
    local_log: EventLog,                    // SQLite
    remote_tx: Option<WebSocketSender>,     // To collab server
    subscribers: Vec<Sender<Event>>,        // Local UI
}

impl EventBus {
    fn emit(&self, event: Event) {
        // Append to SQLite
        self.local_log.append(&event);

        // Broadcast to collab server
        if let Some(tx) = &self.remote_tx {
            tx.send(event.clone());
        }

        // Notify local subscribers
        for sub in &self.subscribers {
            sub.send(event.clone());
        }
    }

    fn subscribe(&self) -> Receiver<Event> {
        // Return channel for UI updates
    }
}
Build effort: Small

~200 LOC
SQLite wrapper, channel management
7. Circuit Breaker
rust
struct CircuitBreaker {
    state: CircuitState,
    failures: Vec<Escalation>,
    threshold: usize,
}

impl CircuitBreaker {
    fn on_escalation(&mut self, escalation: Escalation) {
        self.failures.push(escalation);

        if self.detect_pattern() && self.failures.len() >= self.threshold {
            self.state = CircuitState::Open;
            // Emit event: pause new loops
        }
    }

    fn detect_pattern(&self) -> bool {
        // Check if last N errors are similar (>80% match)
        let recent = &self.failures[self.failures.len().saturating_sub(3)..];
        let similarity = self.calculate_similarity(recent);
        similarity > 0.8
    }
}
Build effort: Small

~200 LOC
String similarity algorithm (Levenshtein or embeddings)

9. Collab Server
rust
// Separate binary: scope-collab-server

struct CollabServer {
    rooms: HashMap<ProjectId, Room>,
}

struct Room {
    clients: HashMap<UserId, WebSocket>,
}

async fn handle_websocket(
    ws: WebSocket,
    project_id: ProjectId,
    user_id: UserId,
) {
    let room = ROOMS.entry(project_id).or_insert_with(Room::new);
    room.clients.insert(user_id, ws);

    // Relay messages
    while let Some(msg) = ws.recv().await {
        let event: Event = serde_json::from_str(&msg)?;

        // Broadcast to all clients in room except sender
        for (uid, client_ws) in &room.clients {
            if uid != &user_id {
                client_ws.send(&msg).await;
            }
        }
    }
}
Build effort: Small

~300 LOC
Simple relay, no business logic
Axum + tokio-tungstenite

1. Issue Tracker View
tsx
function IssueTrackerView() {
    const [tickets, setTickets] = useState<Ticket[]>([]);

    useEffect(() => {
        // Fetch from backend
        invoke('list_tickets').then(setTickets);

        // Subscribe to updates
        listen('ticket.updated', (event) => {
            setTickets(prev => updateTicket(prev, event.payload));
        });
    }, []);

    return (
        <Table>
            {tickets.map(ticket => (
                <Row
                    key={ticket.id}
                    onClick={() => navigate(`/dag/${ticket.id}`)}
                >
                    <Cell>{ticket.id}</Cell>
                    <Cell>{ticket.title}</Cell>
                    <Cell>{ticket.progress}</Cell>
                    {ticket.has_escalations && <Badge>⚠</Badge>}
                </Row>
            ))}
        </Table>
    );
}

2. DAG Visualization
tsx
import ReactFlow from 'reactflow';

function DAGViewer({ ticketId }) {
    const [dag, setDag] = useState<DAG>(null);

    useEffect(() => {
        invoke('get_dag', { ticketId }).then(setDag);
    }, [ticketId]);

    const nodes = dag.loops.map(loop => ({
        id: loop.id,
        data: { label: loop.task, status: loop.status },
        position: calculatePosition(loop),
    }));

    const edges = dag.dependencies.map(([from, to]) => ({
        id: `${from}-${to}`,
        source: from,
        target: to,
    }));

    return <ReactFlow nodes={nodes} edges={edges} />;
}
Build effort: Small

Library (ReactFlow) does heavy lifting
~200 LOC for integration
3. Terminal Embed
tsx
import { Terminal } from 'xterm';
import 'xterm/css/xterm.css';

function LoopTerminal({ loopId }) {
    const termRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);
    const term = useRef<Terminal>(null);

    useEffect(() => {
        term.current = new Terminal();
        term.current.open(termRef.current);

        // Stream output from backend
        listen('loop.terminal_output', (event) => {
            if (event.payload.loop_id === loopId) {
                term.current.write(event.payload.data);
            }
        });
    }, [loopId]);

    return <div ref={termRef} />;
}
Build effort: Small

xterm.js handles rendering
~100 LOC for integration

Risks & Unknowns
High Risk

Agent adapter reliability: What if Claude Code changes API/behavior?

Mitigation: Adapter interface makes swapping easy


Checker agent quality: What if checker gives false positives/negatives?

Mitigation: Human can override, multi-checker consensus (v1.1)


Decomposer accuracy: What if DAG is wrong?

Mitigation: Human can edit DAG, manual task breakdown option



Medium Risk

SQLite concurrency: Can event log handle high write volume?

Mitigation: WAL mode, write batching


Collab server scaling: Can simple relay handle 50+ users?

Mitigation: Redis pub/sub (v1.1), multiple server instances



Low Risk

Git worktree disk usage: Will 20+ worktrees fill disk?

Mitigation: Auto-cleanup old worktrees, configurable limit

Want me to generate starter code for any of these components?Circuit breaker is simple, only keep count of escalated loops, no similarity measures required.Feb 14Perfect. Much simpler.

Circuit Breaker (Simplified)
Design
Just count escalations. When threshold hit → pause.
ruststruct CircuitBreaker {
    state: CircuitState,
    escalation_count: usize,
    threshold: usize,  // Default: 3
}

enum CircuitState {
    Closed,      // Normal operation
    Open,        // Paused (too many escalations)
    HalfOpen,    // Testing after user fix
}

impl CircuitBreaker {
    fn on_escalation(&mut self) {
        self.escalation_count += 1;

        if self.escalation_count >= self.threshold {
            self.state = CircuitState::Open;
            // Emit event: pause new loops
        }
    }

    fn on_resolution(&mut self) {
        self.escalation_count = self.escalation_count.saturating_sub(1);

        if self.state == CircuitState::Open && self.escalation_count < self.threshold {
            // User has resolved enough escalations
            // Don't auto-resume; require explicit user action
        }
    }

    fn on_user_resume(&mut self) {
        self.state = CircuitState::HalfOpen;
        self.escalation_count = 0;
    }

    fn on_loop_passed(&mut self) {
        if self.state == CircuitState::HalfOpen {
            // Test loop passed, fix worked
            self.state = CircuitState::Closed;
        }
    }

    fn should_pause_new_loops(&self) -> bool {
        self.state == CircuitState::Open
    }
}
```

**Build effort:** Small (~50 LOC, not 200)

---

## Behavior

### Scenario 1: Normal Operation
```
Circuit: Closed (0/3 escalations)

Loop 1 → Passes ✓
Loop 2 → Passes ✓
Loop 3 → Passes ✓

Circuit: Still closed (0/3)
Continue spawning loops normally
```

---

### Scenario 2: Circuit Opens
```
Circuit: Closed (0/3)

Loop 1 → Escalates ⚠
Circuit: Closed (1/3)

Loop 2 → Escalates ⚠
Circuit: Closed (2/3)

Loop 3 → Escalates ⚠
Circuit: Open (3/3) 🔴

┌────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Circuit breaker opened                 │
├────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ 3 loops have escalated                 │
│                                        │
│ New loops are paused until you         │
│ resolve some escalations.              │
│                                        │
│ [Review Escalations] [Resume Anyway]  │
└────────────────────────────────────────┘

Action: Scope stops spawning new loops
Running loops: Continue executing
Queued loops: Stay queued
```

---

### Scenario 3: User Resolves, Manual Resume
```
Circuit: Open (3/3)

User fixes escalation 1 → Resolves
Circuit: Open (2/3)

User fixes escalation 2 → Resolves
Circuit: Open (1/3)

User clicks "Resume"
Circuit: HalfOpen (0/3) 🟡

Scope spawns 1 test loop:

Test loop → Passes ✓
Circuit: Closed (0/3) 🟢

Resume all queued loops
```

---

### Scenario 4: Test Loop Fails
```
Circuit: HalfOpen (0/3)

Test loop → Escalates ⚠
Circuit: Open (1/3) 🔴

┌────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Circuit re-opened                      │
├────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Test loop failed. The issue may not   │
│ be fully resolved.                     │
│                                        │
│ [Review Test Loop] [Try Again]        │
└────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

---
```

Configuration
yaml# .scope/config.yaml
circuit_breaker:
  enabled: true
  threshold: 3              # Open circuit after N escalations
  require_manual_resume: true   # Don't auto-resume when count drops
  test_loop_on_resume: true     # Run 1 test loop before resuming all

Events
typescripttype CircuitBreakerEvent =
  | { type: "circuit_breaker.opened", escalation_count: number }
  | { type: "circuit_breaker.half_open", test_loop_id: string }
  | { type: "circuit_breaker.closed" }
  | { type: "circuit_breaker.test_passed", test_loop_id: string }
  | { type: "circuit_breaker.test_failed", test_loop_id: string }

Implementation
rust// src/circuit_breaker.rs

#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, PartialEq)]
pub enum CircuitState {
    Closed,
    Open,
    HalfOpen,
}

pub struct CircuitBreaker {
    state: CircuitState,
    escalation_count: usize,
    threshold: usize,
}

impl CircuitBreaker {
    pub fn new(threshold: usize) -> Self {
        Self {
            state: CircuitState::Closed,
            escalation_count: 0,
            threshold,
        }
    }

    pub fn on_escalation(&mut self) -> Option<CircuitBreakerEvent> {
        self.escalation_count += 1;

        if self.escalation_count >= self.threshold {
            self.state = CircuitState::Open;
            Some(CircuitBreakerEvent::Opened {
                escalation_count: self.escalation_count,
            })
        } else {
            None
        }
    }

    pub fn on_resolution(&mut self) {
        self.escalation_count = self.escalation_count.saturating_sub(1);
        // Note: Don't auto-close circuit, require manual resume
    }

    pub fn user_resume(&mut self) -> CircuitBreakerEvent {
        self.state = CircuitState::HalfOpen;
        self.escalation_count = 0;
        CircuitBreakerEvent::HalfOpen
    }

    pub fn on_test_loop_result(&mut self, passed: bool) -> CircuitBreakerEvent {
        if passed {
            self.state = CircuitState::Closed;
            CircuitBreakerEvent::Closed
        } else {
            self.state = CircuitState::Open;
            self.escalation_count = 1;
            CircuitBreakerEvent::TestFailed
        }
    }

    pub fn should_pause_new_loops(&self) -> bool {
        self.state == CircuitState::Open
    }

    pub fn can_spawn_test_loop(&self) -> bool {
        self.state == CircuitState::HalfOpen
    }
}

#[derive(Debug, Clone)]
pub enum CircuitBreakerEvent {
    Opened { escalation_count: usize },
    HalfOpen,
    Closed,
    TestFailed,
}
Build effort: ~50 LOC (was 200)

Integration with Loop Orchestrator
ruststruct LoopOrchestrator {
    circuit_breaker: CircuitBreaker,
    // ...
}

impl LoopOrchestrator {
    fn should_spawn_loop(&self, loop_id: &LoopId) -> bool {
        // Check circuit breaker
        if self.circuit_breaker.should_pause_new_loops() {
            return false;
        }

        // Check other conditions (dependencies, WIP limits, etc.)
        // ...

        true
    }

    fn on_loop_escalated(&mut self, loop_id: LoopId) {
        if let Some(event) = self.circuit_breaker.on_escalation() {
            self.event_bus.emit(Event::CircuitBreaker(event));

            // Pause queued loops
            self.pause_queued_loops();
        }
    }

    fn on_escalation_resolved(&mut self, loop_id: LoopId) {
        self.circuit_breaker.on_resolution();
        // Don't auto-resume, wait for user
    }

    async fn user_resume_loops(&mut self) {
        let event = self.circuit_breaker.user_resume();
        self.event_bus.emit(Event::CircuitBreaker(event));

        // Spawn 1 test loop
        let test_loop = self.queued_loops.pop_front().unwrap();
        let result = self.execute_loop(test_loop).await;

        let passed = matches!(result, LoopResult::Success { .. });
        let event = self.circuit_breaker.on_test_loop_result(passed);
        self.event_bus.emit(Event::CircuitBreaker(event));

        if passed {
            // Resume all queued loops
            self.resume_queued_loops();
        }
    }
}

What's built:
Backend (Rust)

Agent trait + Claude Code adapter (maker only) (~200 LOC)

rust   trait Agent {
       async fn execute(&self, task: String) -> Result<String>;
   }

   struct ClaudeCodeAdapter;

Simple checker (just run tests, no LLM) (~100 LOC)

rust   struct SimpleChecker;

   impl SimpleChecker {
       fn verify(&self, worktree: &Path) -> bool {
           // Just run: pytest / jest / cargo test
           Command::new("pytest").current_dir(worktree).status().unwrap().success()
       }
   }

Worktree manager (~100 LOC)

rust   struct WorktreeManager;

   impl WorktreeManager {
       fn create(&self, loop_id: &str) -> PathBuf { /* git worktree add */ }
       fn merge(&self, loop_id: &str) -> String { /* git merge --squash */ }
       fn cleanup(&self, loop_id: &str) { /* git worktree remove */ }
   }

Loop executor (~150 LOC)

rust   struct LoopExecutor {
       maker: ClaudeCodeAdapter,
       checker: SimpleChecker,
       worktree_mgr: WorktreeManager,
   }

   impl LoopExecutor {
       async fn execute(&self, task: String) -> LoopResult {
           let worktree = self.worktree_mgr.create("loop-123");

           // Maker generates code
           self.maker.execute(task).await?;

           // Checker verifies
           let passed = self.checker.verify(&worktree);

           if passed {
               let commit = self.worktree_mgr.merge("loop-123");
               LoopResult::Success { commit }
           } else {
               LoopResult::Failure
           }
       }
   }

CLI binary (~50 LOC)

LLM checker agent (~150 LOC)

rust   struct LLMChecker {
       client: AnthropicClient,
   }

   impl LLMChecker {
       async fn verify(&self, diff: &str, intent: &str) -> VerificationResult {
           let prompt = format!(
               "Intent: {}\nDiff:\n{}\n\nDoes this meet the intent? Reply YES or NO with feedback.",
               intent, diff
           );

           let response = self.client.complete(prompt).await?;

           VerificationResult {
               passed: response.contains("YES"),
               feedback: response,
           }
       }
   }

Loop executor with retries (~100 LOC added)

rust   async fn execute(&self, task: String) -> LoopResult {
       let worktree = self.worktree_mgr.create("loop-123");
       let mut attempts = vec![];

       for attempt_num in 1..=5 {
           println!("Attempt {}/5:", attempt_num);

           // Maker sees history
           let output = self.maker.execute(task, &attempts).await?;

           // Checker verifies
           let result = self.checker.verify(&output, &task).await?;

           attempts.push(Attempt {
               num: attempt_num,
               output,
               feedback: result.feedback.clone(),
           });

           if result.passed {
               let commit = self.worktree_mgr.merge("loop-123");
               return LoopResult::Success { commit, attempts };
           }
       }

       LoopResult::Failure { attempts }
   }
Added: ~250 LOC
Total: ~850 LOC
