LLM decomposer (~200 LOC)

rust   struct LLMDecomposer {
       client: AnthropicClient,
   }

   impl LLMDecomposer {
       async fn decompose(&self, ticket: &Ticket) -> DAG {
           let prompt = format!(
               "Decompose this ticket into atomic tasks:\n{}\n\nReturn JSON.",
               ticket.description
           );

           let response = self.client.complete(prompt).await?;
           serde_json::from_str(&response)?
       }
   }

DAG executor (~200 LOC)

rust   struct DAGExecutor;

   impl DAGExecutor {
       async fn execute(&self, dag: DAG) {
           let mut completed = HashSet::new();

           loop {
               // Find loops ready to run (dependencies met)
               let ready = dag.nodes.iter()
                   .filter(|n| !completed.contains(&n.id))
                   .filter(|n| n.deps.iter().all(|d| completed.contains(d)))
                   .collect::<Vec<_>>();

               if ready.is_empty() { break; }

               // Run ready loops in parallel
               for node in ready {
                   let result = self.loop_executor.execute(node.task).await;
                   if result.is_success() {
                       completed.insert(node.id);
                   }
               }
           }
       }
   }

DAG visualization in UI (~200 LOC)

tsx   import ReactFlow from 'reactflow';

   function DAGView({ ticketId }) {
       const [dag, setDag] = useState(null);

       useEffect(() => {
           fetch(`/api/tickets/${ticketId}/dag`)
               .then(r => r.json())
               .then(setDag);
       }, [ticketId]);

       return <ReactFlow nodes={dag.nodes} edges={dag.edges} />;
   }

What's added:

Escalation handling (~100 LOC)

rust   enum LoopResult {
       Success { commit: String },
       Escalated { attempts: Vec<Attempt>, reason: String },
   }

   impl DAGExecutor {
       async fn execute(&self, dag: DAG) {
           // ... existing code ...

           for node in ready {
               let result = self.loop_executor.execute(node.task).await;

               match result {
                   LoopResult::Success { commit } => {
                       completed.insert(node.id);
                   }
                   LoopResult::Escalated { reason, .. } => {
                       // Pause downstream loops
                       self.pause_dependents(node.id);
                       // Emit escalation event
                       self.event_bus.emit(Event::LoopEscalated {
                           loop_id: node.id,
                           reason,
                       });
                   }
               }
           }
       }
   }

Monitor View UI (~400 LOC)

tsx   function MonitorView() {

rust   struct EventLog {
       conn: Connection,
   }

   impl EventLog {
       fn append(&self, event: &Event) {
           self.conn.execute(
               "INSERT INTO events (type, payload, timestamp) VALUES (?1, ?2, ?3)",
               params![event.type_, serde_json::to_string(&event)?, Utc::now()],
           )?;
       }
   }

WebSocket for events (~150 LOC)

rust   async fn ws_handler(ws: WebSocket) {
       let (mut sender, _) = ws.split();
       let mut rx = EVENT_BUS.subscribe();

       while let Some(event) = rx.recv().await {
           sender.send(serde_json::to_string(&event)?).await;
       }
   }

rust// src/main.rs
use std::process::Command;

fn main() {
    let ticket_id = std::env::args().nth(1).expect("Usage: scope spawn <ticket-id>");

    // 1. Read ticket
    let ticket = read_ticket(&ticket_id);
    println!("Task: {}", ticket.title);

    // 2. Create worktree
    create_worktree(&ticket_id);

    // 3. Spawn Claude Code
    spawn_claude_code(&ticket_id, &ticket.title);
}

fn read_ticket(id: &str) -> Ticket {
    let path = format!(".tickets/{}.md", id);
    let content = std::fs::read_to_string(&path).unwrap();

    // Parse YAML frontmatter + markdown (simple regex for now)
    Ticket {
        id: id.to_string(),
        title: extract_title(&content),
        description: extract_description(&content),
    }
}

fn create_worktree(id: &str) {
    let path = format!(".scope/worktrees/{}", id);

    Command::new("git")
        .args(&["worktree", "add", &path, "-b", &format!("scope-{}", id)])
        .status()
        .expect("Failed to create worktree");

    println!("Created worktree: {}", path);
}

fn spawn_claude_code(id: &str, task: &str) {
    let worktree = format!(".scope/worktrees/{}", id);

    // Spawn Claude Code in worktree
    Command::new("claude-code")
        .current_dir(&worktree)
        .arg(task)
        .spawn()
        .expect("Failed to spawn Claude Code");

    println!("Agent running in {}", worktree);
}

rustfn spawn_claude_code(id: &str, task: &str) {
    let worktree = format!(".scope/worktrees/{}", id);

    // Maker runs
    Command::new("claude-code")
        .current_dir(&worktree)
        .arg(task)
        .status()  // Wait for completion
        .expect("Maker failed");

    println!("Maker done");

    // Get diff
    let diff = get_diff(&worktree);

    // Checker runs
    let passed = run_checker(&diff, task);

    if passed {
        println!("Checker: PASS ✓");
        let commit = merge_worktree(id);
        println!("Commit: {}", commit);
    } else {
        println!("Checker: FAIL ✗");
    }
}

fn get_diff(worktree: &str) -> String {
    let output = Command::new("git")
        .current_dir(worktree)
        .args(&["diff", "HEAD"])
        .output()
        .unwrap();

    String::from_utf8(output.stdout).unwrap()
}

fn run_checker(diff: &str, intent: &str) -> bool {
    // Simple: just call Claude API with prompt
    let prompt = format!(
        "Intent: {}\n\nDiff:\n{}\n\nDoes this correctly implement the intent? Reply YES or NO.",
        intent, diff
    );

    let response = call_claude_api(&prompt);
    response.contains("YES")
}

fn call_claude_api(prompt: &str) -> String {
    // Use anthropic SDK
    let client = anthropic::Client::new();
    let response = client.complete(prompt);
    response.text
}

fn merge_worktree(id: &str) -> String {
    // git merge --squash scope-{id}
    // git commit -m "..."
    // return commit SHA
}

Slice 4: Loop (Retries)
Goal: If checker fails, retry with feedback
bash$ scope spawn nw-5c46

Attempt 1/5:
  Maker done (30s)
  Checker: FAIL (missing tests)

Attempt 2/5:
  Maker done (25s, added tests)
  Checker: PASS ✓

Result: Passed in 2 attempts
Commit: a1b2c3d
What's added: ~150 LOC
rustfn spawn_loop(id: &str, task: &str) {
    let worktree = format!(".scope/worktrees/{}", id);
    let max_attempts = 5;
    let mut history = vec![];

    for attempt in 1..=max_attempts {
        println!("\nAttempt {}/{}:", attempt, max_attempts);

        // Maker runs (with history for context)
        run_maker(&worktree, task, &history);
        println!("  Maker done");

        // Get diff
        let diff = get_diff(&worktree);

        // Checker runs
        let result = run_checker(&diff, task);

        history.push(CheckerResult {
            attempt,
            diff: diff.clone(),
            passed: result.passed,
            feedback: result.feedback.clone(),
        });

        if result.passed {
            println!("  Checker: PASS ✓");
            let commit = merge_worktree(id);
            println!("\nResult: Passed in {} attempts", attempt);
            println!("Commit: {}", commit);
            return;
        } else {
            println!("  Checker: FAIL ({})", result.feedback);
        }
    }

    println!("\nResult: Failed after {} attempts", max_attempts);
}

fn run_maker(worktree: &str, task: &str, history: &[CheckerResult]) {
    // Build context from history
    let context = if history.is_empty() {
        task.to_string()
    } else {
        format!(
            "{}\n\nPrevious attempts:\n{}",
            task,
            history.iter()
                .map(|h| format!("Attempt {}: {}", h.attempt, h.feedback))
                .collect::<Vec<_>>()
                .join("\n")
        )
    };

    Command::new("claude-code")
        .current_dir(worktree)
        .arg(&context)
        .status()
        .unwrap();
}
What you can do:

Auto-merge on success

What you CAN'T do yet:

See loop progress in real-time
Manually fix after failure

rust// Save state to disk
fn save_loop_state(id: &str, state: &LoopState) {
    let path = format!(".scope/loops/{}.json", id);
    std::fs::create_dir_all(".scope/loops").unwrap();

    let json = serde_json::to_string_pretty(state).unwrap();
    std::fs::write(path, json).unwrap();
}

// Load state from disk
fn load_loop_state(id: &str) -> LoopState {
    let path = format!(".scope/loops/{}.json", id);
    let json = std::fs::read_to_string(path).unwrap();
    serde_json::from_str(&json).unwrap()
}

What's Built:
Backend (Rust/Tauri) - ~400 LOC
rust// src-tauri/src/main.rs

use tauri::command;
use std::process::Command;
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};

#[derive(Debug, Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct Ticket {
    id: String,
    title: String,
    status: String,
    type_: String,
    priority: i32,
    assignee: String,
    dependencies: Vec<String>,
    description: String,
    created: String,
    updated: String,
}

#[command]
fn list_tickets() -> Result<Vec<Ticket>, String> {
    // Call: tk query
    let output = Command::new("tk")
        .arg("query")
        .arg(".")  // All tickets
        .output()
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    let json = String::from_utf8(output.stdout).map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;
    let tickets: Vec<Ticket> = serde_json::from_str(&json).map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    Ok(tickets)
}

#[command]
fn get_ticket(id: String) -> Result<Ticket, String> {
    let output = Command::new("tk")
        .arg("query")
        .arg(&format!(r#".[] | select(.id == "{}")"#, id))
        .output()
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    let json = String::from_utf8(output.stdout).map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;
    let ticket: Ticket = serde_json::from_str(&json).map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    Ok(ticket)
}

#[command]
fn create_ticket(
    title: String,
    description: Option<String>,
    type_: Option<String>,
    priority: Option<i32>,
) -> Result<String, String> {
    let mut cmd = Command::new("tk");
    cmd.arg("create").arg(&title);

    if let Some(desc) = description {
        cmd.arg("-d").arg(desc);
    }
    if let Some(t) = type_ {
        cmd.arg("-t").arg(t);
    }
    if let Some(p) = priority {
        cmd.arg("-p").arg(p.to_string());
    }

    let output = cmd.output().map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;
    let ticket_id = String::from_utf8(output.stdout)
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())?
        .trim()
        .split_whitespace()
        .last()
        .unwrap()
        .to_string();

    Ok(ticket_id)
}

#[command]
fn update_ticket_status(id: String, status: String) -> Result<(), String> {
    Command::new("tk")
        .arg("status")
        .arg(&id)
        .arg(&status)
        .output()
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    Ok(())
}

#[command]
fn add_dependency(ticket_id: String, depends_on: String) -> Result<(), String> {
    Command::new("tk")
        .arg("dep")
        .arg(&ticket_id)
        .arg(&depends_on)
        .output()
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    Ok(())
}

#[command]
fn edit_ticket(id: String) -> Result<(), String> {
    // Opens ticket in $EDITOR
    Command::new("tk")
        .arg("edit")
        .arg(&id)
        .spawn()
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    Ok(())
}

fn main() {
    tauri::Builder::default()
        .invoke_handler(tauri::generate_handler![
            list_tickets,
            get_ticket,
            create_ticket,
            update_ticket_status,
            add_dependency,
            edit_ticket,
        ])
        .run(tauri::generate_context!())
        .expect("error while running tauri application");
}

tsx// src/App.tsx
import { useState } from 'react';
import IssueTracker from './views/IssueTracker';
import TicketDetail from './views/TicketDetail';

function App() {
    const [currentView, setCurrentView] = useState<'tracker' | 'detail'>('tracker');
    const [selectedTicket, setSelectedTicket] = useState<string | null>(null);

    return (
        <div className="app">
            <Sidebar>
                <button onClick={() => setCurrentView('tracker')}>
                    Issues
                </button>
            </Sidebar>

            <main>
                {currentView === 'tracker' && (
                    <IssueTracker
                        onSelectTicket={(id) => {
                            setSelectedTicket(id);
                            setCurrentView('detail');
                        }}
                    />
                )}

                {currentView === 'detail' && selectedTicket && (
                    <TicketDetail
                        ticketId={selectedTicket}
                        onBack={() => setCurrentView('tracker')}
                    />
                )}
            </main>
        </div>
    );
}
Issue Tracker (Linear-style table):
tsx// src/views/IssueTracker.tsx
import { useState, useEffect } from 'react';
import { invoke } from '@tauri-apps/api';
import TicketRow from '../components/TicketRow';
import TicketForm from '../components/TicketForm';

interface Ticket {
    id: string;
    title: string;
    status: string;
    type_: string;
    priority: number;
    assignee: string;
}

function IssueTracker({ onSelectTicket }: { onSelectTicket: (id: string) => void }) {
    const [tickets, setTickets] = useState<Ticket[]>([]);
    const [filter, setFilter] = useState<string>('all');
    const [showNewTicket, setShowNewTicket] = useState(false);

    useEffect(() => {
        loadTickets();
    }, []);

    async function loadTickets() {
        const result = await invoke<Ticket[]>('list_tickets');
        setTickets(result);
    }

    const filteredTickets = tickets.filter(t => {
        if (filter === 'all') return true;
        if (filter === 'open') return t.status === 'open';
        if (filter === 'in_progress') return t.status === 'in_progress';
        if (filter === 'closed') return t.status === 'closed';
        return true;
    });

    return (
        <div className="issue-tracker">
            <header>
                <h1>Issues</h1>
                <div className="filters">
                    <select value={filter} onChange={(e) => setFilter(e.target.value)}>
                        <option value="all">All</option>
                        <option value="open">Open</option>
                        <option value="in_progress">In Progress</option>
                        <option value="closed">Closed</option>
                    </select>
                </div>
                <button onClick={() => setShowNewTicket(true)}>
                    + New Issue
                </button>
            </header>

            <table>
                <thead>
                    <tr>
                        <th>ID</th>
                        <th>Title</th>
                        <th>Status</th>
                        <th>Type</th>
                        <th>Priority</th>
                        <th>Assignee</th>
                    </tr>
                </thead>
                <tbody>
                    {filteredTickets.map(ticket => (
                        <TicketRow
                            key={ticket.id}
                            ticket={ticket}
                            onClick={() => onSelectTicket(ticket.id)}
                        />
                    ))}
                </tbody>
            </table>

            {showNewTicket && (
                <TicketForm
                    onClose={() => setShowNewTicket(false)}
                    onCreated={() => {
                        setShowNewTicket(false);
                        loadTickets();
                    }}
                />
            )}
        </div>
    );
}

function TicketRow({ ticket, onClick }: { ticket: Ticket, onClick: () => void }) {

            </td>

                <PriorityIndicator priority={ticket.priority} />
            </td>

}
New Ticket Form:
tsx// src/components/TicketForm.tsx
import { useState } from 'react';
import { invoke } from '@tauri-apps/api';

function TicketForm({ onClose, onCreated }) {
    const [title, setTitle] = useState('');
    const [description, setDescription] = useState('');
    const [type, setType] = useState('task');
    const [priority, setPriority] = useState(2);

    async function handleSubmit(e: React.FormEvent) {
        e.preventDefault();

        const ticketId = await invoke<string>('create_ticket', {
            title,
            description: description || null,
            type_: type,
            priority,
        });

        console.log('Created ticket:', ticketId);
        onCreated();
    }

    return (
        <div className="modal-overlay">
            <div className="modal">
                <header>
                    <h2>New Issue</h2>
                    <button onClick={onClose}>×</button>
                </header>

                <form onSubmit={handleSubmit}>
                    <div className="form-group">
                        <label>Title</label>
                        <input
                            type="text"
                            value={title}
                            onChange={(e) => setTitle(e.target.value)}
                            required
                            autoFocus
                        />
                    </div>

                    <div className="form-group">
                        <label>Description</label>
                        <textarea
                            value={description}
                            onChange={(e) => setDescription(e.target.value)}
                            rows={5}
                        />
                    </div>

                    <div className="form-row">
                        <div className="form-group">
                            <label>Type</label>
                            <select value={type} onChange={(e) => setType(e.target.value)}>
                                <option value="bug">Bug</option>
                                <option value="feature">Feature</option>
                                <option value="task">Task</option>
                                <option value="epic">Epic</option>
                                <option value="chore">Chore</option>
                            </select>
                        </div>

                        <div className="form-group">
                            <label>Priority</label>
                            <select value={priority} onChange={(e) => setPriority(Number(e.target.value))}>
                                <option value={0}>0 - Highest</option>
                                <option value={1}>1 - High</option>
                                <option value={2}>2 - Medium</option>
                                <option value={3}>3 - Low</option>
                                <option value={4}>4 - Lowest</option>
                            </select>
                        </div>
                    </div>

                    <footer>
                        <button type="button" onClick={onClose}>
                            Cancel
                        </button>
                        <button type="submit">
                            Create Issue
                        </button>
                    </footer>
                </form>
            </div>
        </div>
    );
}
Ticket Detail View:
tsx// src/views/TicketDetail.tsx
import { useState, useEffect } from 'react';
import { invoke } from '@tauri-apps/api';

function TicketDetail({ ticketId, onBack }) {
    const [ticket, setTicket] = useState<Ticket | null>(null);

    useEffect(() => {
        loadTicket();
    }, [ticketId]);

    async function loadTicket() {
        const result = await invoke<Ticket>('get_ticket', { id: ticketId });
        setTicket(result);
    }

    async function updateStatus(newStatus: string) {
        await invoke('update_ticket_status', {
            id: ticketId,
            status: newStatus,
        });
        loadTicket();
    }

    async function openInEditor() {
        await invoke('edit_ticket', { id: ticketId });
    }

    if (!ticket) return <div>Loading...</div>;

    return (
        <div className="ticket-detail">
            <header>
                <button onClick={onBack}>← Back</button>
                <h1>{ticket.id}: {ticket.title}</h1>
                <button onClick={openInEditor}>Edit in $EDITOR</button>
            </header>

            <div className="ticket-metadata">
                <div className="field">
                    <label>Status</label>
                    <select
                        value={ticket.status}
                        onChange={(e) => updateStatus(e.target.value)}
                    >
                        <option value="open">Open</option>
                        <option value="in_progress">In Progress</option>
                        <option value="closed">Closed</option>
                    </select>
                </div>

                <div className="field">
                    <label>Type</label>
                    <span>{ticket.type_}</span>
                </div>

                <div className="field">
                    <label>Priority</label>
                    <span>{ticket.priority}</span>
                </div>

                <div className="field">
                    <label>Assignee</label>
                    <span>{ticket.assignee}</span>
                </div>
            </div>

            <div className="ticket-content">
                <h2>Description</h2>
                <div className="markdown-content">
                    {ticket.description}
                </div>
            </div>

            <div className="ticket-dependencies">
                <h2>Dependencies</h2>
                {ticket.dependencies.length > 0 ? (
                    <ul>
                        {ticket.dependencies.map(dep => (
                            <li key={dep}>{dep}</li>
                        ))}
                    </ul>
                ) : (
                    <p>No dependencies</p>
                )}
            </div>

            <div className="ticket-actions">
                <button onClick={() => updateStatus('in_progress')}>
                    Start Work
                </button>
                <button onClick={() => updateStatus('closed')}>
                    Close
                </button>
            </div>
        </div>
    );
}

Slice 2: Spawn Maker Agent
Goal: Add "Start Work" button that spawns Claude Code
What's added:
Backend:
rust#[command]
fn start_work(ticket_id: String) -> Result<(), String> {
    let ticket = get_ticket(ticket_id.clone())?;

    // Create worktree
    let worktree = format!(".scope/worktrees/{}", ticket_id);
    Command::new("git")
        .args(&["worktree", "add", &worktree, "-b", &format!("scope-{}", ticket_id)])
        .output()
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    // Spawn Claude Code
    Command::new("claude-code")
        .current_dir(&worktree)
        .arg(&ticket.title)
        .spawn()
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())?;

    // Update tk status
    update_ticket_status(ticket_id, "in_progress".to_string())?;

    Ok(())
}
Frontend:
tsx// In TicketDetail.tsx
async function startWork() {
    await invoke('start_work', { ticketId: ticket.id });
    alert('Claude Code spawned in worktree');
}

// Update button:
<button onClick={startWork}>
    Start Work (Spawn Claude Code)
</button>

Build time: +1 dayCan we embed the tmux session in a way that we can type into the running claude code seesion?Feb 14Yes, absolutely.
You can embed an interactive tmux session where users can type directly into the Claude Code process.
Two approaches:
Option 1: PTY Bridge (Full Control)

Tauri backend spawns PTY with Claude Code
xterm.js in frontend connects via WebSocket/IPC
Bidirectional: user types → sends to PTY → output streams back
No tmux needed, direct PTY control

Option 2: tmux Attach (Simpler)

Backend spawns Claude Code in tmux
Frontend runs tmux attach in embedded terminal
User gets full tmux session (can detach with Ctrl-b d)
Less control but works immediately

Recommendation: Option 1 (PTY Bridge)
Cleaner UX, user doesn't need to know tmux exists. They just see a terminal that works.

Build time: +1 dayThere's a tension here - if tickets are git tracked how do you enable realtime collaboration?Feb 14Great catch. There's a fundamental conflict:

The Tension
Git (tk tickets):

Files in .tickets/ are git-tracked
Requires commit + push to sync
Conflicts on concurrent edits
Not real-time

Real-time collaboration:

Needs instant sync (WebSocket)
Multiple users see changes < 500ms
No git commits for every status change


Resolution Options
Option 1: Tickets stay git-only, collaboration is view-only

Tickets (.tickets/) = git-tracked, manual sync
Loops (.scope/) = gitignored, real-time via collab server
Trade-off: Can't collaborate on ticket editing, only view loops together
Pro: Simple, tk stays pure
Con: Limited collaboration

Option 2: Hybrid: Git for source, WebSocket for ephemeral state

Ticket content (.tickets/) = git-tracked (description, acceptance criteria)
Ticket metadata (status, assignee) = real-time via collab server
On session end, sync metadata back to git
Trade-off: Metadata can diverge from git until explicit sync
Pro: Real-time status updates, git is still source of truth
Con: Two sources of truth temporarily

Option 3: Operational Transform on git files

Real-time collaboration on .tickets/ files
Auto-commit + push in background
Handle merge conflicts with OT/CRDT
Trade-off: Complex, git history gets noisy
Pro: True real-time on git files
Con: Engineering nightmare

Option 4: Scope owns tickets, tk is read-only view

Tickets live in Scope (SQLite or similar)
tk reads from Scope's data
Git tracks exports/snapshots periodically
Trade-off: tk is no longer the source of truth
Pro: Full control over real-time sync
Con: Defeats purpose of using tk


Recommendation: Option 2 (Hybrid)
Design:
Ticket structure:
├─ .tickets/nw-5c46.md              # Git-tracked (content)
│  └─ Title, description, acceptance criteria
│
└─ .scope/ephemeral/nw-5c46.json    # Gitignored (state)
   └─ Status, assignee, loops, real-time metadata
Flow:

User creates ticket: tk create writes .tickets/nw-5c46.md
Scope reads ticket: Parses .tickets/nw-5c46.md for content
Real-time updates: Status changes broadcast via WebSocket, saved to .scope/ephemeral/
Periodic sync: Every N minutes or on close, write status back to git
Git push: User manually pushes when ready

Collaboration:

Alice and Bob both viewing ticket nw-5c46
Alice clicks "Start Work" → status changes to "in_progress" → WebSocket broadcasts → Bob sees update instantly
Loop progress syncs in real-time (all in .scope/, gitignored)
Ticket content (title, description) doesn't change often, so git sync is fine

Edge case handling:

Conflict: Alice edits ticket description in git, Bob edits in UI → Scope detects git change, prompts Bob to refresh
Offline: User works without collab server, state saved locally, syncs when reconnected


Revised File Structure
.tickets/                    # Git-tracked
├─ nw-5c46.md               # Ticket content (stable)
└─ nw-7e8f.md

.scope/                      # Gitignored
├─ ephemeral/
│  ├─ nw-5c46.json          # Real-time state (status, assignee, etc.)
│  └─ nw-7e8f.json
├─ loops/
│  └─ loop-abc123/
└─ worktrees/
tk integration:

Scope reads .tickets/ for content
Scope reads/writes .scope/ephemeral/ for state
Periodically: Scope runs tk status nw-5c46 in_progress to sync back to git


Does this resolve the tension?
Tickets = git (slow, versioned, content)
State = ephemeral (fast, real-time, metadata)I think we probably need to rip out ticket dependency. We add the ticket context to be git tracked once it's been spawned. Otherwise we have realtime collaborationFeb 14Ah, much cleaner. Let me reframe:

New Design: Rip Out Ticket Dependencies
What Changes:
Remove from tk:

❌ No tk dep (ticket-level dependencies)
❌ No dependency tracking in .tickets/*.md

Add to Scope:

✅ Ticket spawns loops → DAG with dependencies gets created
✅ DAG saved to .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml (git-tracked)
✅ This becomes the "context" for the ticket


File Structure (Revised)
.tickets/                          # Git-tracked (tk owns this)
├─ nw-5c46.md                     # Ticket content only
│  └─ title, description, acceptance criteria
└─ nw-7e8f.md

.scope/                            # Git-tracked (Scope owns this)
├─ tickets/
│  ├─ nw-5c46.yaml                # DAG + loop definitions (committed after spawn)
│  │  └─ loops, dependencies, rubrics
│  └─ nw-7e8f.yaml
│
├─ loops/                          # Gitignored (ephemeral state)
│  ├─ loop-abc123/
│  │  ├─ state.json               # Current status, attempts
│  │  └─ terminal.log             # PTY output
│  └─ loop-def456/
│
└─ worktrees/                      # Gitignored
   └─ loop-abc123/

Workflow
1. Create Ticket (tk)
bash$ tk create "Add authentication"
Created: nw-5c46

# Only writes .tickets/nw-5c46.md
# No dependencies, no state
2. Spawn Loops (Scope)
bash$ scope start nw-5c46

Reading .tickets/nw-5c46.md...
Decomposing into loops...

DAG:
├─ loop-1: Schema
├─ loop-2: API (depends on loop-1)
└─ loop-3: Tests (depends on loop-2)

Saved to .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
What gets committed to git:
yaml# .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
ticket_id: nw-5c46
loops:
  - id: loop-1
    task: "Implement user schema"
    rubric: { ... }

  - id: loop-2
    task: "Implement POST /auth/login"
    depends_on: [loop-1]
    rubric: { ... }

  - id: loop-3
    task: "Write integration tests"
    depends_on: [loop-2]
    rubric: { ... }

created_at: 2026-02-14T10:00:00Z
This gets git-tracked immediately.
3. Real-time Collaboration (Scope)
Ephemeral state (NOT git-tracked):
json// .scope/loops/loop-1/state.json
{
  "status": "passed",
  "attempts": 2,
  "commit_sha": "a1b2c3d",
  "updated_at": "2026-02-14T10:15:00Z"
}
```

**Collab server broadcasts:**
- Loop status changes (running → passed)
- Attempt progress (attempt 2/5)
- Escalations
- User presence (who's viewing what)

**Git commits only happen:**
- When DAG is created (`.scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml`)
- When loop passes (actual code changes in worktree → merged to main branch)
- NOT for status changes (those are ephemeral)

---

## Why This Works

### Git-tracked (slow, versioned):
- `.tickets/nw-5c46.md` - ticket content
- `.scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml` - DAG structure (created once, rarely changes)
- Actual code changes (when loops pass)

### Real-time (fast, ephemeral):
- Loop status (running, passed, failed, escalated)
- Attempt progress (attempt 2/5)
- Terminal output (live streaming)
- User presence (who's looking at what)

### No conflicts because:
- Ticket content (`.tickets/`) doesn't change during execution
- DAG (`.scope/tickets/`) is created once, then read-only during execution
- Ephemeral state (`.scope/loops/`) is gitignored, synced via WebSocket
- Code changes (from loops) go through normal git merge flow (one loop = one commit)

---

## Collaboration Scenario

**Alice and Bob both working on ticket nw-5c46:**
```
9:00 - Alice: scope start nw-5c46
       └─ Creates .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
       └─ Commits to git
       └─ Pushes

9:05 - Bob: git pull
       └─ Gets .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
       └─ Opens Scope UI
       └─ Sees same DAG as Alice

9:10 - Alice: loop-1 starts
       └─ WebSocket broadcasts: loop-1 status = running
       └─ Bob sees "loop-1 running" in real-time

9:15 - Alice: loop-1 passes
       └─ Git commit (code changes from loop-1)
       └─ WebSocket broadcasts: loop-1 status = passed
       └─ Bob sees "loop-1 passed ✓"

9:20 - Bob: Opens loop-2 inspector
       └─ WebSocket broadcasts: Bob viewing loop-2
       └─ Alice sees "Bob is viewing loop-2" (presence)

9:25 - loop-2 escalates
       └─ Alice clicks "Fix Now"
       └─ Bob sees Alice's presence on escalation
       └─ They coordinate in separate chat

9:30 - Alice fixes escalation, loop-2 passes
       └─ Git commit (code changes)
       └─ WebSocket: loop-2 passed
       └─ Bob sees update
No git conflicts because:

Only code changes get committed (via loop pass → merge)
Status/progress is ephemeral (WebSocket only)
DAG structure was committed once at the start


Summary of Changes
Remove:

❌ tk dep commands
❌ Ticket dependencies in .tickets/*.md

Add:

✅ .scope/tickets/{id}.yaml (git-tracked DAG)
✅ .scope/loops/{id}/state.json (gitignored ephemeral state)
✅ Collab server for real-time sync of ephemeral state

Keep:

✅ tk for ticket content management
✅ Git for versioning tickets + DAGs + code
✅ Real-time collaboration for loop execution


Does this resolve the tension? Git tracks structure, WebSocket syncs execution state.What's a good server tech for this? Elixir? Convex?Feb 14Good question. Let's evaluate:

Requirements for Scope Collab Server
What it needs to do:

WebSocket connections (50-100 concurrent users)
Broadcast events to rooms (project-based)
Minimal state (just routing, no business logic)
Presence tracking (who's viewing what)
Low latency (<100ms event propagation)
Easy to self-host (Docker)

What it does NOT need:

Database (clients own state)
Complex queries
Authentication (v1 is trust-based)
File storage
Heavy computation


Options
1. Elixir/Phoenix ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐
Pros:

Built for this (Phoenix Channels = WebSocket at scale)
Presence tracking is built-in (Phoenix.Presence)
Handles 1M+ WebSocket connections per node
Fault-tolerant (OTP supervision trees)
PubSub is native (Phoenix.PubSub)
Easy clustering (multi-node if needed later)
Self-hosting is trivial (Mix release → Docker)

Cons:

Learning curve if you don't know Elixir
Overkill for <100 users (but future-proof)

Code size: ~200 LOC for full collab server
Example:
elixir# lib/scope_web/channels/project_channel.ex
defmodule ScopeWeb.ProjectChannel do
  use Phoenix.Channel

  def join("project:" <> project_id, _params, socket) do
    send(self(), :after_join)
    {:ok, assign(socket, :project_id, project_id)}
  end

  def handle_in("event", event, socket) do
    broadcast!(socket, "event", event)
    {:noreply, socket}
  end

  def handle_info(:after_join, socket) do
    push(socket, "presence_state", Presence.list(socket))
    {:ok, _} = Presence.track(socket, socket.assigns.user_id, %{
      viewing: nil
    })
    {:noreply, socket}
  end
end
Deployment:
bashmix release
docker build -t scope-collab .
docker run -p 4000:4000 scope-collab
Verdict: Best technical fit, handles scale, built for this exact use case.

2. Convex ⭐⭐⭐
Pros:

Zero backend code (reactive queries + mutations)
Built-in real-time (subscriptions)
TypeScript (same language as frontend)
Hosted (no ops)
Free tier is generous

Cons:

❌ Not self-hostable (deal-breaker for "self-host first" philosophy)
Vendor lock-in
Less control over WebSocket behavior
Overkill (includes database, functions, file storage you don't need)
Costs scale with usage (not flat self-host cost)

Code size: ~100 LOC (simpler than Elixir)
Example:
ts// convex/messages.ts
export const sendEvent = mutation({
  args: { projectId: v.string(), event: v.any() },
  handler: async (ctx, { projectId, event }) => {
    await ctx.db.insert("events", { projectId, event, timestamp: Date.now() });
  },
});

export const subscribeToProject = query({
  args: { projectId: v.string() },
  handler: async (ctx, { projectId }) => {
    return await ctx.db
      .query("events")
      .filter((q) => q.eq(q.field("projectId"), projectId))
      .collect();
  },
});
Verdict: Great for hosted SaaS, but can't self-host. Not aligned with Scope's philosophy.

3. Rust/Axum + tokio-tungstenite ⭐⭐⭐⭐
Pros:

You're already writing Rust (Tauri backend)
Minimal dependencies
Extremely performant (low memory, high throughput)
Full control
Self-hostable (single binary)
No runtime needed

Cons:

More code than Elixir (no built-in Presence, PubSub)
Manual state management (rooms, presence)
No clustering out-of-box (need Redis if multi-node)

Code size: ~400 LOC for full collab server
Example:
rustuse axum::{Router, extract::ws::{WebSocket, WebSocketUpgrade}};
use tokio::sync::broadcast;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() {
    let app = Router::new()
        .route("/ws/:project_id", get(ws_handler));

    axum::Server::bind(&"0.0.0.0:4000".parse().unwrap())
        .serve(app.into_make_service())
        .await
        .unwrap();
}

async fn ws_handler(
    ws: WebSocketUpgrade,
    Path(project_id): Path<String>,
) -> impl IntoResponse {
    ws.on_upgrade(|socket| handle_socket(socket, project_id))
}

async fn handle_socket(mut socket: WebSocket, project_id: String) {
    let (tx, mut rx) = broadcast::channel(100);
    let room_tx = ROOMS.entry(project_id).or_insert(tx).clone();

    // Relay messages
    loop {
        tokio::select! {
            Some(msg) = socket.recv() => {
                room_tx.send(msg);
            }
            Ok(msg) = rx.recv() => {
                socket.send(msg).await;
            }
        }
    }
}
Verdict: Good if you want single-language stack, but more work than Elixir.

4. Node.js + Socket.io ⭐⭐⭐
Pros:

JavaScript/TypeScript (same as frontend)
Socket.io has rooms built-in
Huge ecosystem
Easy to prototype

Cons:

Doesn't scale as well as Elixir/Rust
Memory usage higher than Rust
Socket.io is heavy (fallbacks, polling, etc.)
Less elegant than Phoenix Channels

Code size: ~250 LOC
Example:
tsimport { Server } from 'socket.io';

const io = new Server(4000);

io.on('connection', (socket) => {
  socket.on('join', (projectId) => {
    socket.join(projectId);
  });

  socket.on('event', (projectId, event) => {
    socket.to(projectId).emit('event', event);
  });
});
Verdict: Fine for prototyping, but Elixir/Rust are better long-term.

Privacy-first (code stays local)

Everything else stays local (Tauri/Rust) because:

Desktop UI (native app)
File system access (repos, worktrees)

Verdict

Tauri/Rust: Everything except real-time sync
Elixir/Phoenix: Just WebSocket relay

Don't try to move more to Elixir. It's not designed for desktop apps or local file operations.
The stack is already optimal.Do we need a centralized db for managing tickets? Or is it p2p?Feb 14No centralized DB needed. Here's why:

Tickets Are Already Distributed (Git)
Source of Truth: Git Repository
.tickets/nw-5c46.md    ← Git-tracked
.scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml    ← Git-tracked (DAG)
.scope/loops/*/    ← Gitignored (ephemeral)
Sync mechanism: git pull / git push

Collaboration Model
For Ticket Content (Slow-Changing):
Git is the database:

Alice creates ticket: commits .tickets/nw-5c46.md
Alice pushes to origin
Bob pulls
Bob sees ticket

No central DB needed. Git IS the distributed database.

For Loop Execution State (Fast-Changing):
**Claude's response was interruptedI told you, tickets require realtime collaboration. Ticket files only get tracked along with the loop that gets spawned. Otherwise they are not git tracked.Feb 14Got it. Let me reframe completely:

Revised: Tickets Are NOT Git-Tracked (Until Spawned)
Storage Model
Before spawn:

Tickets live in Elixir/Phoenix server (centralized DB)
Real-time collaboration works (everyone sees updates instantly)
NOT in git yet

After spawn:

Ticket + DAG get committed to git (.scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml)
Now git-tracked (becomes permanent record)
Real-time state continues in server for loop execution


Architecture
Elixir/Phoenix Server Now Owns:

Ticket database (PostgreSQL/Ecto)

Create, read, update, delete tickets
Real-time sync via Phoenix PubSub
All users see changes instantly


WebSocket for real-time

Ticket updates broadcast immediately
Loop status updates
Presence tracking

User clicks "Start Work"
Server serializes ticket → .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
Client commits to git
From that point: ticket is versioned




Flow
1. Create Ticket (Real-time, NOT Git)
Alice creates ticket "Add auth"
  ↓
Saved to Phoenix DB (PostgreSQL)
  ↓
Broadcast via PubSub
  ↓
Bob sees new ticket instantly (no git pull)
2. Edit Ticket (Real-time)
Bob updates description
  ↓
Update in Phoenix DB
  ↓
Broadcast
  ↓
Alice sees change <500ms
3. Spawn Loops (Snapshot to Git)
Alice clicks "Start Work"
  ↓
Phoenix generates DAG
  ↓
Sends to Tauri client: {ticket, dag}
  ↓
Tauri writes .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
  ↓
Tauri commits to git
  ↓
Now permanently tracked
4. Loop Execution (Real-time)
Loops run on Alice's machine
  ↓
Status updates → Phoenix server
  ↓
Broadcast to Bob
  ↓
Bob sees live progress

What Gets Git-Tracked
Only after spawn:
.scope/
└─ tickets/
   └─ nw-5c46.yaml    # Committed when loops spawn
      ├─ ticket: {title, description, acceptance}
      ├─ dag: {loops, dependencies}
      └─ spawned_at: timestamp
Never git-tracked:
# No .tickets/ directory at all
# Tickets live in Phoenix DB only

Data Model
Phoenix (PostgreSQL)
elixir# Schema
defmodule Scope.Ticket do
  schema "tickets" do
    field :title, :string
    field :description, :string
    field :status, :string  # open, in_progress, closed
    field :type, :string
    field :priority, :integer
    field :assignee, :string
    field :acceptance_criteria, {:array, :string}
    field :spawned, :boolean, default: false
    field :spawned_at, :utc_datetime

    timestamps()
  end
end
Git (After Spawn)
yaml# .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
ticket:
  id: nw-5c46
  title: Add authentication
  description: Implement JWT-based auth
  acceptance_criteria:
    - POST /auth/login works
    - Tokens expire after 1 hour

dag:
  loops:
    - id: loop-1
      task: Schema
    - id: loop-2
      task: API
      depends_on: [loop-1]

spawned_at: 2026-02-14T10:00:00Z

Elixir Server Responsibilities
1. Ticket CRUD (Database)
elixir# Create
def create_ticket(attrs) do
  %Ticket{}
  |> Ticket.changeset(attrs)
  |> Repo.insert()
  |> broadcast_ticket_created()
end

# Update
def update_ticket(ticket, attrs) do
  ticket
  |> Ticket.changeset(attrs)
  |> Repo.update()
  |> broadcast_ticket_updated()
end

# List
def list_tickets do
  Repo.all(Ticket)
end
2. Real-time Broadcasting
elixirdefp broadcast_ticket_created({:ok, ticket}) do
  Phoenix.PubSub.broadcast(
    Scope.PubSub,
    "tickets",
    {:ticket_created, ticket}
  )
  {:ok, ticket}
end
3. DAG Generation (When Spawning)
elixirdef spawn_ticket(ticket_id) do
  ticket = Repo.get!(Ticket, ticket_id)

  # Call LLM to decompose
  dag = TaskDecomposer.decompose(ticket)

  # Mark as spawned
  ticket
  |> Ticket.changeset(%{spawned: true, spawned_at: DateTime.utc_now()})
  |> Repo.update!()

  # Return ticket + dag for client to commit to git
  %{ticket: ticket, dag: dag}
end
4. Loop Status Updates
elixirdef update_loop_status(loop_id, status) do
  # Ephemeral, just broadcast
  # Don't persist in DB
  Phoenix.PubSub.broadcast(
    Scope.PubSub,
    "loops",
    {:loop_status_changed, loop_id, status}
  )
end

Tauri Client Changes
On Spawn:
rust#[tauri::command]
async fn start_work(ticket_id: String) -> Result<()> {
    // 1. Call Phoenix server
    let response = http_client
        .post("http://localhost:4000/api/tickets/{ticket_id}/spawn")
        .send()
        .await?;

    let spawn_data: SpawnData = response.json().await?;

    // 2. Write to git
    let yaml = serde_yaml::to_string(&spawn_data)?;
    std::fs::write(
        format!(".scope/tickets/{}.yaml", ticket_id),
        yaml
    )?;

    // 3. Git commit
    Command::new("git")
        .args(&["add", ".scope/tickets"])
        .status()?;

    Command::new("git")
        .args(&["commit", "-m", &format!("Spawn ticket {}", ticket_id)])
        .status()?;

    // 4. Start loops locally
    for loop in spawn_data.dag.loops {
        start_loop(loop).await?;
    }

    Ok(())
}
```

---

## Phoenix vs P2P?

**Phoenix (Centralized) Wins:**

### Why NOT P2P:
- ❌ Complex peer discovery
- ❌ Conflict resolution nightmare (CRDTs for every field)
- ❌ No "source of truth" when peers disagree
- ❌ Offline edits = merge hell
- ❌ Can't query "all tickets" without asking every peer

### Why Phoenix:
- ✅ Simple (PostgreSQL = source of truth)
- ✅ Fast queries (SQL, not gossip protocol)
- ✅ Real-time built-in (PubSub)
- ✅ Offline: cache locally, sync when reconnect
- ✅ No conflicts (server is authoritative)
- ✅ Self-hostable (one Phoenix server for team)

---

## Deployment Options

### Option 1: Hosted Phoenix (SaaS)
- You run Phoenix server
- Users connect to `collab.scope.dev`
- Free tier for small teams
- Paid for large teams

### Option 2: Self-Hosted
- User runs Phoenix server (Docker)
- Team connects to internal server
- All data stays on-prem

### Option 3: Local-Only (Fallback)
- No Phoenix server
- Tickets in local SQLite
- No real-time collaboration
- Works offline always

---

## Final Architecture
```
┌─────────────────────────────────────┐

│  • Git operations                   │
│  • Commits .scope/ on spawn         │
│                                     │
└──────────────┬──────────────────────┘
               │ HTTP + WebSocket
               ↓
┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Phoenix Server (Elixir)             │
│                                      │
│  • Ticket DB (PostgreSQL)            │
│  • Real-time PubSub                  │
│  • DAG generation                    │
│  • WebSocket broadcasting            │
│                                      │
└──────────────────────────────────────┘

Summary
Tickets:

Live in Phoenix/PostgreSQL (NOT git)
Real-time collaboration via PubSub
Get snapshots to git only when spawned

Loops:

Execute locally (Tauri)
Status syncs via Phoenix WebSocket
Code changes committed to git when loops pass

Git tracks:

.scope/tickets/*.yaml (after spawn)
Code changes (from loop merges)
NOT live ticket edits

Phoenix owns:

Ticket database
Real-time sync
DAG generation

Auto-Detection
Tauri app checks on startup:
rust#[tauri::command]
async fn initialize() -> AppMode {
    // Try to connect to Phoenix server
    match try_connect_to_server().await {
        Ok(_) => AppMode::Collaborative,
        Err(_) => {
            println!("No Phoenix server found, running in solo mode");
            AppMode::Solo
        }
    }
}

async fn try_connect_to_server() -> Result<()> {
    // Check configured server URL or default
    let server_url = get_config_or_default("http://localhost:4000");

    // Try HTTP ping
    let response = timeout(
        Duration::from_secs(2),
        http_client.get(&format!("{}/health", server_url)).send()
    ).await;

    match response {
        Ok(Ok(_)) => Ok(()),
        _ => Err(anyhow!("Server unreachable"))
    }
}

Data Layer Abstraction
Single interface, two implementations:
rust// Trait for ticket storage
#[async_trait]
trait TicketStore {
    async fn create_ticket(&self, ticket: NewTicket) -> Result<Ticket>;
    async fn list_tickets(&self) -> Result<Vec<Ticket>>;
    async fn get_ticket(&self, id: &str) -> Result<Ticket>;
    async fn update_ticket(&self, id: &str, updates: TicketUpdate) -> Result<Ticket>;
    async fn delete_ticket(&self, id: &str) -> Result<()>;

    // Subscribe to changes (for real-time updates)
    fn subscribe(&self) -> Receiver<TicketEvent>;
}

// Implementation 1: Phoenix Server (Collaborative)
struct PhoenixTicketStore {
    client: HttpClient,
    ws: WebSocket,
    event_rx: Receiver<TicketEvent>,
}

impl TicketStore for PhoenixTicketStore {
    async fn create_ticket(&self, ticket: NewTicket) -> Result<Ticket> {
        // POST to Phoenix server
        let response = self.client
            .post("http://localhost:4000/api/tickets")
            .json(&ticket)
            .send()
            .await?;

        response.json().await
    }

    fn subscribe(&self) -> Receiver<TicketEvent> {
        // Return WebSocket receiver (real-time updates)
        self.event_rx.clone()
    }

    // ... other methods call Phoenix API
}

// Implementation 2: SQLite (Solo)
struct LocalTicketStore {
    db: SqliteConnection,
    event_tx: Sender<TicketEvent>,
    event_rx: Receiver<TicketEvent>,
}

impl TicketStore for LocalTicketStore {
    async fn create_ticket(&self, ticket: NewTicket) -> Result<Ticket> {
        // Insert into SQLite
        let ticket = sqlx::query_as!(
            Ticket,
            r#"
            INSERT INTO tickets (id, title, description, status, type, priority)
            VALUES (?, ?, ?, ?, ?, ?)
            RETURNING *
            "#,
            ticket.id,
            ticket.title,
            ticket.description,
            "open",
            ticket.type_,
            ticket.priority
        )
        .fetch_one(&self.db)
        .await?;

        // Emit event locally (for UI reactivity)
        self.event_tx.send(TicketEvent::Created(ticket.clone()));

        Ok(ticket)
    }

    fn subscribe(&self) -> Receiver<TicketEvent> {
        // Return local channel (no network, just UI updates)
        self.event_rx.clone()
    }

    // ... other methods use SQLite
}

App Initialization
rust// src-tauri/src/main.rs

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() {
    let ticket_store: Box<dyn TicketStore> = match try_connect_to_server().await {
        Ok(ws) => {
            println!("✓ Connected to Phoenix server (collaborative mode)");
            Box::new(PhoenixTicketStore::new(ws))
        }
        Err(_) => {
            println!("✓ Running in solo mode (local SQLite)");
            Box::new(LocalTicketStore::new().await)
        }
    };

    tauri::Builder::default()
        .manage(ticket_store)
        .invoke_handler(tauri::generate_handler![
            create_ticket,
            list_tickets,
            // ...
        ])
        .run(tauri::generate_context!())
        .expect("error while running tauri application");
}

#[tauri::command]
async fn create_ticket(
    store: State<'_, Box<dyn TicketStore>>,
    ticket: NewTicket,
) -> Result<Ticket, String> {
    store.create_ticket(ticket)
        .await
        .map_err(|e| e.to_string())
}

UI Indication
Show mode in app header:
tsxfunction AppHeader() {
    const [mode, setMode] = useState<'solo' | 'collaborative'>('solo');

    useEffect(() => {
        invoke('get_mode').then(setMode);
    }, []);

    return (

                </Badge>

                    Collaborative Mode
                    <OnlineIndicator />
                </Badge>
            )}
        </header>
    );
}

Migration Path (Solo → Collaborative)
User starts solo, later wants to collaborate:
rust#[tauri::command]
async fn migrate_to_collaborative(server_url: String) -> Result<()> {
    // 1. Export tickets from SQLite
    let local_store = LocalTicketStore::new().await;
    let tickets = local_store.list_tickets().await?;

    // 2. Connect to Phoenix server
    let phoenix_store = PhoenixTicketStore::connect(&server_url).await?;

    // 3. Upload all tickets
    for ticket in tickets {
        phoenix_store.create_ticket(ticket.into()).await?;
    }

    // 4. Update config to use Phoenix server
    save_config("server_url", &server_url)?;

    // 5. Restart app (or hot-swap store)
    println!("Migration complete! Restart to use collaborative mode.");

    Ok(())
}
UI:
tsxfunction Settings() {
    async function enableCollaboration() {
        const serverUrl = prompt("Enter Phoenix server URL:");
        await invoke('migrate_to_collaborative', { serverUrl });
        alert("Migrated to collaborative mode! Restart the app.");
    }

    return (
        <div>
            <h2>Collaboration</h2>
            {mode === 'solo' && (
                <button onClick={enableCollaboration}>
                    Enable Team Collaboration
                </button>
            )}
        </div>
    );
}

Reverse Migration (Collaborative → Solo)
User wants to go offline:
rust#[tauri::command]
async fn go_offline() -> Result<()> {
    // 1. Sync all tickets from Phoenix to SQLite
    let phoenix_store = PhoenixTicketStore::current();
    let tickets = phoenix_store.list_tickets().await?;

    let local_store = LocalTicketStore::new().await;
    for ticket in tickets {
        local_store.create_ticket(ticket.into()).await?;
    }

    // 2. Remove server URL from config
    remove_config("server_url")?;

    println!("Switched to solo mode. You can work offline now.");

    Ok(())
}

Does this work?There must be more seamless local first waysFeb 14You're right. Let's go fully local-first.

Local-First Architecture (No Phoenix DB)
Core Principle: Git IS the Database
All ticket data lives in git-tracked files from the start:
.scope/
├─ tickets/
│  ├─ nw-5c46.yaml          # Git-tracked from creation
│  ├─ nw-7e8f.yaml
│  └─ nw-9a0b.yaml
│
└─ loops/                    # Gitignored (ephemeral)
   └─ loop-abc123/
Sync mechanism: Git (pull/push)
Real-time collaboration: Phoenix server is just a relay for live updates, not storage.

How It Works
1. Create Ticket (Local First)
rust// User creates ticket
#[tauri::command]
fn create_ticket(ticket: NewTicket) -> Result<String> {
    let id = generate_id();  // e.g., "nw-5c46"

    // Write to local file immediately
    let ticket_data = Ticket {
        id: id.clone(),
        title: ticket.title,
        status: "open",
        spawned: false,
        created_at: Utc::now(),
        // ...
    };

    let yaml = serde_yaml::to_string(&ticket_data)?;
    std::fs::write(
        format!(".scope/tickets/{}.yaml", id),
        yaml
    )?;

    // Git commit (auto)
    git_add_and_commit(&format!("Create ticket {}", id))?;

    // Optional: Broadcast to Phoenix relay (if connected)
    if let Some(ws) = get_phoenix_connection() {
        ws.send(Event::TicketCreated { ticket: ticket_data });
    }

    Ok(id)
}
Result:

Ticket saved locally (.scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml)
Git commit created
If online: other users get live update via Phoenix
If offline: changes sync when you git push


2. Edit Ticket (Local First)
rust#[tauri::command]
fn update_ticket(id: String, updates: TicketUpdate) -> Result<()> {
    // Read from file
    let path = format!(".scope/tickets/{}.yaml", id);
    let yaml = std::fs::read_to_string(&path)?;
    let mut ticket: Ticket = serde_yaml::from_str(&yaml)?;

    // Update
    ticket.apply_updates(updates.clone());
    ticket.updated_at = Utc::now();

    // Write back
    let yaml = serde_yaml::to_string(&ticket)?;
    std::fs::write(&path, yaml)?;

    // Git commit (auto)
    git_add_and_commit(&format!("Update ticket {}", id))?;

    // Optional: Broadcast to Phoenix relay
    if let Some(ws) = get_phoenix_connection() {
        ws.send(Event::TicketUpdated { id, updates });
    }

    Ok(())
}

3. Real-Time Sync (Phoenix Relay)
Phoenix server doesn't store tickets, just relays events:
elixir# Phoenix Channel
defmodule ScopeWeb.ProjectChannel do
  use Phoenix.Channel

  # User joins project room
  def join("project:" <> project_id, _params, socket) do
    {:ok, assign(socket, :project_id, project_id)}
  end

  # User sends ticket event
  def handle_in("ticket_event", event, socket) do
    # Just broadcast to other users in room
    # Don't persist anything
    broadcast!(socket, "ticket_event", event)
    {:noreply, socket}
  end
end
Client receives event:
rust// WebSocket message received
fn on_phoenix_event(event: TicketEvent) {
    match event {
        TicketEvent::TicketUpdated { id, updates } => {
            // Another user updated ticket
            // Pull from git to get latest
            git_pull()?;

            // Notify UI to refresh
            emit_to_frontend(Event::TicketChanged { id });
        }
    }
}
```

---

## Conflict Resolution

### Scenario: Alice and Bob Edit Same Ticket

**Traditional DB:** Last write wins or complex CRDT

**Git-based:** Standard git merge
```
Alice (offline):
  ├─ Edits nw-5c46.yaml: status = "in_progress"
  └─ Commits locally

Bob (online):
  ├─ Edits nw-5c46.yaml: assignee = "bob"
  ├─ Commits + pushes
  └─ Phoenix broadcasts: ticket updated

Alice comes online:
  ├─ git pull
  ├─ CONFLICT in .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
  └─ User resolves (same as normal git workflow)
```

**Auto-merge works for most cases:**
- Different fields changed → git auto-merges
- Same field changed → conflict marker, user chooses

---

## Phoenix Server Role (Optional Relay)

### With Phoenix (Online):
```
Alice edits ticket
  ↓
Write to .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
  ↓
Git commit (local)
  ↓
Send event to Phoenix relay
  ↓
Phoenix broadcasts to Bob
  ↓
Bob gets notification: "Alice updated ticket"
  ↓
Bob runs git pull (gets latest)
  ↓
Bob's UI refreshes
```

### Without Phoenix (Offline):
```
Alice edits ticket
  ↓
Write to .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
  ↓
Git commit (local)
  ↓
(No broadcast, Alice working solo)
  ↓
Later: git push
  ↓
Bob runs git pull manually
  ↓
Bob sees changes
```

## Data Flow
```
┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│   Alice's Machine                   │
│                                     │
│   .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml       │
│   (Git-tracked)                     │
│         ↓                           │
│   Git commit                        │
│         ↓                           │
│   Git push (when ready)             │
└──────────┬──────────────────────────┘
           │
           ↓
┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
│   Git Remote (GitHub/GitLab)         │
│   (Source of truth)                  │
└──────────┬───────────────────────────┘
           │
           ↓
┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
│   Bob's Machine                      │
│                                      │
│   Git pull                           │
│         ↓                            │
│   .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml        │
│   (Updated)                          │
└──────────────────────────────────────┘

           ┌────────────────────┐
           │  Phoenix Server    │
           │  (Optional relay)  │
           │                    │
           │  Broadcasts:       │
           │  "Alice updated    │
           │   ticket nw-5c46"  │
           │                    │
           │  (Doesn't store    │
           │   ticket data)     │
           └────────────────────┘

Auto-Pull Strategy
Instead of polling git, use Phoenix as notification:
rust// When Phoenix says "ticket changed"
async fn on_ticket_changed(ticket_id: String) {
    // Pull from git
    let output = Command::new("git")
        .arg("pull")
        .arg("--rebase")
        .output()?;

    if output.status.success() {
        // Reload ticket from file
        let ticket = read_ticket(&ticket_id)?;

        // Notify UI
        emit_to_frontend(Event::TicketUpdated { ticket });
    } else {
        // Conflict - notify user
        emit_to_frontend(Event::ConflictDetected { ticket_id });
    }
}

Just a relay + presence tracker:
elixirdefmodule ScopeWeb.ProjectChannel do

  def join("project:" <> project_id, %{"user_id" => user_id}, socket) do
    send(self(), :after_join)

  def handle_info(:after_join, socketClaude's response was interruptedNo git as DB. Get it straight. I will not repeat it again. Local first sqlite <-> phoenix sync. There's also electric sql etcFeb 14Got it. SQLite local-first with server sync.

Architecture: Local-First with Sync
Local Storage: SQLite

All tickets in local SQLite (.scope/scope.db)
Works offline completely
Instant reads/writes (no network)

Server: Phoenix + PostgreSQL

Optional sync target
CRDTs or last-write-wins for conflict resolution
Real-time broadcasts

Sync Libraries to Consider:

ElectricSQL - Postgres ↔ SQLite sync
PowerSync - Similar to Electric
Custom sync - Phoenix + SQLite with event log


Recommended: ElectricSQL
Why ElectricSQL:

Postgres ↔ SQLite bidirectional sync
Conflict-free (CRDT-based)
Real-time (WebSocket subscriptions)
Works offline (local SQLite)
Open source, self-hostable


Architecture with ElectricSQL
┌─────────────────────────────────┐
│  Tauri App (Client)             │
│                                 │
│  ┌───────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  SQLite (.scope/scope.db) │  │
│  │  • tickets                │  │
│  │  • loops (ephemeral)      │  │
│  └───────────┬───────────────┘  │
│              │                  │
│              │ ElectricSQL      │
│              │ Client           │
└──────────────┼──────────────────┘
               │
               │ WebSocket
               │ (sync protocol)
               ↓
┌──────────────────────────────────┐
│  Electric Sync Service           │
│  (Elixir/Postgres)               │
│                                  │
│  ┌────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  PostgreSQL                │  │
│  │  • tickets (replicated)    │  │
│  └────────────────────────────┘  │
└──────────────────────────────────┘

How It Works
1. Create Ticket (Local-First)
rust// User creates ticket in Tauri
#[tauri::command]
async fn create_ticket(ticket: NewTicket) -> Result<Ticket> {
    // Write to local SQLite immediately
    let ticket = sqlx::query_as!(
        Ticket,
        r#"
        INSERT INTO tickets (id, title, description, status, type, priority)
        VALUES (?, ?, ?, 'open', ?, ?)
        RETURNING *
        "#,
        generate_id(),
        ticket.title,
        ticket.description,
        ticket.type_,
        ticket.priority
    )
    .fetch_one(&local_db)
    .await?;

    // ElectricSQL client automatically syncs to server
    // (happens in background, no blocking)

    Ok(ticket)
}
What happens:

Write to local SQLite (instant)
ElectricSQL detects change (triggers)
Sends to Electric sync service (background)
Postgres gets updated
Other clients receive update via WebSocket
Their local SQLite updates automatically

User sees: Instant (no waiting for server)

2. Edit Ticket (Offline Works)
rust#[tauri::command]
async fn update_ticket(id: String, status: String) -> Result<()> {
    // Update local SQLite
    sqlx::query!(
        "UPDATE tickets SET status = ?, updated_at = ? WHERE id = ?",
        status,
        Utc::now(),
        id
    )
    .execute(&local_db)
    .await?;

    // ElectricSQL queues sync (works offline)
    // Will sync when connection restored

    Ok(())
}
```

**Offline:**
- Changes saved to local SQLite
- ElectricSQL queues operations
- When online: syncs automatically

---

### 3. Real-Time Updates (When Online)

**Alice edits ticket:**
```
Alice's SQLite: UPDATE tickets SET status = 'in_progress'
    ↓
ElectricSQL client: Detects change
    ↓
Sends to Electric sync service
    ↓
Postgres: Updated
    ↓
Broadcasts to Bob's ElectricSQL client
    ↓
Bob's SQLite: Automatically updated
    ↓
Bob's UI: Reactively updates (no manual refresh)

SQLite Schema
sql-- .scope/scope.db

CREATE TABLE tickets (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    title TEXT NOT NULL,
    description TEXT,
    status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'open',
    type TEXT NOT NULL,
    priority INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 2,
    assignee TEXT,
    acceptance_criteria TEXT, -- JSON
    spawned BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE,
    spawned_at TIMESTAMP,

    -- ElectricSQL metadata
    created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    updated_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,

    -- CRDT metadata (managed by ElectricSQL)
    _electric_id TEXT,
    _electric_version INTEGER
);

-- ElectricSQL handles conflict resolution automatically
```

---

## Conflict Resolution

**Scenario: Alice and Bob both edit offline**
```
Alice (offline):
  UPDATE tickets SET assignee = 'alice' WHERE id = 'nw-5c46'

Bob (offline):
  UPDATE tickets SET status = 'in_progress' WHERE id = 'nw-5c46'

Both come online:
  ↓
ElectricSQL syncs both changes
  ↓
CRDT merge: { assignee: 'alice', status: 'in_progress' }
  ↓
Both clients converge to same state
ElectricSQL uses CRDTs:

Last-write-wins for simple fields
Automatic conflict resolution
No manual merge needed


Setup: ElectricSQL Integration
1. Tauri Side (Rust)
rust// src-tauri/src/electric.rs

use electric_sql::client::Electric;
use sqlx::SqlitePool;

pub async fn init_electric() -> Result<Electric> {
    // Create local SQLite database
    let db = SqlitePool::connect("sqlite:.scope/scope.db").await?;

    // Apply migrations
    sqlx::migrate!("./migrations").run(&db).await?;

    // Connect to Electric sync service
    let electric = Electric::connect(ElectricConfig {
        db,
        server_url: "ws://localhost:5133", // Electric service
        auth_token: get_auth_token(),
    }).await?;

    // Subscribe to tickets table
    electric.sync_table("tickets").await?;

    Ok(electric)
}
2. Electric Sync Service (Elixir/Postgres)
bash# Deploy Electric (Docker)
docker run -p 5133:5133 \
  -e DATABASE_URL=postgresql://localhost/scope \
  electricsql/electric:latest
Electric sits between Postgres and clients:

Listens to Postgres logical replication
Broadcasts changes via WebSocket to clients
Receives client changes, writes to Postgres


Alternative: PowerSync
Similar to Electric, slightly different API:
rust// PowerSync client
use powersync::PowerSync;

let ps = PowerSync::new(PowerSyncConfig {
    database_path: ".scope/scope.db",
    sync_url: "https://sync.yourdomain.com",
    schema: include_str!("schema.json"),
}).await?;

// Auto-sync
ps.connect().await?;

Alternative: Custom Sync (More Control)
If you don't want third-party dependency:
Custom Sync Protocol
rust// Event-sourced sync

// 1. Track all changes in event log
CREATE TABLE _sync_events (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
    table_name TEXT,
    record_id TEXT,
    operation TEXT, -- INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE
    data TEXT, -- JSON
    timestamp INTEGER,
    synced BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE
);

// 2. Trigger on ticket changes
CREATE TRIGGER ticket_changes_trigger
AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ON tickets
BEGIN
    INSERT INTO _sync_events (table_name, record_id, operation, data, timestamp)
    VALUES ('tickets', NEW.id, 'UPDATE', json_object(...), unixepoch());
END;

// 3. Sync worker
async fn sync_loop() {
    loop {
        // Get unsynced events
        let events = get_unsynced_events().await;

        // Send to Phoenix
        for event in events {
            ws.send(Event::Sync(event)).await;
        }

        // Mark as synced
        mark_synced(events).await;

        tokio::time::sleep(Duration::from_secs(5)).await;
    }
}
Phoenix Receives Events
elixirdef handle_in("sync_event", event, socket) do
    # Apply to Postgres
    apply_event(event)

    # Broadcast to other clients
    broadcast!(socket, "sync_event", event)

    {:noreply, socket}
end
```

---

## Recommendation: Start Simple, Upgrade Later

### Phase 1: SQLite Only (Solo)
- Local SQLite (`.scope/scope.db`)
- No server needed
- Works offline
- Ships in MVP

### Phase 2: Add Phoenix Relay (Team)
- Custom sync protocol (event log)
- Phoenix broadcasts changes
- Manual conflict resolution (last-write-wins)
- Good enough for small teams

### Phase 3: Add ElectricSQL (Scale)
- Replace custom sync with Electric
- CRDT conflict resolution
- Handles offline better
- Production-ready for large teams

---

## File Structure
```
.scope/
├─ scope.db              # SQLite (local-first)
│  └─ Tables: tickets, loops, _sync_events
│
├─ tickets/              # Git-tracked snapshots (after spawn)
│  └─ nw-5c46.yaml      # Committed when loops start
│
└─ loops/                # Gitignored (ephemeral state)
   └─ loop-abc123/

Git Still Used For:
✅ Code changes (when loops pass)
✅ Snapshot of ticket+DAG (when spawned)
✅ Team can see "what was worked on" in git history
❌ NOT used for live ticket updates
❌ NOT used for real-time sync
❌ NOT the "database"

Summary
Storage:

Local: SQLite (.scope/scope.db)
Server: PostgreSQL (via Phoenix)

Sync:

Option 1: ElectricSQL (recommended, production-ready)
Option 2: PowerSync (alternative)
Option 3: Custom (simpler, MVP-friendly)

Modes:

Solo: SQLite only, no server
Team: SQLite + Phoenix sync

Offline:

Fully functional (all data in SQLite)
Syncs when reconnected

Conflicts:

ElectricSQL: CRDT auto-merge
Custom: Last-write-wins or manual resolution

Does this finally match your vision?Why phase 2? Why not phase 3 directly?Feb 14You're right. Skip Phase 2, go straight to ElectricSQL.

Why Jump to ElectricSQL Immediately
Phase 2 (Custom Sync) Problems:

❌ Have to build sync protocol from scratch
❌ Manual conflict resolution (buggy, complex)
❌ Event log management (ordering, deduplication)
❌ Reconnection logic (missed events, backfill)
❌ Partial sync bugs (what if sync fails halfway?)
❌ Will eventually want CRDTs anyway
❌ Basically rebuilding what Electric already does

ElectricSQL Advantages:

✅ Sync protocol is solved (Postgres ↔ SQLite)
✅ CRDTs built-in (conflict-free)
✅ Offline-first by design
✅ Production-ready
✅ Open source, self-hostable
✅ Active development
✅ Less code for you to maintain


Revised Plan: Two Phases Only
Phase 1: Solo Mode (SQLite Only)
Build time: Week 1-4
User's Machine:
└─ .scope/scope.db (SQLite)
   └─ All tickets, loops, state

No server needed.
What works:

All ticket CRUD
Spawn loops
Loop execution
Everything offline

Limitations:

No collaboration
One user only


Phase 2: Team Mode (Add ElectricSQL)
Build time: Week 5-6
User's Machine:
└─ .scope/scope.db (SQLite)
   └─ ElectricSQL client
      ↓ sync

Electric Sync Service (self-hosted):
└─ PostgreSQL
   └─ All synced data
What's added:

Real-time sync (SQLite ↔ Postgres)
Multiple users see changes instantly
Offline still works (queues sync)
CRDT conflict resolution

Migration from Phase 1:
rust// User enables team mode
#[tauri::command]
async fn enable_team_mode(server_url: String) -> Result<()> {
    // Initialize ElectricSQL client
    let electric = Electric::connect(ElectricConfig {
        db: existing_sqlite_db,
        server_url, // "ws://sync.yourteam.com"
    }).await?;

    // Start syncing
    electric.sync_table("tickets").await?;

    // Done! Existing SQLite data uploads to server
    Ok(())
}

Why This Is Better
No wasted effort:

Don't build custom sync (Phase 2) then throw it away
ElectricSQL is the endgame, just start there

Simpler codebase:

Phase 1: SQLite queries
Phase 2: SQLite queries + Electric client (add ~200 LOC)
vs. Phase 2 custom: SQLite + event log + sync worker + conflict resolution (~1000 LOC you delete later)

Faster to production:

Week 5: Enable ElectricSQL
vs. Week 5-8: Build custom sync, Week 9-10: Throw it away and add Electric


ElectricSQL Integration (Phase 2)
Install Electric Client
toml# Cargo.toml
[dependencies]
electric-sql = "0.10"
sqlx = { version = "0.7", features = ["sqlite", "runtime-tokio"] }
Initialize in Tauri
rust// src-tauri/src/electric.rs

use electric_sql::Electric;

pub async fn init_electric(server_url: Option<String>) -> Result<Electric> {
    let db = SqlitePool::connect("sqlite:.scope/scope.db").await?;

    let electric = if let Some(url) = server_url {
        // Team mode
        Electric::connect(ElectricConfig {
            db,
            server_url: url,
        }).await?
    } else {
        // Solo mode (local only)
        Electric::local_only(db).await?
    };

    // Enable sync for tickets table
    electric.sync_table("tickets").await?;

    Ok(electric)
}
Deploy Electric Sync Service
yaml# docker-compose.yml
version: '3.8'
services:
  postgres:
    image: postgres:15
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: scope
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: postgres
    volumes:
      - postgres_data:/var/lib/postgresql/data

  electric:
    image: electricsql/electric:latest
    ports:
      - "5133:5133"
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgresql://postgres:postgres@postgres:5432/scope
      ELECTRIC_WRITE_TO_PG_MODE: logical_replication
    depends_on:
      - postgres

volumes:
  postgres_data:
Run:
bashdocker-compose up -d
Users connect to: ws://yourteam.com:5133

Configuration
yaml# .scope/config.yaml

mode: solo  # or: team

# Team mode config:
team:
  sync_url: ws://sync.yourteam.com:5133
  # or for SaaS: wss://sync.scope.dev

Cost Comparison
Custom Sync (Phase 2):

Development: 2 weeks (build event log, sync protocol, conflict resolution)
Maintenance: Ongoing (bugs, edge cases, scaling issues)
Lines of code: ~1000 (eventually thrown away)

ElectricSQL (Phase 3 directly):

Development: 2 days (integrate client, deploy Electric)
Maintenance: Minimal (Electric team maintains sync engine)
Lines of code: ~200 (Electric client integration)

Savings: 12 days of work + ongoing maintenance burden

Rollout Plan
Week 1-4: Solo Mode
rust// Just SQLite
let db = SqlitePool::connect("sqlite:.scope/scope.db").await?;

// All queries direct to SQLite
sqlx::query!("SELECT * FROM tickets").fetch_all(&db).await?;
Week 5: Add ElectricSQL
rust// Initialize Electric (wraps SQLite)
let electric = init_electric(config.team.sync_url).await?;

// Queries still look the same!
// But now they auto-sync in background
sqlx::query!("SELECT * FROM tickets").fetch_all(&electric.db).await?;
```

**No rewrite needed.** Electric wraps your existing SQLite queries.

---

## Final Architecture
```
┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Phase 1: Solo                      │
│                                     │
│  SQLite (.scope/scope.db)           │
│  └─ All data local                  │
│  └─ No server                       │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

            ↓ (upgrade to team)

┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Phase 2: Team (ElectricSQL)        │
│                                     │
│  SQLite (.scope/scope.db)           │
│  └─ ElectricSQL Client              │
│      ↓ sync                         │
│      Electric Service               │
│      └─ PostgreSQL                  │
│      ↓ broadcast                    │
│  All team members                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

Decision
Skip custom sync entirely. Phase 1 → Phase 2 (ElectricSQL) directly.
Why:

Saves 2 weeks development
Avoids maintenance burden
Production-ready from day one (team mode)
No throwaway code

MVP Scope (Keep Minimal)
What Ships in MVP:
Tickets:

✅ Title
✅ Description (plain text)
✅ Status (open/in_progress/closed)
✅ Type (bug/feature/task)
✅ Priority (0-4)
✅ Assignee
✅ Acceptance criteria (list of strings)

Loops:

✅ Spawn from ticket
✅ Maker/checker agents
✅ DAG visualization
✅ Escalation handling
✅ Real-time status

Collaboration:

✅ Real-time ticket updates (ElectricSQL)
✅ Presence (who's viewing what)
✅ Loop status sync


Post-MVP Features
v1.1: Comments
Why later:

Not critical for core loop execution
Adds complexity (threading, mentions, reactions)
Can use external chat (Slack, Discord) for now

Schema addition:
sqlCREATE TABLE comments (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    ticket_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    user_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    content TEXT NOT NULL,
    created_at TIMESTAMP,
    FOREIGN KEY (ticket_id) REFERENCES tickets(id)
);

v1.1: Multimedia (Images, Files)
Why later:

Storage complexity (S3? Local files? Git LFS?)
Sync complexity (large files over ElectricSQL?)
Not needed for agent workflows (agents don't read images in tickets)

Use case:

User uploads screenshot of bug
Attach design mockup to feature ticket

Implementation:

File storage: S3 or local .scope/attachments/
Database: Just store references

sqlCREATE TABLE attachments (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    ticket_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    filename TEXT NOT NULL,
    url TEXT NOT NULL, -- S3 URL or local path

);

Can use tickets without projects initially
Adds navigation complexity (project switcher)
Can use git repos as "projects" for now

    name TEXT NOT NULL,
    description TEXT
);

v1.2: Milestones
Why later:

Not critical for individual ticket execution
Grouping/filtering can be done manually initially
Linear has this, but teams often don't use it

Schema:
sqlCREATE TABLE milestones (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    name TEXT NOT NULL,
    due_date DATE,
    status TEXT
);

ALTER TABLE tickets ADD COLUMN milestone_id TEXT REFERENCES milestones(id);

Why later:

Estimates don't affect agent execution
Can track time manually for now
Needs: time tracking, burndown charts, velocity

Schema:
sqlALTER TABLE tickets ADD COLUMN estimate_hours REAL;

    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,

MVP SQLite Schema (Minimal)
sql-- Tickets (core)
CREATE TABLE tickets (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    title TEXT NOT NULL,
    description TEXT,
    status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'open',
    type TEXT NOT NULL,
    priority INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 2,
    assignee TEXT,
    acceptance_criteria TEXT, -- JSON array
    spawned BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE,
    spawned_at TIMESTAMP,
    created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    updated_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
);

-- Loops (ephemeral, not synced)
CREATE TABLE loops (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    ticket_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    task TEXT NOT NULL,
    status TEXT NOT NULL,
    attempts INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    commit_sha TEXT,
    created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    FOREIGN KEY (ticket_id) REFERENCES tickets(id)
);
