Does this make sense?Okay so how should I build this? Product Doc, System Design Doc, Per component spec ? Don't generate anything, just teach me how to structure a large project like thisFeb 14Good question. Here's how to structure a large project build:

Document Hierarchy (Top-Down)
1. Product Requirements Doc (PRD) - What & Why

Audience: Everyone (stakeholders, engineers, designers)
Purpose: Define the product vision, goals, features
What it contains:

Problem statement
User stories
Feature list (MVP vs post-MVP)
Success metrics
Non-goals


When to write: Before anything else
Status: ✅ You have this (we just wrote it together)


2. System Design Doc (SDD) - How (High-Level)

Audience: Engineers (backend, frontend, infra)
Purpose: Architecture decisions, component boundaries, data flow
What it contains:

Architecture diagram (boxes and arrows)
Technology choices (Tauri, Elixir, SQLite, ElectricSQL)
Data models (database schemas)
API contracts (Tauri commands, WebSocket events)
Deployment model (solo vs team mode)
Trade-offs and alternatives considered


When to write: After PRD, before coding
Output: Engineers know what to build and how it fits together


3. Technical Specs (Per Component) - How (Detailed)

Audience: Engineer implementing that component
Purpose: Implementation details for a single component
What it contains:

Component API (inputs, outputs, signatures)
Internal logic (algorithms, state machines)
Dependencies (what it calls, what calls it)
Error handling
Test cases
Edge cases


When to write: Before implementing each component
Examples:

"Loop Executor Spec"
"ElectricSQL Integration Spec"
"PTY Bridge Spec"




When to Write What
Phase 1: Planning (Before Code)

PRD (1-2 days)

Finalize feature scope
Define success metrics
Get alignment on MVP


System Design Doc (2-3 days)

Draw architecture diagrams
Choose tech stack
Define database schema
API contracts (Tauri commands, events)
Deployment strategy



Phase 2: Implementation (During Code)

Component Specs (Write as you go)

Before building each major component
Can be lightweight (1-2 pages)
Helps clarify thinking before coding




What Each Document Prevents
Without PRD:

❌ Feature creep (everyone adds pet features)
❌ Unclear goals (what does success look like?)
❌ Scope confusion (is this MVP or v2?)

Without System Design:

❌ Incompatible components (they don't fit together)
❌ Tech debt (make wrong architecture choices)
❌ Rewrites (realize design is flawed after building)

Without Component Specs:

❌ Implementation thrashing (start coding, get stuck, refactor)
❌ Missing edge cases (didn't think through error handling)
❌ Interface mismatches (components don't integrate cleanly)


How Detailed Should Each Be?
PRD: High-level

✅ "User can create tickets"
✅ "Tickets sync in real-time"
❌ NOT: "Tickets use ElectricSQL with CRDT conflict resolution"

System Design: Medium-detail

✅ "SQLite local storage, ElectricSQL for sync"
✅ "Tauri commands: create_ticket(title, desc) → ticket_id"
❌ NOT: "Function signature: async fn create_ticket(...) -> Result<String>"

Component Spec: Detailed

✅ "Function signature: async fn create_ticket(...) -> Result<String>"
✅ "Error cases: duplicate ID → return Err(AlreadyExists)"
✅ "Calls ElectricSQL sync after SQLite insert"


Template Structures
PRD Template:
markdown# Product Requirements: [Feature Name]

## Problem
[What problem are we solving?]

## Goals
- [Primary goal]
- [Secondary goal]

## User Stories
As a [user], I want [feature] so that [benefit].

## Features (MVP)
- Feature 1
- Feature 2

## Features (Post-MVP)
- Feature 3
- Feature 4

## Success Metrics
- [How do we measure success?]

## Non-Goals
- [What are we explicitly NOT doing?]

System Design Template:
markdown# System Design: [System Name]

## Overview
[1-2 paragraph summary]

## Architecture Diagram
[Boxes and arrows showing components]

## Components
### Component 1
- Responsibility: [What it does]
- Technology: [What it's built with]
- Interfaces: [How others interact with it]

### Component 2
...

## Data Models
### Entity 1
```sql
CREATE TABLE ...
```

## API Contracts
### Tauri Commands
- `create_ticket(title, desc) -> ticket_id`
- `list_tickets() -> tickets[]`

## Data Flow
[Sequence diagrams for key flows]

## Deployment
- Solo mode: [How it runs]
- Team mode: [How it runs]

## Alternatives Considered
- Option A: [Why rejected]
- Option B: [Why rejected]

## Open Questions
- [What's still TBD?]

Component Spec Template:
markdown# Component Spec: [Component Name]

## Responsibility
[What does this component do?]

## Interface
```rust
pub trait ComponentName {
    fn method1(...) -> Result;
    fn method2(...) -> Result;
}
```

## Dependencies
- Uses: [What it calls]
- Used by: [What calls it]

## Internal Logic
[How it works internally]

## Error Handling
- Error 1: [What to do]
- Error 2: [What to do]

## Test Cases
- Happy path: [...]
- Edge case 1: [...]
- Edge case 2: [...]

## Implementation Notes
[Anything tricky or non-obvious]

Practical Workflow
Week 0: Planning

Write PRD (we did this)
Write System Design (should do next)
Review with team/advisor/yourself

Week 1-6: Building MVP
For each vertical slice:

Write lightweight component spec (30 min - 1 hour)
Implement component (1-3 days)
Test component
Move to next slice

Don't spec everything upfront - write specs just-in-time before building each component.

Document Evolution
Living Documents:

PRD: Rarely changes (locked after approval)
System Design: Updates as architecture evolves
Component Specs: Can be quick notes, then formalized if complex

When to Update:

PRD: Only for major scope changes (requires approval)
System Design: When architecture changes (add new component, change tech)
Component Specs: When refactoring component


Anti-Patterns to Avoid
❌ Over-Documentation

Don't write 50-page specs before coding
Don't document obvious things
Don't maintain docs that are never read

❌ Under-Documentation

Don't skip System Design (causes rewrites)
Don't skip component specs for complex logic (causes thrashing)
Don't leave architecture decisions undocumented (causes confusion)

❌ Stale Documentation

Don't let docs diverge from code
Don't keep outdated diagrams
Delete docs when they become irrelevant


For Scope Specifically
What You Should Write Next:
1. System Design Doc (2-3 days)
Sections:

Architecture diagram (Tauri ↔ SQLite ↔ ElectricSQL ↔ Phoenix)
Database schema (tickets, loops tables)
Tauri command API (all commands listed)
Event schema (all events listed)
Deployment (solo vs team)

Why: This prevents rewrites. You need to know how components fit together.
2. Component Specs (As You Build)
Write before implementing:

Loop Executor (complex state machine)
ElectricSQL Integration (sync protocol)
PTY Bridge (tricky IPC)

Skip for simple components:

Ticket CRUD (straightforward SQLite queries)
UI components (React patterns are standard)


How Much Time to Spend
PRD: 10-15% of project time

6-week project → 1 week on PRD
You've done this ✅

System Design: 10-15% of project time

6-week project → 1 week on SDD
Do this next

Component Specs: 5-10% per component

Complex component: 1 day spec, 3 days implementation
Simple component: 30 min notes, 1 day implementation


Recommended Next Steps for You:

Write System Design Doc (spend 2-3 days)

Architecture diagram
Database schemas
API contracts
Deployment model


Start Slice 1 (Ticket Management UI)

Write quick spec for ticket CRUD (30 min)
Implement (2-3 days)
Move to Slice 2


Iterate

Spec → Build → Test → Next slice
Update System Design if architecture changes




Tools
Diagramming:

Excalidraw (quick sketches)
Draw.io (polished diagrams)
Mermaid (diagrams as code, good for git)

Documentation:

Markdown in repo (/docs folder)
Notion (if you want rich formatting)
Google Docs (if collaborating with non-engineers)

Code Comments:

In-code docs for tricky logic
README per component/module
ADRs (Architecture Decision Records) for big choices


Final Answer
How to structure:

PRD (done ✅)
System Design (do next)
Component specs (write as you go, before each component)

Time split:

Planning: 20-30% (PRD + SDD)
Implementation: 60-70%
Testing/polish: 10-20%

Next action:
Write System Design Doc. Spend 2-3 days. Cover:

Architecture
Database
APIs
Deployment

Then start coding Slice 1.
Make sense?Let's do system design nextFeb 14Perfect. Let's build the System Design Doc.

Scope: System Design Document
Version: 1.0
Date: February 14, 2026
Status: Draft
Authors: [Your name]

1. Overview
Scope is a local-first agentic coding orchestration system. It manages AI agents (makers and checkers) to turn natural language tickets into verified code changes through a loop-based execution model.
Core value proposition: Execute coding tasks through maker↔checker loops with real-time collaboration, offline support, and full transparency.
Key architectural principles:

Local-first (SQLite primary storage)
Optional real-time sync (ElectricSQL)
Event-driven architecture
Modular agent adapters
Git for code versioning (not data)


2. Architecture Diagram
High-Level System Architecture
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                  Tauri Desktop App                      │
│                                                         │
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │         Frontend (React/TypeScript)            │    │
│  │  • Issue Tracker View                          │    │
│  │  • DAG Viewer                                  │    │
│  │  • Loop Inspector (Terminal, Diff)            │    │
│  │  • Monitor View                                │    │
│  └──────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┘    │
│                     │ Tauri IPC                        │
│  ┌──────────────────▼─────────────────────────────┐    │
│  │         Backend (Rust)                         │    │
│  │                                                │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐           │    │
│  │  │ Loop         │  │ Agent        │           │    │
│  │  │ Orchestrator │  │ Adapters     │           │    │
│  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘           │    │
│  │                                                │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐           │    │
│  │  │ PTY Bridge   │  │ Worktree     │           │    │
│  │  │              │  │ Manager      │           │    │
│  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘           │    │
│  │                                                │    │
│  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐     │    │
│  │  │  SQLite (.scope/scope.db)            │     │    │
│  │  │  • tickets                           │     │    │
│  │  │  • loops (ephemeral)                 │     │    │
│  │  └──────────────┬───────────────────────┘     │    │

3.2 Backend (Rust/Tauri)

Modules:
3.2.1 Loop Orchestrator
rust// src/orchestrator.rs
struct LoopOrchestrator {
    maker: Box<dyn Agent>,
    checker: Box<dyn Agent>,
    worktree_mgr: WorktreeManager,
    event_bus: EventBus,
    circuit_breaker: CircuitBreaker,
}

impl LoopOrchestrator {
    async fn execute_loop(&self, loop_def: LoopDefinition) -> LoopResult;
    async fn execute_dag(&self, dag: DAG) -> DAGResult;
}
Responsibilities:

Execute maker↔checker loops
Handle retries (max 5 attempts)
Manage DAG dependencies
Circuit breaker integration
Escalation handling


3.2.2 Agent Adapters

3.2.6 Circuit Breaker

struct CircuitBreaker {
    state: CircuitState,
    escalation_count: usize,
    threshold: usize,  // Default: 3
}

    Closed,    // Normal
    Open,      // Paused
    HalfOpen,  // Testing
}

impl CircuitBreaker {

    fn on_resolution(&mut self);
    fn should_pause_new_loops(&self) -> bool;
}
```

**Responsibilities:**
- Count escalations
- Pause loops when threshold hit

---

### 3.3 Storage Layer (SQLite)

**Location:** `.scope/scope.db`

**Technology:** SQLite 3.40+

**Responsibilities:**
- Primary data storage (tickets, loops)
- Event log (append-only)
- Works offline
- Syncs via ElectricSQL (optional)

**Schema:** (see Section 4)

---

### 3.4 Sync Layer (ElectricSQL)

**Responsibility:** Bidirectional sync between local SQLite and server PostgreSQL.

**Technology:** ElectricSQL client (Rust)

**Modes:**
- **Solo:** ElectricSQL disabled, SQLite only
- **Team:** ElectricSQL enabled, syncs to server

**Data Flow:**
```
SQLite INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE
    ↓
ElectricSQL detects change (trigger)
    ↓
Sends to Electric Sync Service (WebSocket)
    ↓
Postgres updated
    ↓
Broadcasts to other clients
    ↓
Their SQLite updated
    ↓
UI reactively updates

3.5 Electric Sync Service (Server)
Responsibility: Relay changes between clients, persist in Postgres.
Technology:

Electric (Elixir/Erlang)
PostgreSQL 15+
Logical replication

Deployment:

Docker Compose (self-hosted)
Or SaaS (future)

Not responsible for:

Business logic (clients handle this)
File storage
Authentication (v1 trust-based)


4. Data Models
4.1 SQLite Schema
sql-- Tickets (synced via ElectricSQL)
CREATE TABLE tickets (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    title TEXT NOT NULL,
    description TEXT,
    status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'open',
    type TEXT NOT NULL,
    priority INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 2,
    assignee TEXT,
    acceptance_criteria TEXT,  -- JSON array
    spawned BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE,
    spawned_at TIMESTAMP,
    created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    updated_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,

    -- ElectricSQL metadata (managed automatically)
    _electric_id TEXT,
    _electric_version INTEGER
);

-- Loops (NOT synced, local only)
CREATE TABLE loops (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    ticket_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    task TEXT NOT NULL,
    status TEXT NOT NULL,  -- pending, running, passed, failed, escalated
    attempts INTEGER DEFAULT 0,
    current_attempt INTEGER,
    commit_sha TEXT,
    worktree_path TEXT,
    created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    updated_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    FOREIGN KEY (ticket_id) REFERENCES tickets(id)
);

-- Loop Attempts (history)
CREATE TABLE loop_attempts (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
    loop_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    attempt_num INTEGER NOT NULL,
    maker_output TEXT,
    checker_feedback TEXT,
    checker_passed BOOLEAN,
    checker_scores TEXT,  -- JSON
    created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    FOREIGN KEY (loop_id) REFERENCES loops(id)
);

-- Events (append-only log)
CREATE TABLE events (
    id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
    type TEXT NOT NULL,
    payload TEXT NOT NULL,  -- JSON
    timestamp TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP
);

-- DAG storage (when ticket spawned)
CREATE TABLE dags (
    ticket_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    definition TEXT NOT NULL,  -- JSON
    created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    FOREIGN KEY (ticket_id) REFERENCES tickets(id)
);

4.2 PostgreSQL Schema (Server)
Same as SQLite for synced tables:
sql-- Only tickets table replicates
CREATE TABLE tickets (
    -- Same schema as SQLite
    -- ElectricSQL handles sync automatically
);
```

**Loops/attempts/events stay local (not synced).**

### 4.3 Git-Tracked Files

**Not in database, stored in filesystem:**
```
.scope/
├─ tickets/                  # Snapshots (when spawned)
│  └─ nw-5c46.yaml
│     └─ ticket + DAG definition
│
└─ logs/                     # Optional exports
   └─ loop-abc123.json
Format:
yaml# .scope/tickets/nw-5c46.yaml
ticket:
  id: nw-5c46
  title: Add authentication
  description: ...
  acceptance_criteria:
    - POST /auth/login works
    - Tokens expire

dag:
  loops:
    - id: loop-1
      task: Schema
    - id: loop-2
      task: API
      depends_on: [loop-1]

spawned_at: 2026-02-14T10:00:00Z

5.2 Events (Backend → Frontend)
typescripttype ScopeEvent =
  // Tickets
  | { type: "ticket.created", ticket: Ticket }
  | { type: "ticket.updated", ticket: Ticket }
  | { type: "ticket.deleted", id: string }

  // DAG
  | { type: "dag.created", ticket_id: string, dag: DAG }

  // Loops
  | { type: "loop.started", loop_id: string, task: string }
  | { type: "loop.attempt", loop_id: string, attempt_num: number }
  | { type: "loop.maker_output", loop_id: string, diff_preview: string }
  | { type: "loop.checker_running", loop_id: string }
  | { type: "loop.checker_result", loop_id: string, passed: boolean, scores: PyramidScores, feedback: string }
  | { type: "loop.passed", loop_id: string, commit_sha: string }
  | { type: "loop.failed", loop_id: string, reason: string }
  | { type: "loop.escalated", loop_id: string }

  // Terminal
  | { type: "terminal.output", loop_id: string, data: Uint8Array }
  | { type: "terminal.exited", loop_id: string, exit_code: number }

  // Circuit Breaker
  | { type: "circuit_breaker.opened", escalation_count: number }
  | { type: "circuit_breaker.half_open" }
  | { type: "circuit_breaker.closed" }

  // Presence (from ElectricSQL sync)
  | { type: "user.joined", user_id: string, viewing_ticket?: string }
  | { type: "user.left", user_id: string }
Frontend subscribes:
typescriptimport { listen } from '@tauri-apps/api/event';

listen('scope-event', (event) => {
  const scopeEvent = event.payload as ScopeEvent;
  // Update UI based on event
});

5.3 ElectricSQL Sync Protocol
Automatic, no explicit API.
Trigger-based:
sql-- SQLite trigger (ElectricSQL manages this)
CREATE TRIGGER ticket_changes
AFTER INSERT OR UPDATE OR DELETE ON tickets
BEGIN
    -- ElectricSQL captures change
    -- Sends to sync service via WebSocket
END;
WebSocket messages (internal to Electric):
json{
  "type": "transaction",
  "lsn": "0/12345",
  "changes": [
    {
      "relation": ["public", "tickets"],
      "record": {...},
      "tags": ["insert"]
    }
  ]
}
Client receives:
rust// ElectricSQL client handles this automatically
// Applies changes to local SQLite
// Emits event to Tauri frontend
```

## 6. Data Flow Diagrams

### 6.1 Create Ticket (Solo Mode)
```
User clicks "New Ticket"
    ↓
Frontend: TicketForm modal
    ↓
User submits form
    ↓
Frontend: invoke('create_ticket', { title, description, ... })
    ↓
Backend: INSERT INTO tickets VALUES (...)
    ↓
Backend: emit(Event::TicketCreated { ticket })
    ↓
Frontend: listen('scope-event') → update UI
    ↓
User sees new ticket in list
```

---

### 6.2 Create Ticket (Team Mode)
```
User clicks "New Ticket"
    ↓
Frontend: TicketForm modal
    ↓
User submits form
    ↓
Frontend: invoke('create_ticket', { title, description, ... })
    ↓
Backend: INSERT INTO tickets VALUES (...)
    ↓
ElectricSQL: Detects change (trigger)
    ↓
ElectricSQL: Sends to sync service (WebSocket)
    ↓
Sync Service: Writes to Postgres
    ↓
Sync Service: Broadcasts to other clients
    ↓
Other clients: Receive WebSocket message
    ↓
Other clients: Apply to local SQLite
    ↓
Other clients: emit(Event::TicketCreated { ticket })
    ↓
Other frontends: Update UI (see new ticket)
```

---

### 6.3 Spawn Loops (Start Work)
```
User clicks "Start Work" on ticket
    ↓
Frontend: invoke('start_work', { ticket_id })
    ↓
Backend: Read ticket from SQLite
    ↓
Backend: Call TaskDecomposer (LLM)
    ↓
TaskDecomposer: Returns DAG (loops + dependencies)
    ↓
Backend: INSERT INTO dags (ticket_id, definition)
    ↓
Backend: Write .scope/tickets/{id}.yaml (git-tracked snapshot)
    ↓
Backend: Git commit
    ↓
Backend: For each ready loop:
    ├─ Create worktree
    ├─ INSERT INTO loops (id, ticket_id, task, status='running')
    └─ Start LoopOrchestrator.execute_loop()
    ↓
Backend: emit(Event::DAGCreated { dag })
    ↓
Frontend: Navigate to DAG Viewer
```

---

### 6.4 Loop Execution (Maker → Checker)
```
LoopOrchestrator.execute_loop(loop_def)
    ↓
For attempt in 1..=5:
    ├─ emit(Event::LoopAttempt { loop_id, attempt_num })
    │
    ├─ PTYBridge.spawn_command("claude-code", task)
    ├─ Stream terminal output → Frontend (xterm.js)
    ├─ Wait for process exit
    ├─ emit(Event::TerminalExited { loop_id })
    │
    ├─ WorktreeManager.get_diff(loop_id)
    ├─ emit(Event::LoopMakerOutput { diff_preview })
    │
    ├─ emit(Event::LoopCheckerRunning { loop_id })
    ├─ ClaudeAPIAdapter.verify(diff, intent, rubric)
    ├─ emit(Event::LoopCheckerResult { scores, passed, feedback })
    │
    ├─ INSERT INTO loop_attempts (loop_id, attempt_num, checker_passed, ...)
    │
    ├─ If passed:
    │   ├─ WorktreeManager.merge(loop_id)
    │   ├─ UPDATE loops SET status='passed', commit_sha=...
    │   ├─ emit(Event::LoopPassed { loop_id, commit_sha })
    │   └─ RETURN Success
    │
    └─ If failed and attempt < 5:
        └─ Continue (maker sees feedback in next iteration)

If max attempts reached:
    ├─ UPDATE loops SET status='escalated'
    ├─ emit(Event::LoopEscalated { loop_id })
    ├─ CircuitBreaker.on_escalation()
    └─ RETURN Failure
```

---

### 6.5 Terminal Interaction
```
User types in xterm.js
    ↓
Frontend: invoke('send_terminal_input', { loop_id, data })
    ↓
Backend: PTYBridge.send_input(data)
    ↓
PTY writes to Claude Code stdin
    ↓
Claude Code outputs to stdout
    ↓
PTY captures output
    ↓
Backend: emit(Event::TerminalOutput { loop_id, data })
    ↓
Frontend: xterm.write(data)
    ↓
User sees output in terminal
```

8. Technology Stack
8.1 Frontend

Framework: React 18
Language: TypeScript 5
State: React hooks + context
Styling: Tailwind CSS
Terminal: xterm.js
Diff: monaco-editor
Graphs: reactflow
Build: Vite

8.2 Backend

Language: Rust 1.75+
Framework: Tauri 2.0

PTY: portable-pty

8.3 Storage

Local: SQLite 3.40+

Sync Service: Electric (Elixir/Erlang)
Deployment: Docker Compose
Protocol: WebSocket (WSS)


9. Security Considerations
9.1 Local Security

File permissions: .scope/scope.db readable only by user
Secrets: Never log API keys, store in system keychain
Git: Never commit .scope/scope.db (gitignored)

9.2 Network Security (Team Mode)

Transport: WSS (WebSocket over TLS)
Auth: v1 trust-based, v2 add JWT tokens
Data: All synced data in Postgres, encrypted at rest

9.3 Agent Safety

Isolation: Each loop in separate git worktree
Sandboxing: Rely on agent's own sandboxing (Claude Code, etc.)
Review: User can review diffs before merge


10. Performance Requirements
10.1 Latency Targets

Ticket CRUD: < 50ms (local SQLite)
Loop start: < 2s (worktree creation + agent spawn)
Event propagation: < 100ms (local emit)
Sync latency: < 500ms (ElectricSQL round-trip)
Terminal streaming: < 16ms (60 FPS)

10.2 Throughput

Concurrent loops: 10-20 per machine
Synced clients: 50-100 per server
Events/sec: 100+ local, 1000+ server

10.3 Storage

SQLite size: < 100MB for 1000 tickets
Worktree disk: Auto-cleanup, < 5GB total
Event log: Prune events older than 30 days


11. Error Handling
11.1 Error Categories
User Errors:

Invalid ticket input → Show validation message
Duplicate ticket ID → Auto-generate new ID
Missing required fields → Form validation

System Errors:

SQLite write failure → Retry 3x, then alert user
Git worktree failure → Cleanup partial state, retry
Agent spawn failure → Log error, escalate loop

Network Errors:

Sync service unreachable → Queue changes, retry when online
WebSocket disconnect → Auto-reconnect with exponential backoff

11.2 Recovery Strategies
Loop failures:

Max attempts reached → Escalate to human
Agent crashed → Restart attempt
Worktree corrupted → Recreate worktree

Sync failures:

Conflict → CRDT auto-merge (ElectricSQL)
Partial sync → Full resync on reconnect
Server down → Queue locally, sync when up

Ready to start building? Or want to refine any sections first?Okay, let's dig deeper into task decomposition - we need recursion here right? There can be multiple levels until the task gets small enough for an atomic loop. Also we need to avoid duplicate workFeb 14Yes, absolutely. Task decomposition needs recursion AND deduplication.

Task Decomposition: Deep Dive
The Problem
Simple decomposition (what we have now):
Ticket: "Add user authentication"
    ↓
DAG: [Schema, API, Tests, Middleware]
But what if subtasks are still too large?
Ticket: "Build social media platform"
    ↓
Subtask: "User authentication" (still too big!)
        ↓
        Need to decompose again: [Schema, API, Tests, ...]
And what if multiple tickets need the same thing?
Ticket A: "Add user login"
    ↓ needs Auth

Ticket B: "Add OAuth"
    ↓ needs Auth (same thing!)

Don't want two loops doing the same work.

Solution: Hierarchical Task Network (HTN) with Deduplication
Key Concepts

Recursive decomposition - Keep breaking down until atomic
Atomic test - Can this be done in one loop?
Deduplication - Reuse existing loops/commits
Dependency tracking - Loops can depend on loops from other tickets


1. Recursive Decomposition Algorithm
rust// src/decomposer.rs

struct TaskDecomposer {
    llm: Box<dyn Agent>,
}

impl TaskDecomposer {
    async fn decompose(&self, task: Task) -> Result<DAG> {
        self.decompose_recursive(task, 0).await
    }

    async fn decompose_recursive(
        &self,
        task: Task,
        depth: usize,
    ) -> Result<DAG> {
        // Safety: prevent infinite recursion
        if depth > 5 {
            return Err(anyhow!("Max decomposition depth reached"));
        }

        // Check if task is atomic
        if self.is_atomic(&task).await? {
            // Base case: create single loop
            return Ok(DAG {
                nodes: vec![LoopNode {
                    id: generate_id(),
                    task: task.description,
                    dependencies: vec![],
                }],
                edges: vec![],
            });
        }

        // Recursive case: decompose into subtasks
        let subtasks = self.decompose_into_subtasks(&task).await?;

        let mut all_nodes = vec![];
        let mut all_edges = vec![];

        for subtask in subtasks {
            // Recursively decompose each subtask
            let sub_dag = self.decompose_recursive(subtask, depth + 1).await?;

            all_nodes.extend(sub_dag.nodes);
            all_edges.extend(sub_dag.edges);
        }

        Ok(DAG {
            nodes: all_nodes,
            edges: all_edges,
        })
    }
}

2. Atomic Test
LLM prompt to determine if task is atomic:
rustasync fn is_atomic(&self, task: &Task) -> Result<bool> {
    let prompt = format!(
        r#"
Task: {task}

Context window limit: 200k tokens
Max code changes: 500 lines
Repository context: {repo_context}

Can this task be completed in a SINGLE coding session?

Consider:
- Does it fit in context window?
- Is it a single logical unit?
- Can it be verified with one rubric?
- Does it require < 5 attempts to complete?

Answer YES or NO, followed by brief reasoning.
        "#,
        task = task.description,
        repo_context = get_repo_summary()
    );

    let response = self.llm.execute(&prompt).await?;
    Ok(response.starts_with("YES"))
}
```

**Examples:**
```
Task: "Add user authentication"
→ NO (needs schema, API, tests - multiple concerns)

Task: "Implement User schema with email and password fields"
→ YES (single file, clear scope)

Task: "Write tests for login endpoint"
→ YES (focused on one endpoint)

Task: "Build entire admin dashboard"
→ NO (too broad, many components)

3. Subtask Decomposition
rustasync fn decompose_into_subtasks(&self, task: &Task) -> Result<Vec<Task>> {
    let prompt = format!(
        r#"
Task: {task}
Repository: {repo_context}
Acceptance criteria: {acceptance}

Decompose into 3-7 subtasks that:
1. Are independent or have clear dependencies
2. Can each be completed in one coding session
3. Together fully implement the parent task

Return JSON:
{{
  "subtasks": [
    {{
      "id": "subtask-1",
      "description": "...",
      "depends_on": [],
      "acceptance_criteria": [...]
    }},
    ...
  ]
}}
        "#,
        task = task.description,
        repo_context = get_repo_summary(),
        acceptance = task.acceptance_criteria.join(", ")
    );

    let response = self.llm.execute(&prompt).await?;
    let decomposition: DecompositionResponse = serde_json::from_str(&response)?;

    Ok(decomposition.subtasks)
}
```

**Example decomposition:**
```
Parent: "Add user authentication"
    ↓
Subtasks:
  1. "Implement User schema with email/password fields"
     depends_on: []

  2. "Implement POST /auth/login endpoint"
     depends_on: [subtask-1]

  3. "Implement JWT token generation and validation"
     depends_on: [subtask-1]

  4. "Add authentication middleware"
     depends_on: [subtask-2, subtask-3]

  5. "Write integration tests for auth flow"
     depends_on: [subtask-4]
```

---

## 4. Deduplication Strategy

### Problem: Multiple tickets need same work
```
Ticket A: "Add user login"
    ↓ needs User schema

Ticket B: "Add user profile"
    ↓ needs User schema (same!)
Without deduplication:

Loop A-1: Create User schema
Loop B-1: Create User schema (duplicate work!)

With deduplication:

Loop 1: Create User schema (shared)
Loop A-2: Depends on Loop 1
Loop B-2: Depends on Loop 1


Deduplication Algorithm
ruststruct LoopRegistry {
    db: SqliteConnection,
}

impl LoopRegistry {
    // Check if similar loop already exists
    async fn find_similar(&self, task: &str) -> Result<Option<LoopId>> {
        // Use embeddings for semantic similarity
        let task_embedding = self.get_embedding(task).await?;

        let existing_loops = sqlx::query!(
            "SELECT id, task, embedding FROM loops WHERE status = 'passed'"
        )
        .fetch_all(&self.db)
        .await?;

        for loop_record in existing_loops {
            let similarity = cosine_similarity(
                &task_embedding,
                &loop_record.embedding
            );

            if similarity > 0.9 {
                // Very similar task already completed
                return Ok(Some(loop_record.id));
            }
        }

        Ok(None)
    }

    // Or simpler: exact string match on normalized task
    async fn find_exact(&self, normalized_task: &str) -> Result<Option<LoopId>> {
        sqlx::query_scalar!(
            "SELECT id FROM loops WHERE normalized_task = ? AND status = 'passed'",
            normalized_task
        )
        .fetch_optional(&self.db)
        .await
    }
}

Using Deduplication in Decomposer
rustasync fn decompose_with_dedup(&self, task: Task) -> Result<DAG> {
    let mut dag = self.decompose_recursive(task, 0).await?;

    // For each node, check if similar loop exists
    for node in &mut dag.nodes {
        if let Some(existing_loop_id) = self.registry.find_similar(&node.task).await? {
            // Reuse existing loop
            node.id = existing_loop_id;
            node.status = LoopStatus::Completed; // Already done
            node.reused = true;
        }
    }

    Ok(dag)
}
```

**Example:**
```
Ticket A spawns:
    Loop 1: "Create User schema"
    Loop 2: "Implement login endpoint" (depends on Loop 1)

Ticket B spawns:
    Loop 3: "Create User schema"
        → DEDUPLICATED! Reuse Loop 1 (already passed)
    Loop 4: "Implement profile endpoint" (depends on Loop 1, not Loop 3)
```

---

## 5. Cross-Ticket Dependencies

**Scenario:**
```
Ticket A: "Add user authentication"
    Loop A-1: User schema
    Loop A-2: Login endpoint (depends on A-1)

Ticket B: "Add user profile"
    Loop B-1: Profile endpoint (depends on A-1!)
DAG with cross-ticket dependencies:
ruststruct LoopNode {
    id: String,
    ticket_id: String,        // Which ticket spawned this
    task: String,
    dependencies: Vec<String>, // Can reference loops from other tickets
    reused: bool,             // Was this deduplicated?
}
Database schema update:
sqlCREATE TABLE loop_dependencies (
    loop_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    depends_on_loop_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (loop_id, depends_on_loop_id),
    FOREIGN KEY (loop_id) REFERENCES loops(id),
    FOREIGN KEY (depends_on_loop_id) REFERENCES loops(id)
);
```

## 6. Task Normalization (Better Deduplication)

**Problem:** Same task, different wording
```
Task A: "Create User model with email and password"
Task B: "Implement User schema (email, password fields)"
Task C: "Add User table with email/password columns"

→ All the same! But string comparison would miss it.
Solution: Normalize before comparing
rustasync fn normalize_task(&self, task: &str) -> Result<String> {
    let prompt = format!(
        r#"
Normalize this task description to a canonical form:
"{task}"

Remove:
- Filler words (please, could you, etc.)
- Implementation details (table/model/schema are equivalent)
- Formatting differences

Output only the normalized task, nothing else.
        "#
    );

    let normalized = self.llm.execute(&prompt).await?;
    Ok(normalized.trim().to_lowercase())
}
Before deduplication check:
rustasync fn find_duplicate(&self, task: &str) -> Result<Option<LoopId>> {
    let normalized = self.normalize_task(task).await?;

    // Check exact match on normalized form
    self.registry.find_exact(&normalized).await
}
```

## 7. Recursive Decomposition Example

**Input:**
```
Ticket: "Build authentication system with OAuth and 2FA"
```

**Decomposition tree:**
```
Level 0: "Build authentication system with OAuth and 2FA"
  ├─ Atomic? NO
  ↓
Level 1:
  ├─ "Implement basic username/password auth"
  │   ├─ Atomic? NO
  │   ↓
  │   Level 2:
  │   ├─ "Create User schema" ← ATOMIC ✓
  │   ├─ "Implement login endpoint" ← ATOMIC ✓
  │   └─ "Add JWT middleware" ← ATOMIC ✓
  │
  ├─ "Add OAuth integration"
  │   ├─ Atomic? NO
  │   ↓
  │   Level 2:
  │   ├─ "Implement OAuth flow for Google" ← ATOMIC ✓
  │   ├─ "Implement OAuth flow for GitHub" ← ATOMIC ✓
  │   └─ "Store OAuth tokens securely" ← ATOMIC ✓
  │
  └─ "Implement 2FA"
      ├─ Atomic? NO
      ↓
      Level 2:
      ├─ "Generate TOTP secrets" ← ATOMIC ✓
      ├─ "Implement 2FA verification endpoint" ← ATOMIC ✓
      └─ "Add 2FA to login flow" ← ATOMIC ✓

Final DAG: 9 atomic loops with dependencies

8. Preventing Duplicate Work (Strategies)
Strategy 1: Exact Match (Fast)
rust// Normalize and hash task description
let task_hash = hash(&normalize(task));

// Check if already exists
if let Some(loop_id) = db.get_by_hash(task_hash) {
    return ReuseExisting(loop_id);
}
Pros: Fast (hash lookup)
Cons: Misses semantically similar tasks with different wording

Strategy 2: Embedding Similarity (Accurate)
rust// Get embedding vector for task
let embedding = openai.embed(task).await?;

// Find similar loops (cosine similarity > 0.9)
let similar = db.find_by_embedding_similarity(embedding, 0.9).await?;

if let Some(loop_id) = similar {
    return ReuseExisting(loop_id);
}
Pros: Catches semantic duplicates
Cons: Requires embedding API calls, slower

Strategy 3: Hybrid (Best)
rustasync fn find_duplicate(&self, task: &str) -> Result<Option<LoopId>> {
    // 1. Try exact match (fast)
    let normalized = normalize(task);
    if let Some(id) = self.db.find_exact(&normalized).await? {
        return Ok(Some(id));
    }

    // 2. Try embedding similarity (accurate)
    let embedding = self.embed(task).await?;
    if let Some(id) = self.db.find_similar(&embedding, 0.9).await? {
        return Ok(Some(id));
    }

    // 3. No duplicate found
    Ok(None)
}

9. Updated Database Schema
sql-- Loops with deduplication support
CREATE TABLE loops (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    ticket_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    parent_loop_id TEXT,          -- For hierarchical decomposition
    task TEXT NOT NULL,
    normalized_task TEXT NOT NULL, -- For exact match dedup
    task_embedding BLOB,           -- Vector for similarity search
    status TEXT NOT NULL,
    depth INTEGER DEFAULT 0,       -- Decomposition depth
    reused_from TEXT,              -- If deduplicated, original loop ID
    created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP,
    FOREIGN KEY (ticket_id) REFERENCES tickets(id),
    FOREIGN KEY (parent_loop_id) REFERENCES loops(id),
    FOREIGN KEY (reused_from) REFERENCES loops(id)
);

-- Fast lookup index
CREATE INDEX idx_normalized_task ON loops(normalized_task) WHERE status = 'passed';

-- Dependencies (including cross-ticket)
CREATE TABLE loop_dependencies (
    loop_id TEXT NOT NULL,
    depends_on TEXT NOT NULL,
    dependency_type TEXT DEFAULT 'sequential', -- or 'shared_resource'
    PRIMARY KEY (loop_id, depends_on),
    FOREIGN KEY (loop_id) REFERENCES loops(id),
    FOREIGN KEY (depends_on) REFERENCES loops(id)
);
```

---

## 10. Decomposition Flow (Complete)
```
User: "Start work" on ticket
    ↓
TaskDecomposer.decompose(ticket)
    ↓
Is task atomic?
    ├─ YES → Create single loop
    │         ├─ Check for duplicate (normalize + embedding)
    │         ├─ If duplicate found → reuse existing loop
    │         └─ If not → create new loop
    │
    └─ NO → Decompose into subtasks
              ├─ LLM generates 3-7 subtasks
              ├─ For each subtask:
              │   └─ Recursively call decompose() [depth + 1]
              │
              └─ Merge all sub-DAGs
                  ├─ Deduplicate nodes (check all against existing)
                  ├─ Build dependency edges
                  └─ Return combined DAG

Save DAG to database
    ↓
Start executing loops (respecting dependencies)
```

---

12. System Design Updates
Component: Task Decomposer (Detailed)
rust// src/decomposer.rs

struct TaskDecomposer {
    llm: Box<dyn Agent>,
    registry: LoopRegistry,
    max_depth: usize,
}

impl TaskDecomposer {
    async fn decompose(&self, task: Task) -> Result<DAG> {
        self.decompose_recursive(task, 0, vec![]).await
    }

    async fn decompose_recursive(
        &self,
        task: Task,
        depth: usize,
        parent_chain: Vec<String>, // Prevent circular dependencies
    ) -> Result<DAG> {
        // Safety checks
        if depth > self.max_depth {
            return Err(anyhow!("Max depth exceeded"));
        }

        if parent_chain.contains(&task.id) {
            return Err(anyhow!("Circular dependency detected"));
        }

        // Check if task is atomic
        if self.is_atomic(&task).await? {
            // Try to find existing loop
            if let Some(existing_id) = self.registry.find_duplicate(&task.description).await? {
                return Ok(DAG {
                    nodes: vec![LoopNode {
                        id: existing_id,
                        task: task.description.clone(),
                        reused: true,
                        status: LoopStatus::Completed,
                        dependencies: vec![],
                    }],
                    edges: vec![],
                });
            }

            // Create new atomic loop
            let loop_id = generate_id();
            return Ok(DAG {
                nodes: vec![LoopNode {
                    id: loop_id.clone(),
                    task: task.description,
                    reused: false,
                    status: LoopStatus::Pending,
                    dependencies: vec![],
                }],
                edges: vec![],
            });
        }

        // Decompose into subtasks
        let subtasks = self.decompose_into_subtasks(&task).await?;

        let mut combined_dag = DAG::empty();
        let mut new_parent_chain = parent_chain.clone();
        new_parent_chain.push(task.id.clone());

        for subtask in subtasks {
            let sub_dag = self.decompose_recursive(
                subtask,
                depth + 1,
                new_parent_chain.clone()
            ).await?;

            combined_dag.merge(sub_dag);
        }

        Ok(combined_dag)
    }
}

Summary: What Changes
PRD Updates:

✅ Add: "Recursive task decomposition"
✅ Add: "Loop deduplication across tickets"
✅ Add: "Cross-ticket dependencies"

ALTER TABLE loops ADD COLUMN normalized_task TEXT;
ALTER TABLE loops ADD COLUMN task_embedding BLOB;
ALTER TABLE loops ADD COLUMN depth INTEGER;

New algorithms:

Does this address your concerns about recursion and deduplication?Simplify. Instead of calling llm spawn claude code in cli mode. Deduplicate via rag matchFeb 14Much simpler. Good call.

Simplified Task Decomposition
Core Changes

No LLM calls for decomposition → Use Claude Code in CLI mode
No embedding API → Use RAG search over existing loops
No normalization LLM → Simple string matching + RAG


1. Decomposition: Claude Code CLI Mode
Instead of calling Claude API to analyze if atomic:
rustasync fn decompose(&self, task: Task) -> Result<DAG> {
    // Spawn Claude Code in CLI mode with decomposition prompt
    let prompt = format!(
        r#"
Task: {task}

Decompose this into a list of atomic coding tasks. Each task should:
- Be completable in one coding session
- Be independently verifiable
- Have clear acceptance criteria

If the task is already atomic, return just that task.

Output format (JSON):
{{
  "tasks": [
    {{
      "id": "task-1",
      "description": "...",
      "depends_on": []
    }}
  ]
}}
        "#,
        task = task.description
    );

    // Spawn Claude Code in non-interactive mode
    let output = Command::new("claude-code")
        .arg("--non-interactive")
        .arg("--prompt")
        .arg(&prompt)
        .output()?;

    let response = String::from_utf8(output.stdout)?;
    let decomposition: DecompositionResponse = parse_json_from_output(&response)?;

    // Build DAG from tasks
    let mut dag = DAG::empty();
    for task in decomposition.tasks {
        dag.add_node(LoopNode {
            id: generate_id(),
            task: task.description,
            dependencies: task.depends_on,
            reused: false,
            status: LoopStatus::Pending,
        });
    }

    Ok(dag)
}
Key simplification:

❌ No separate LLM API calls
✅ Just spawn Claude Code once with prompt
✅ Parse structured output (JSON)
✅ Build DAG from result


2. Deduplication: RAG Search
Instead of embeddings + similarity:
ruststruct LoopRegistry {
    rag_index: RAGIndex,  // Vector search over loop descriptions
    db: SqliteConnection,
}

impl LoopRegistry {
    async fn find_duplicate(&self, task_description: &str) -> Result<Option<LoopId>> {
        // Search existing completed loops via RAG
        let results = self.rag_index.search(
            task_description,
            limit: 5,
            min_score: 0.85  // High threshold for duplication
        ).await?;

        if let Some(top_result) = results.first() {
            if top_result.score > 0.85 {
                // Found very similar task
                return Ok(Some(top_result.loop_id));
            }
        }

        Ok(None)
    }

    // After loop completes, add to RAG index
    async fn index_completed_loop(&self, loop_id: &str, task: &str, commit_sha: &str) {
        self.rag_index.add_document(
            loop_id,
            task,
            metadata: json!({
                "commit_sha": commit_sha,
                "completed_at": Utc::now(),
            })
        ).await;
    }
}
RAG Implementation Options:
Option A: Use tantivy (Rust full-text search)
toml[dependencies]
tantivy = "0.21"
rustuse tantivy::*;

struct RAGIndex {
    index: Index,
    reader: IndexReader,
}

impl RAGIndex {
    fn new() -> Result<Self> {
        let schema = Self::build_schema();
        let index = Index::create_in_dir(".scope/rag_index", schema)?;
        let reader = index.reader()?;

        Ok(Self { index, reader })
    }

    fn build_schema() -> Schema {
        let mut schema_builder = Schema::builder();
        schema_builder.add_text_field("task", TEXT | STORED);
        schema_builder.add_text_field("loop_id", STRING | STORED);
        schema_builder.build()
    }

    async fn search(&self, query: &str, limit: usize) -> Result<Vec<SearchResult>> {
        let searcher = self.reader.searcher();
        let query_parser = QueryParser::for_index(&self.index, vec![/* fields */]);

        let query = query_parser.parse_query(query)?;
        let top_docs = searcher.search(&query, &TopDocs::with_limit(limit))?;

        // Convert to SearchResult with scores
        let results = top_docs.iter()
            .map(|(score, doc_address)| {
                let doc = searcher.doc(*doc_address)?;
                let loop_id = doc.get_first(/* loop_id field */).unwrap();

                Ok(SearchResult {
                    loop_id: loop_id.as_text().unwrap().to_string(),
                    score: *score,
                })
            })
            .collect::<Result<Vec<_>>>()?;

        Ok(results)
    }

    fn add_document(&self, loop_id: &str, task: &str) -> Result<()> {
        let mut index_writer = self.index.writer(50_000_000)?;

        let doc = doc!(
            self.schema.get_field("loop_id").unwrap() => loop_id,
            self.schema.get_field("task").unwrap() => task
        );

        index_writer.add_document(doc)?;
        index_writer.commit()?;

        Ok(())
    }
}
Pros:

✅ Pure Rust (no external dependencies)
✅ Fast full-text search
✅ Works offline
✅ Small index size

Cons:

❌ Not semantic (keyword-based)
❌ Won't catch paraphrases


Option B: Use local embedding model (simpler than OpenAI)
toml[dependencies]
ort = "2.0"  # ONNX runtime for Rust
rustuse ort::Session;

struct RAGIndex {
    model: Session,  // Local sentence-transformer model
    vectors: Vec<(String, Vec<f32>)>,  // (loop_id, embedding)
}

impl RAGIndex {
    fn new() -> Result<Self> {
        // Load pre-trained model (e.g., all-MiniLM-L6-v2)
        let model = Session::builder()?
            .with_model_from_file("models/all-MiniLM-L6-v2.onnx")?;

        Ok(Self {
            model,
            vectors: vec![],
        })
    }

    fn embed(&self, text: &str) -> Result<Vec<f32>> {
        // Run inference on local model
        let tokens = self.tokenize(text);
        let output = self.model.run(tokens)?;
        Ok(output.extract_tensor()?.view().to_vec())
    }

    fn search(&self, query: &str, limit: usize, min_score: f32) -> Result<Vec<SearchResult>> {
        let query_vec = self.embed(query)?;

        let mut results: Vec<_> = self.vectors.iter()
            .map(|(loop_id, vec)| {
                let score = cosine_similarity(&query_vec, vec);
                SearchResult {
                    loop_id: loop_id.clone(),
                    score,
                }
            })
            .filter(|r| r.score >= min_score)
            .collect();

        results.sort_by(|a, b| b.score.partial_cmp(&a.score).unwrap());
        results.truncate(limit);

        Ok(results)
    }

    fn add_document(&mut self, loop_id: &str, task: &str) -> Result<()> {
        let embedding = self.embed(task)?;
        self.vectors.push((loop_id.to_string(), embedding));
        Ok(())
    }
}

fn cosine_similarity(a: &[f32], b: &[f32]) -> f32 {
    let dot: f32 = a.iter().zip(b).map(|(x, y)| x * y).sum();
    let mag_a: f32 = a.iter().map(|x| x * x).sum::<f32>().sqrt();
    let mag_b: f32 = b.iter().map(|x| x * x).sum::<f32>().sqrt();
    dot / (mag_a * mag_b)
}
```

**Pros:**
- ✅ Semantic search (catches paraphrases)
- ✅ Works offline (local model)
- ✅ No API costs

**Cons:**
- ❌ Need to ship embedding model (~100MB)
- ❌ Slower than tantivy

---

### Recommendation: **Option A (tantivy)** for MVP

**Why:**
- Simpler (no ML model)
- Faster
- Smaller binary
- Good enough for most duplicates

**Upgrade to Option B (local embeddings) in v1.1 if needed.**

---

## 3. Simplified Flow

### Before (Complex):
```
User: Start work
    ↓
Call LLM: Is task atomic?
    ↓ YES
    Call embedding API: Get vector
    ↓
    Vector search: Find similar
    ↓
    Return existing loop or create new
    ↓ NO
    Call LLM: Decompose into subtasks
    ↓
    For each subtask:
        Recursively call: Is atomic? ...
```

### After (Simple):
```
User: Start work
    ↓
Spawn Claude Code CLI:
    "Decompose this task into atomic tasks (JSON)"
    ↓
Parse JSON response
    ↓
For each task:
    ├─ RAG search: tantivy.search(task_description)
    ├─ If match > 0.85 → reuse existing loop
    └─ Else → create new loop
    ↓
Build DAG with dependencies
    ↓
Save to database
    ↓
Execute loops

4. Updated Component: TaskDecomposer
rust// src/decomposer.rs

struct TaskDecomposer {
    registry: LoopRegistry,
}

impl TaskDecomposer {
    async fn decompose(&self, task: Task) -> Result<DAG> {
        // 1. Use Claude Code to decompose
        let tasks = self.decompose_via_claude(&task).await?;

        // 2. For each task, check for duplicates via RAG
        let mut dag = DAG::empty();

        for subtask in tasks {
            if let Some(existing_id) = self.registry.find_duplicate(&subtask.description).await? {
                // Reuse existing loop
                dag.add_node(LoopNode {
                    id: existing_id,
                    task: subtask.description,
                    reused: true,
                    status: LoopStatus::Completed,
                    dependencies: subtask.depends_on,
                });
            } else {
                // Create new loop
                let loop_id = generate_id();
                dag.add_node(LoopNode {
                    id: loop_id,
                    task: subtask.description,
                    reused: false,
                    status: LoopStatus::Pending,
                    dependencies: subtask.depends_on,
                });
            }
        }

        Ok(dag)
    }

    async fn decompose_via_claude(&self, task: &Task) -> Result<Vec<SubTask>> {
        let prompt = self.build_decomposition_prompt(task);

        // Spawn Claude Code in CLI mode
        let output = Command::new("claude-code")
            .arg("--non-interactive")
            .arg("--prompt")
            .arg(&prompt)
            .output()?;

        let response = String::from_utf8(output.stdout)?;

        // Extract JSON from output (Claude might wrap in markdown)
        let json_str = self.extract_json(&response)?;
        let decomposition: DecompositionResponse = serde_json::from_str(&json_str)?;

        Ok(decomposition.tasks)
    }

    fn build_decomposition_prompt(&self, task: &Task) -> String {
        format!(
            r#"
Decompose this task into atomic coding tasks:

Task: {}
Description: {}
Acceptance Criteria: {}

Rules:
- Each task should be completable in one coding session (<500 lines)
- Tasks should be independently verifiable
- If task is already atomic, return just that task
- Include dependencies between tasks

Output JSON only:
{{
  "tasks": [
    {{
      "id": "task-1",
      "description": "Create User schema with email and password fields",
      "depends_on": []
    }},
    {{
      "id": "task-2",
      "description": "Implement POST /auth/login endpoint",
      "depends_on": ["task-1"]
    }}
  ]
}}
            "#,
            task.title,
            task.description.as_deref().unwrap_or(""),
            task.acceptance_criteria.join("\n- ")
        )
    }

    fn extract_json(&self, text: &str) -> Result<String> {
        // Find JSON block (might be wrapped in ```json ... ```)
        if let Some(start) = text.find("```json") {
            let start = start + 7; // Skip ```json
            if let Some(end) = text[start..].find("```") {
                return Ok(text[start..start + end].trim().to_string());
            }
        }

        // Try finding raw JSON
        if let Some(start) = text.find('{') {
            if let Some(end) = text.rfind('}') {
                return Ok(text[start..=end].to_string());
            }
        }

        Err(anyhow!("No JSON found in output"))
    }
}

#[derive(Deserialize)]
struct DecompositionResponse {
    tasks: Vec<SubTask>,
}

#[derive(Deserialize)]
struct SubTask {
    id: String,
    description: String,
    depends_on: Vec<String>,
}

5. Updated Component: LoopRegistry (RAG)
rust// src/loop_registry.rs

use tantivy::*;

struct LoopRegistry {
    rag: RAGIndex,
    db: SqliteConnection,
}

impl LoopRegistry {
    async fn find_duplicate(&self, task: &str) -> Result<Option<String>> {
        // Search via RAG (tantivy)
        let results = self.rag.search(task, 5).await?;

        if let Some(top) = results.first() {
            if top.score > 0.85 {
                // Very similar task found
                println!("Found duplicate: {} (score: {})", top.loop_id, top.score);
                return Ok(Some(top.loop_id.clone()));
            }
        }

        Ok(None)
    }

    async fn register_completed(&self, loop_id: &str, task: &str) -> Result<()> {
        // Add to RAG index for future deduplication
        self.rag.add_document(loop_id, task).await?;

        // Also save to database
        sqlx::query!(
            "UPDATE loops SET status = 'passed' WHERE id = ?",
            loop_id
        )
        .execute(&self.db)
        .await?;

        Ok(())
    }
}

struct RAGIndex {
    index: Index,
    reader: IndexReader,
}

impl RAGIndex {
    fn new() -> Result<Self> {
        let index_path = ".scope/rag_index";
        std::fs::create_dir_all(index_path)?;

        let mut schema_builder = Schema::builder();
        let task_field = schema_builder.add_text_field("task", TEXT | STORED);
        let id_field = schema_builder.add_text_field("id", STRING | STORED);
        let schema = schema_builder.build();

        let index = Index::create_in_dir(index_path, schema.clone())?;
        let reader = index.reader()?;

        Ok(Self { index, reader })
    }

    async fn search(&self, query: &str, limit: usize) -> Result<Vec<SearchResult>> {
        let searcher = self.reader.searcher();

        let task_field = self.index.schema().get_field("task").unwrap();
        let id_field = self.index.schema().get_field("id").unwrap();

        let query_parser = QueryParser::for_index(&self.index, vec![task_field]);
        let query = query_parser.parse_query(query)?;

        let top_docs = searcher.search(&query, &TopDocs::with_limit(limit))?;

        let results = top_docs.iter()
            .map(|(score, doc_address)| {
                let doc = searcher.doc(*doc_address)?;
                let loop_id = doc.get_first(id_field)
                    .and_then(|v| v.as_text())
                    .unwrap()
                    .to_string();

                Ok(SearchResult {
                    loop_id,
                    score: *score,
                })
            })
            .collect::<Result<Vec<_>>>()?;

        Ok(results)
    }

    async fn add_document(&self, loop_id: &str, task: &str) -> Result<()> {
        let mut writer = self.index.writer(50_000_000)?;

        let task_field = self.index.schema().get_field("task").unwrap();
        let id_field = self.index.schema().get_field("id").unwrap();

        let doc = doc!(
            id_field => loop_id,
            task_field => task
        );

        writer.add_document(doc)?;
        writer.commit()?;

        Ok(())
    }
}

struct SearchResult {
    loop_id: String,
    score: f32,
}

6. What Got Simpler
Removed:

❌ Separate LLM API calls for is_atomic check
❌ Separate LLM API calls for normalization
❌ Embedding API (OpenAI/Anthropic)
❌ Recursive decomposition logic (Claude does it)
❌ Complex CRDT for task normalization

Added:

✅ Single Claude Code CLI call for decomposition
✅ tantivy for RAG search (pure Rust, offline)
✅ Simple JSON parsing
✅ Direct duplicate detection (>0.85 score)

Code Reduction:

Before: ~800 LOC (decomposer + normalization + embeddings)
After: ~300 LOC (decomposer + tantivy wrapper)


7. Example Flow
rust// User clicks "Start Work" on ticket

let ticket = get_ticket("nw-5c46")?;

// Decompose via Claude Code
let dag = decomposer.decompose(ticket).await?;

/*
Claude Code returns:
{
  "tasks": [
    { "id": "1", "description": "Create User schema", "depends_on": [] },
    { "id": "2", "description": "Implement login endpoint", "depends_on": ["1"] },
    { "id": "3", "description": "Add JWT middleware", "depends_on": ["1"] },
    { "id": "4", "description": "Write auth tests", "depends_on": ["2", "3"] }
  ]
}
*/

// Check each for duplicates via RAG
for task in dag.nodes.iter_mut() {
    if let Some(existing) = registry.find_duplicate(&task.task).await? {
        // RAG found "Create User schema" was already done in loop-abc123
        task.id = existing;
        task.reused = true;
        task.status = LoopStatus::Completed;
    }
}

// Save DAG
save_dag(&dag).await?;

// Execute (reused loops are skipped)
orchestrator.execute_dag(dag).await?;

8. System Design Updates
Removed Components:

TaskNormalizer
EmbeddingService
AtomicityChecker

Updated Components:
TaskDecomposer:
ruststruct TaskDecomposer {
    registry: LoopRegistry,  // Only dependency
}

// Single method:
async fn decompose(&self, task: Task) -> Result<DAG>;
LoopRegistry:
ruststruct LoopRegistry {
    rag: RAGIndex,           // tantivy search
    db: SqliteConnection,
}

// Two methods:
async fn find_duplicate(&self, task: &str) -> Result<Option<String>>;
async fn register_completed(&self, loop_id: &str, task: &str) -> Result<()>;
RAGIndex:
ruststruct RAGIndex {
    index: tantivy::Index,
    reader: IndexReader,
}

// Two methods:
async fn search(&self, query: &str, limit: usize) -> Result<Vec<SearchResult>>;
async fn add_document(&self, id: &str, text: &str) -> Result<()>;

Summary
Simplifications:

Decomposition: Claude Code CLI (one call) instead of multiple LLM API calls
Deduplication: tantivy RAG search instead of embeddings API
Atomicity check: Removed (Claude Code handles in decomposition)
Normalization: Removed (RAG handles fuzzy matching)

What remains:

Claude Code decomposes tasks (via CLI prompt)
tantivy searches for duplicates (local, fast)
DAG built from decomposition + dedup results
Loops execute (reused loops skipped)

Code reduction: ~500 LOC removed

Executive Summary
Warp Oz is a cloud-based orchestration platform for coding agents launched in January 2025. It focuses on running agents at scale in the cloud with centralized visibility, scheduling, and integrations.
Scope is a local-first orchestration system focused on maker-checker loops with offline capability, git-native workflows, and human attention management.
Core difference: Warp Oz = cloud-first SaaS platform for autonomous agents. Scope = local-first desktop app with optional sync.

Feature Comparison Matrix
FeatureWarp OzScopeWinnerDeploymentCloud-hosted (Warp's infra) or self-hosted runnersDesktop app (local-first), optional sync serverTie (different philosophies)Agent ExecutionCloud agents in Docker environmentsLocal PTY with git worktreesScope (more control)Offline Support❌ Requires cloud connection✅ Fully offline (solo mode)ScopeMulti-Agent Parallelism✅ Hundreds in parallel (cloud)⚠️ 10-20 per machineWarpVerification❌ No built-in checker agents✅ Independent checker agentsScopeLoop RetriesUnknown✅ Max 5 attempts with feedbackScopeTerminal Access✅ Shared session links✅ Interactive PTY (xterm.js)TieIntegrations✅ Slack, Linear, GitHub, webhooks❌ Not in MVPWarpScheduling✅ Cron-based scheduling❌ Not in MVPWarpTeam Collaboration✅ Session sharing, centralized dashboard✅ Real-time sync (ElectricSQL)TieAudit Trail✅ Every run tracked, shareable links✅ Event log (SQLite)TieGit IntegrationUnknown (multi-repo support)✅ Native (worktrees, commits)ScopePricing ModelCredits (inference + compute)Unknown (likely one-time purchase)TBDData PrivacyCloud-hosted (SOC2, ZDR)✅ Local-first, never leaves machineScopeAPI/SDK✅ REST API, Python/TS SDKs❌ Not in MVPWarp

Deep Dive Comparison
1. Architecture Philosophy
Warp Oz: Cloud-First SaaS
User triggers agent (Slack/CLI/API/Schedule)
    ↓
Warp's orchestrator creates task
    ↓
Agent runs in Docker container (cloud)
    ↓
Session link generated (shareable)
    ↓
Results tracked in centralized dashboard
Scope: Local-First Desktop
User creates ticket (local SQLite)
    ↓
Decompose into loops (local)
    ↓
Agents run in PTY on user's machine
    ↓
Optional: Sync ticket metadata to team
    ↓
Results git-committed locally
Key Difference:

Warp = Agent-as-a-Service (run anywhere, managed for you)
Scope = Agent orchestration on your machine (you control everything)


2. Execution Model
Warp Oz:

Where: Cloud containers (Docker)
Isolation: Process-level (Docker)
Parallelism: Hundreds of agents (limited by credits)
State: Tracked in Warp's database
Access: Shared session links

Scope:

Where: Local machine (PTY)
Isolation: Git worktrees
Parallelism: 10-20 per machine
State: Local SQLite (optionally synced)
Access: Direct PTY attachment

Analysis:

Warp wins on scale (hundreds of parallel agents)
Scope wins on control (direct machine access, offline)


3. Verification & Quality
Warp Oz:

❌ No explicit verification layer
Agents run autonomously
User reviews results after completion
Relies on agent's own judgment

Scope:

✅ Independent checker agents
Maker generates code → Checker verifies
5-layer verification pyramid
Auto-retry with feedback (up to 5 attempts)
Escalate to human on failure

Analysis:

Scope's killer feature: Adversarial verification
Warp has no equivalent (agents self-verify)


4. Team Collaboration
Warp Oz:

✅ Centralized dashboard (oz.warp.dev)
✅ Session sharing (anyone can join running agent)
✅ Filters (source, status, creator, day)
✅ Mobile access (web app works on phones)
✅ Credit visibility (who spent what)

Scope:

✅ Real-time sync (ElectricSQL)
✅ Presence tracking (who's viewing what)
✅ Shared escalations
⚠️ Desktop-only (no mobile in MVP)
⚠️ Limited filtering (status, ticket)

Analysis:

Warp wins on observability (better dashboards)
Scope wins on privacy (data stays local unless you opt-in)


5. Integrations & Triggers
Warp Oz:

✅ Slack (trigger agents from Slack messages)
✅ Linear (trigger on issue events)
✅ GitHub Actions (CI integration)
✅ Webhooks (custom integrations)
✅ Cron schedules (recurring tasks)
✅ API/SDK (Python, TypeScript)

Scope:

❌ No integrations (MVP)
❌ No scheduling
❌ No API
✅ Manual trigger only (click "Start Work")

Analysis:

Warp dominates here (built for automation)
Scope is manual-first (human-in-the-loop)


6. Pricing
Warp Oz:

Credit-based (inference + compute)
1,000 free credits/month for Build/Business/Max tiers
BYOK not supported for cloud agents
Pay for cloud execution time

Scope:

Unknown (likely one-time purchase or subscription)
No cloud costs (local execution)
BYOK supported (API keys stay local)
Pay for LLM API calls directly

Analysis:

Warp = operational cost (ongoing credits)
Scope = capital cost (buy once, run forever)


7. Privacy & Data Residency
Warp Oz:

All data flows through Warp's cloud
SOC2 compliant
Zero Data Retention (ZDR) with LLM providers
Code/prompts touch Warp's servers
Self-hosting available (for execution, not orchestration)

Scope:

100% local in solo mode
Code never leaves your machine
Optional sync (only ticket metadata, not code)
No vendor lock-in
Full control over data

Analysis:

Scope wins for regulated industries (finance, healthcare)
Warp wins for ease of use (no setup)


8. Use Case Fit
When to Use Warp Oz:
✅ You want to run many agents in parallel
✅ You need integrations (Slack, Linear, GitHub)
✅ You want scheduled/automated workflows
✅ You don't mind cloud dependency
✅ You want minimal setup (SaaS)
✅ You're building apps on top of agents (API/SDK)
When to Use Scope:
✅ You want offline capability
✅ You need data privacy (local-first)
✅ You want verification (maker-checker loops)
✅ You prefer git-native workflows
✅ You want human control over agents
✅ You're working solo or small team (3-10 people)

Competitive Positioning
Warp Oz = "GitHub Actions for Coding Agents"

Cloud-based
Event-driven
Scalable
SaaS pricing
Integration-rich

Scope = "Linear + Vercel Turbo for Agents"

Local-first
Human-in-the-loop
Quality-focused
One-time purchase
Privacy-first


What Warp Does Better

Scale: Run hundreds of agents in parallel
Integrations: Slack, Linear, GitHub, webhooks
Automation: Cron schedules, event triggers
API/SDK: Python/TypeScript for programmatic use
Mobile access: Web app works on phones
Zero setup: Just sign up and start


What Scope Does Better

Verification: Independent checker agents (Warp has nothing like this)
Offline: Works without internet
Privacy: Code never leaves machine
Git-native: Worktrees, commits, squashing built-in
Cost model: Pay LLM providers directly, no middleman
Attention management: Circuit breakers, escalation queue
Local control: Direct PTY access, full transparency


Strategic Recommendations for Scope
Immediate (MVP)
1. Double down on verification differentiator

This is Scope's killer feature
Warp has nothing like independent checker agents
Marketing: "The only orchestrator that doesn't trust agents"

2. Emphasize local-first

Privacy angle (regulated industries)
Offline capability (travel, flaky wifi)
No vendor lock-in

3. Target solo developers first

Warp targets teams
Scope MVP is perfect for solo mode
Easier to win early adopters

Post-MVP (v1.1+)
4. Add minimal integrations (don't compete head-on)

GitHub webhooks (simpler than Warp's full suite)
Slack notifications (not triggers, just alerts)
Focus on async notifications, not event-driven

5. API for read-only access

Let users query loop status
Don't compete with Warp's full API (they win on that)
Focus on transparency/auditability

6. Self-hosted option for teams

Similar to Warp's self-hosted runners
But simpler (just ElectricSQL + Postgres)
Target compliance-focused teams

Never Do (Anti-Patterns)
❌ Don't build cloud-hosted agents

That's Warp's game
You can't out-scale AWS/GCP
Stick to local-first

❌ Don't build complex workflow automation

Warp has cron, webhooks, etc.
You can't beat them on breadth
Stay focused on quality loops

❌ Don't build mobile app

Warp has web app for mobile
Not core to Scope's value prop
Desktop is enough


Market Positioning
                Cloud-First ←────────────→ Local-First
                     │                          │
High Scale ─────────┤                          │
(100+ agents)       │   Warp Oz                │
                     │   (SaaS)                 │
                     │                          │
                     │                          │
Medium Scale ───────┤                          │
(10-20 agents)      │                      Scope
                     │                    (Desktop)
                     │                          │
Low Scale ──────────┤                          │
(1-5 agents)        │   Cursor/Copilot         │
                     │   (Editor-embedded)      │
                     └──────────────────────────┘
Scope occupies: Local-first, medium-scale, quality-focused
White space: Teams who want control + verification but don't need cloud scale

Conclusion
Warp Oz is NOT a direct competitor to Scope.
They solve different problems:

Warp Oz = "How do I run agents at cloud scale?"
Scope = "How do I trust agent output?"

Scope's moat:

Maker-checker verification (Warp has nothing like this)
Local-first (appeals to privacy-conscious users)
Git-native (better for developers who live in git)
Attention management (circuit breakers, escalation queue)

Warp's moat:

Cloud scale (hundreds of parallel agents)
Integrations (Slack, Linear, etc.)
Automation (cron, webhooks)
API/SDK (build on top of agents)

Recommendation: Position Scope as "The only agent orchestrator with built-in verification" — this is defensible and unique. Don't compete on scale or integrations (Warp wins). Compete on quality, privacy, and control.Start your own conversation
